# Prior work, study by study

Generated from the verification record by `docs/research/render.py`. Each headline number is tagged **primary** (read in the document itself) or **secondhand** (press or summary only). Secondhand numbers are not to be quoted in the write-up until someone has read the paper.

## Robyn Meeks (Duke, Sanford School), Jacquelyn Pless (Toronto, Rotman; corresponding author), Zhiyuan Qi (NC State, Economics), Zhenxuan Wang (NC State, Agricultural and Resource Economics). "Who Pays for Growth? Evidence from Data Centers and the Grid." MIT Center for Energy and Environmental Policy Research Working Paper Series, CEEPR WP 2026-13 (file name MIT-CEEPR-WP-2026-13r). Cover: "Revised October 2026"; title page dated October 23, 2026. 100 PDF pages (37 pp. text and references, main figures/tables to p. 51, online appendix SI-1 to SI-42). Original version: same WP number, title page dated June 19, 2026 (90 PDF pages). Not peer reviewed (CEEPR states it does no selection or review before posting).

- **Link:** https://ceepr.mit.edu/wp-content/uploads/2026/06/MIT-CEEPR-WP-2026-13r.pdf
- **How far we got:** read in full. Opened the CEEPR landing page (https://ceepr.mit.edu/workingpaper/who-pays-for-growth-evidence-from-datacenters-and-the-grid/), which links the open PDF of the October revision. Fetched that PDF directly with no access barrier and read the extracted text of all 100 pages: abstract, intro, background, framework, data, empirical strategy, results, robustness, mechanisms, conclusion, main Tables 1-6, appendix sections A-B, appendix Tables A1-A18. Figures 2 and 3 (event studies) were viewed as rendered pages; other figures were read by caption and notes only, so I did not inspect their plotted values. I also opened the original June version (https://ceepr.mit.edu/wp-content/uploads/2026/06/MIT-CEEPR-WP-2026-13.pdf) only far enough to compare the title page, abstract and Tables 2-3: the headline price coefficients are identical in both versions. Page numbers below are the paper's printed page numbers (PDF page = printed page + 3).
- **Funding or affiliation:** Authors are at Duke (Sanford School), University of Toronto (Rotman) and NC State. The only funding disclosed is an Institute for Humane Studies grant (no. IHS020420) to Zhenxuan Wang for data purchase (title-page footnote). Posted in the MIT CEEPR Working Paper series; CEEPR's own boilerplate says the center works in cooperation with industry partners and does not peer review or endorse working papers. No utility, technology-company or advocacy funding is disclosed in the paper; I did not check the authors' external disclosure pages.
- **Unit:** Utility-by-state pair by year (EIA-861 reporting unit). Main panel: 14,733 utility-state-years, 1,048 utility-state pairs, 973 distinct utilities, 48 contiguous states (Sec. 4.3, p. 19; Table 1, p. 46). Only 57 utilities serve more than one state (fn. 18). Footnote 19 (p. 22) refers to 112 treated utilities. Infrastructure outcomes (FERC Form 1 via S&P) are utility-year, IOUs only, N=1,590, 106 utilities; rate-case outcomes are utility-state-year, IOUs only, N=2,070.
- **Years:** Panel 2010-2024 (EIA-861 annual). Treatment cohorts are first activations in 2010-2024; pairs whose first large activation was before 2010 are dropped from the main design (Sec. 5, p. 20). Pre-period covariates for matching and heterogeneity splits are 2005-2007 averages. Wholesale LMP sub-analysis covers 2015-2024 (CAISO from 2016). Rate cases 2010-2025 in source, restricted to 2010-2024. Data center inventory vintage: April 2026.
- **Outcome:** Log average retail price = total retail revenue divided by total retail sales ($/kWh), from EIA-861 at the utility-by-state-year level, for all customers and separately for the residential, commercial and industrial classes; deflated to 2024 dollars with the CPI (Sec. 4.1, p. 15). For utilities in restructured states that report both bundled and delivery-only service, the paper uses the BUNDLED-service price only (p. 15; fn. 10; App. B.1, p. SI-3); the appendix states this is the regulated default-service/standard-offer rate and in heavily restructured states covers a selected and shrinking subset of customers rather than the typical customer. Utilities that ever file the EIA-861 Short Form (under 100,000 MWh) are excluded; pairs must have retail sales in all three classes (p. 21). Customer class follows the utility's own rate-class reporting; since 2022 EIA-861 instructions place data centers in commercial, but utilities may report them as industrial (p. SI-4). This is average revenue per kWh, not a tariff rate and not a bill. Secondary outcomes: FERC Form 1 investment items and customer advances for construction (S&P Capital IQ Pro), rate-case requests/authorizations (S&P RRA), load factor and peak demand (EIA-861 operational data), day-ahead LMP components (seven ISOs via power.dev).
- **Data center data:** Aterio, "US Data Center Locations and Power Demand" (commercial, purchased; NOT public). As of April 2026: 5,937 facilities, of which 1,961 active, 735 under construction, 3,241 announced (Sec. 4.1, p. 15). Unit is the physical building. Fields: GPS coordinates, operator, building size, estimated power capacity (MW), activation year. Aterio compiles from press releases, company sites, satellite imagery, utility/interconnection filings and permits, with analyst validation. Where operators do not report capacity, MW is estimated by Aterio's machine-learning model (App. B.1, p. SI-3, which reports out-of-sample R2 of 0.94 for hyperscalers and 0.97 for others). The paper says activation years before 2005 are often imprecise (p. 18). The paper notes other inventories give different active counts: Bloomberg 1,640 (2025), IM3 Open-Source Data Center Atlas 1,479 (Feb 2026), S&P via Muller 2,465 (2025), DataCenterMap 4,286 total (fn. 9, p. 15). Funding for the data purchase came from an Institute for Humane Studies grant.
- **Treatment:** Binary, absorbing "PostDC": equals 1 from the first year in which newly activated data center capacity above 5 MW appears in any county that the utility-state pair reports serving (Sec. 4.2, p. 18; Sec. 5, p. 20). Construction: facility records aggregated to county-year; county-years with newly activated capacity of 5 MW or less are discarded; earliest qualifying year per county is the county's entry; the utility-state's entry year is the earliest among its EIA-861 service-territory counties in that state. Every utility whose territory includes the county is coded treated, whether or not it actually serves the facility (fn. 17, p. 18, Durham County example). The estimate is therefore the effect of first entry plus all subsequent data center growth in the territory, not a per-facility or per-MW effect (pp. 3, 21, 23). Pairs with first entry before 2010 are dropped; controls are never-treated pairs. A separate IV exercise uses ln(1 + cumulative MW) with no 5 MW threshold and keeps pre-2010 hosts (App. B.4, p. SI-9).
- **Method:** Staggered difference-in-differences using Callaway and Sant'Anna (2021) group-time ATTs with never-treated utility-state pairs as the only comparison group, implemented with the Sant'Anna-Zhao (2020) doubly robust estimator; reported as event studies (roughly -10 to +12 years) and an overall ATT averaging post-treatment effects across cohorts and years (Sec. 5, pp. 20-21, eqs. 13-15). Standard errors clustered by utility-state pair. Identifying assumption is parallel trends between treated and never-treated pairs; siting is acknowledged to be non-random (Sec. 5.1, p. 21). Support offered: flat pre-period event-study coefficients; added utility linear trends and grid-region cubic trends; year effects interacted with pre-period price, customers, sales, manufacturing density, population, GDP per capita (Table A5, p. SI-28); entropy-balanced matched DiD on four 2005-2007 covariates (Tables A6-A7); dropping cohorts before 2012 or after 2022; dropping multi-state utilities; alternative clustering; controls for renewables, storage, wildfire trend, EVs, electric heating, smart meters; excluding CA, and TX+VA (Table A11, p. SI-34). Complementary 2SLS: ln(1+cumulative MW) instrumented by 2005-2007 local data-processing presence (NAICS 518210 establishments per capita, or log employment) interacted with annual combined capex of Alphabet, Amazon, Meta and Microsoft, with pair and year fixed effects and log population (App. B.4). In the ownership splits, the treated group is one ownership type but the control group is ALL never-treated utilities regardless of ownership (Table 3 notes, p. 48). The paper does not state which covariates, if any, enter the doubly robust step in the baseline.

**Headline findings**

- [primary] All-customer average retail price rises after first data center entry (overall ATT, log points) **0.027 (SE 0.009), reported as 2.7%; N=14,733; 973 utilities** (Table 2 col. 1, p. 47; abstract; Sec. 6.1 p. 23)
- [primary] Residential average price effect **0.021 (SE 0.008), reported as 2.1%** (Table 2 col. 2, p. 47)
- [primary] Commercial average price effect **0.028 (SE 0.009), reported as 2.8%** (Table 2 col. 3, p. 47)
- [primary] Industrial average price effect **0.042 (SE 0.016), reported as 4.2%; significant at 5% not 1%** (Table 2 col. 4, p. 47)
- [primary] Investor-owned utilities: all-customer effect **0.056 (SE 0.016), reported as 5.6%** (Table 3 Panel A col. 1, p. 48)
- [primary] Investor-owned utilities by class **Residential 0.052 (0.014); commercial 0.046 (0.015); industrial 0.080 (0.035)** (Table 3 Panel A cols. 2-4, p. 48)
- [primary] Publicly owned utilities: small, weakly significant on the all-customer average, class estimates not distinguishable from zero **All 0.024 (0.015), significant at 10% only; residential 0.017 (0.015); commercial 0.017 (0.015); industrial 0.027 (0.020)** (Table 3 Panel B, p. 48)
- [primary] Cooperatives: no effect **All -0.001 (0.013); residential -0.007 (0.010); commercial 0.018 (0.013); industrial 0.014 (0.022)** (Table 3 Panel C, p. 48)
- [primary] Restructured states: larger effects **All 0.038 (0.017); residential 0.040 (0.016); commercial 0.029 (0.016); industrial 0.047 (0.032, not significant). N=3,421; 239 utilities** (Table 4 Panel A, p. 49)
- [primary] Non-restructured states: small and mostly insignificant **All 0.015 (0.010, n.s.); residential 0.007 (0.009, n.s.); commercial 0.024 (0.010); industrial 0.027 (0.017, n.s.). N=11,312; 755 utilities** (Table 4 Panel B, p. 49)
- [primary] Ownership-by-structure split: IOU effects appear in both market structures; publics and co-ops null in both **IOU all-customer: restructured 0.061 (0.025), non-restructured 0.044 (0.018). IOU residential: 0.061 (0.023) and 0.040 (0.014)** (Table A3 Panel A, p. SI-26)
- [primary] Bill translation of the residential effect **0.32 cents/kWh; about $3.21/month or $38.52-$39/year against a pre-treatment bill of about $1,835/year ($153/month). IOU customers: 0.88 cents/kWh, about $7.34/month ($88/year). Restructured states: about $5.94/month ($71/year)** (Intro p. 3; Sec. 6.3 p. 25)
- [primary] Effects concentrated where more data centers follow the first **With subsequent entry: all 0.033 (0.013), residential 0.029 (0.011). Without subsequent entry: all 0.017 (0.012), residential 0.010 (0.011), both n.s.** (Table A2, p. SI-25)
- [primary] Matched (entropy-balanced) DiD gives similar or larger estimates **Full sample all 0.027 (0.011), residential 0.021 (0.009); IOU all 0.061 (0.019), residential 0.056 (0.015); restructured all 0.056 (0.024); non-restructured all 0.006 (0.010)** (Table A7, p. SI-30)
- [primary] IV estimates on ln(1 + cumulative MW), different sample (16,874 obs, 1,186 pairs, keeps pre-2010 hosts) **All-customer 0.073 (0.022) and 0.078 (0.015); residential 0.056 (0.019) and 0.071 (0.014); first-stage KP F 48.38 and 124.80** (Tables A8-A9, pp. SI-31 to SI-32; App. B.4)
- [primary] Excluding Texas and Virginia, or California, leaves estimates close to baseline **Ex-TX and VA: all 0.025 (0.009), residential 0.019 (0.009). Ex-CA: all 0.027 (0.009), residential 0.022 (0.008)** (Table A11 Panels G-H, p. SI-34)
- [primary] Tight-capacity regions drive the restructured-state effect **Restructured, low reserve margin: all 0.056 (0.020), residential 0.059 (0.019); high reserve margin: all 0.008 (0.028), residential 0.007 (0.026)** (Table A13 Panel B, p. SI-36)
- [primary] IOU investment response (FERC Form 1, utility-year, $2024 millions) **Generator additions +63.57 (20.69), 428% of pre-treatment mean; electric CWIP +137.39 (59.31), 35%; transmission plant additions and new-line spending not significant** (Table A12 Panel A, p. SI-35; Sec. 7.1.1 p. 29)
- [primary] Authorized rate base rises; authorized revenue requirement and authorized ROE do not **Authorized rate base +$1.719 billion (0.460), N=336; authorized revenue requirement change -0.003 (0.013); authorized ROE -0.064 (0.123)** (Table 6 Panel A, p. 51)
- [primary] Utilities that adopt a large-load tariff by 2026 show no price increase (authors call this descriptive only) **No tariff: all 0.026 (0.011); tariff: all -0.022 (0.019), 34 utilities, N=836** (Table A17 Panel A, p. SI-40; Sec. 7.2.2 p. 32)
- [primary] Controlling for day-ahead LMPs attenuates the retail effect only slightly (wholesale-matched subsample) **0.038 (0.012) to 0.034 (0.012); restructured 0.042 to 0.036; N=4,919** (Table A18, p. SI-41; Sec. 7.4 p. 33)
- [primary] Sample exposure: share of utility-state-years that are post-entry, and capacity conditional on entry **PostDC mean 0.06; 908 post-entry utility-state-years with mean cumulative capacity 80.58 MW (SD 161.51); unconditional mean 4.97 MW** (Table 1 Panel B, p. 46)

**Heterogeneity reported:** By customer class (Table 2). By ownership: IOU, public, cooperative (Table 3; event studies Fig. 3). By industry structure: restructured vs non-restructured (Table 4). Two-way ownership by structure (Table A3). By whether further data centers followed the first (Table A2). By pre-period price level and GDP per capita, split at the median (Table A4: residential effect 0.026 in lower-GDP areas vs 0.015 n.s. in higher-GDP areas). By pre-period reserve margin (Table A13) and fossil share (Table A14), each also split by structure. By eventual adoption of a large-load tariff (Table A17). Late-cohort check dropping pre-2016 entrants (Table A10: IOU all 0.048, residential 0.054; public 0.004; co-op 0.015; restructured 0.039; non-restructured 0.007). Mechanism outcomes (investment, rate cases, load factor, customer advances) are split by structure for IOUs only. The paper does not report formal tests of equality across subgroups, and does not report the number of treated pairs in any subgroup.

**Limitations the authors state:** Authors state: (1) estimates are realized average effects for 2010-2024 entrants, not forward-looking projections, and do not capture the most recent hyperscale build-out or high-growth hotspots; some large markets (they cite utility-states in Virginia) entered before 2010 and are outside the treated sample (p. 25 and fn. 21). (2) Commercial and industrial effects mix pass-through to incumbent firms with the rates data centers themselves pay, because EIA-861 class follows utility rate schedules; residential is the cleanest pass-through measure (pp. 3, 13, 23; SI-4). (3) The effect is of entering a period of data center growth, not of one facility (p. 23). (4) Parallel trends cannot be tested directly (p. 22). (5) Spillovers across utilities through wholesale markets and cost allocation would attenuate estimates (p. 22). (6) In restructured states the bundled price is the default-service rate for a selected, shrinking set of customers; an all-in price for delivery-only customers cannot be built from EIA-861 (fn. 10; SI-3). (7) Large-load tariff results are descriptive; adoption is recent and not random (p. 32). (8) Distributional splits do not measure household energy burden or income-specific pass-through (p. 26). (9) Transmission results are imprecise and not significant (p. 29). (10) Wholesale price measures are location-based exposures, not realized procurement cost (SI-5). (11) FERC Form 1 and rate-case evidence exists only for IOUs.

**What a skeptical referee would raise:** Items marked [derived] are my own arithmetic from the paper's tables, not numbers the paper reports. 1. Thin treated sample behind the subgroup headlines. The paper never reports treated counts by subgroup. [derived] Because each ownership panel in Table 3 uses the same never-treated controls, the Ns imply about 13,073 never-treated observations and about 512 treated IOU, 534 treated public and 614 treated co-op observations, i.e. roughly 34-37 treated IOU utility-state pairs. The same algebra on Table A3 implies roughly 16 treated IOU pairs in restructured states and 18 in non-restructured states. The 5.6% and 6.1% figures rest on a few dozen utilities; this assumes ownership groups partition the treated set and near-balanced panels. 2. IOU estimate uses a mostly non-IOU control group. Controls are all never-treated utilities regardless of ownership, and never-treated utilities passing the filters are overwhelmingly municipals and co-ops. If IOU real rates diverged from public and co-op rates over 2010-2024 for reasons unrelated to data centers (rate-base growth, storm and wildfire hardening, grid modernization), the IOU coefficient absorbs that. Flat pre-trends in Fig. 3a help, but no IOU-only-control specification is shown, and the trend and covariate-by-year robustness checks (Table A5) are reported only for the pooled sample. Matching (Table A7) balances on price, size, manufacturing and GDP, not ownership. 3. Subgroup differences are asserted, not tested. [derived] Restructured vs non-restructured: 0.038 vs 0.015, difference 0.023 with a naive SE near 0.020. IOU vs public: 0.056 vs 0.024, difference 0.032 with naive SE near 0.022 (the shared control group makes this approximate). Neither is clearly distinguishable at conventional levels; IOU vs co-op is. 4. Treatment misclassification by county overlap. Any utility serving any part of a county with a new facility is treated, even if another utility serves the facility. Small co-ops and municipals sharing a county with an IOU-served campus are coded treated, which biases their estimates toward zero and may manufacture part of the ownership gradient. The paper does not identify the serving utility. 5. Restructured-state outcome is the default-service price. The paper concedes this covers a selected and shrinking customer subset. Shopping migration changes the composition of bundled sales over time, and default-service generation rates track wholesale and capacity prices. The 4.0% restructured residential effect is therefore an effect on default-service average revenue, not on what the typical household pays. Industrial bundled prices in retail-choice states reflect a small residual of customers. How ERCOT competitive areas enter the sample is not explained. 6. Average revenue per kWh is not a rate. It moves with usage per customer (fixed charges spread over fewer or more kWh), class mix and weather. No results are shown for sales or customer counts as outcomes, so a compositional explanation is not ruled out. 7. External validity is narrow in both directions. Pre-2010 hosts (including Northern Virginia) are excluded from treated units, and the sample ends in 2024, before the 2025-26 PJM capacity-price increases. [derived] Mean post-entry capacity is about 81 MW with SD 162, so the typical treated territory hosts modest cloud-era capacity. The overall ATT weights early cohorts' long horizons heavily (effects grow to roughly 0.05-0.07 by years 10-12 in Fig. 2, with wide intervals, by my visual read); late AI-era cohorts contribute few post-years. 8. Timing and anticipation. Treatment is dated at activation, but interconnection, construction and rate filings precede it. Pre-period coefficients look flat in Figs. 2-3 for all-customer and residential outcomes (industrial is noisier), but activation year is itself partly inferred from satellite imagery and capacity is partly model-estimated. 9. Precision. [derived] The residential 95% interval is roughly 0.5% to 3.7%; all-customer roughly 0.9% to 4.5%; IOU all-customer roughly 2.5% to 8.7%. "Without subsequent entry" is null (Table A2), so the result is driven by territories with continuing build-out. 10. IV is weak evidence. A national big-tech capex series interacted with 2005-2007 data-processing presence is in effect a differential time trend for tech-heavy, urban territories. Exclusion fails if those places had faster rate growth for other reasons. The IV sample and treatment differ from the main design, and the implied elasticity (about 0.07) is large relative to the DiD estimate. 11. Mechanism evidence is suggestive. A 428% jump in generator additions is from a pre-treatment mean of $14.9 million and includes restructured-state utilities that mostly do not own generation. Authorized rate base is observed only in 336 rate-case observations, a selected sample. Authorized revenue requirements do not rise, which sits awkwardly with a retail price increase. The large-load tariff split conditions on a 2026 outcome and has 34 utilities in the adopting group. 12. Many splits, no multiple-testing adjustment, no posted code or data, and the core treatment input is proprietary, so the headline cannot be independently reproduced exactly. 13. Minor: Table 1 gives a sample mean all-customer price of $0.13 while Table 2 gives $0.14 for pre-treatment treated units (different populations, not an error). The intro rounds the IOU monthly figure to $7 while Sec. 6.3 gives $7.34.

**What it would take to reproduce:** No replication package, code repository or data-availability statement appears anywhere in the PDF (searched the full text). The headline price result depends on one proprietary input: Aterio's facility inventory (activation year, county, MW). Everything else for Tables 2-4 is public: EIA-861 Sales to Ultimate Customers (revenue and sales by class, by utility-state, bundled vs delivery service type), EIA-861 Service Territory file (counties served), ownership type, Short Form filer lists, and CPI. Mechanism results additionally need S&P Capital IQ Pro (FERC Form 1 compilation; the underlying Form 1 filings are public but the paper used S&P's version), S&P RRA rate cases (proprietary), the Halcyon Large Load Tariff Tracker March 2026 release (I am uncertain whether this is freely available), power.dev LMPs (underlying ISO data are public), EIA-860/923/411, BEA county GDP, Census CBP. To approximate the headline with public data: (a) substitute a public facility inventory with activation years and MW (the paper itself cites the PNNL IM3 Open-Source Data Center Atlas; I am uncertain whether it carries activation years and capacity for all records); (b) build county-year newly activated MW, keep county-years above 5 MW, take first year per county; (c) map to utility-state pairs through EIA-861 service-territory counties, earliest year across counties; (d) contiguous US, 2010-2024, drop pairs first treated before 2010, drop utilities that ever filed the Short Form, require residential, commercial and industrial sales, use bundled-service revenue/sales where both service types are reported; (e) log real price; Callaway-Sant'Anna with never-treated controls, doubly robust, overall ATT, cluster by utility-state. Calibration targets from the paper: 14,733 observations, 1,048 pairs, 973 utilities, 112 treated utilities (fn. 19), 908 post-entry observations, pre-treatment treated means $0.14 all and $0.15 residential, coefficients 0.027/0.021/0.028/0.042. Unspecified details that could move a replication: the exact aggregation weights for the overall ATT, whether any covariates enter the doubly robust step, the year(s) of the service-territory file used for the main treatment mapping (the wholesale and wildfire appendices use fixed 2010 counties), how utilities that change ownership or merge are handled, and how Texas competitive-area delivery utilities and retail providers are treated under the bundled-price rule.

**Corrections to what we had heard:** The brief's numbers all match the primary document: 2.7% all, 2.1% residential, 2.8% commercial, 4.2% industrial (Table 2); IOU 5.6% (Table 3); 2010-2024. Refinements and cautions: (1) "Small at publics" is 0.024 with SE 0.015, significant only at the 10% level, with all three class estimates insignificant; "none at co-ops" is -0.001. (2) "Larger in restructured markets" is 3.8% vs 1.5% (the latter not significant); residential is 4.0% vs 0.7%. The paper says ownership, not market structure, is the first-order split. (3) These are not per-data-center or per-MW effects. Treatment is the first year more than 5 MW activates in any county the utility serves, and the estimate bundles all later growth; the paper says so explicitly. (4) "2010-2024" is both the panel window and the entry window; places that already had a large data center before 2010 (the paper names Virginia utility-states) are dropped from the main design, so the largest legacy markets do not drive the 2.7%. (5) The 5.6% IOU figure is the all-customer average; the IOU residential figure is 5.2%. (6) The outcome is real (CPI-deflated) average revenue per kWh from EIA-861, using the bundled/default-service price in restructured states, not tariff rates or bills. (7) The landing-page URL slug says "datacenters" but the title is "Data Centers"; the cover reads "CEEPR WP 2026-13" with "Revised October 2026", the file is named 13r, and the title page is dated October 23, 2026. The June 19, 2026 version has identical headline coefficients; the revision relabels "deregulated" as "restructured", adds the IV section (absent from the June text), and corrects a customer-count gap from about 20,000 to about 200,000.

## Asa Watten (EPRI), John Bistline (Watershed), Geoffrey Blanford (EPRI). "Have Data Centers Raised Your Electric Bill? Causal Evidence from the United States." arXiv:2606.19777v1 [physics.soc-ph; cross-list econ.GN], submitted 18 June 2026. 45 pages. v1 is the only version as of 2026-11-11 (arxiv.org/abs/2606.19777v2 returns 404). Not peer reviewed; no journal reference on the arXiv record. Note: the arXiv HTML rendering prints "August 24, 2026" under the authors, but the PDF title page carries no date; I read that as an HTML-conversion artifact, not a revision date.

- **Link:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19777 (PDF: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.19777v1 ; HTML: https://arxiv.org/html/2606.19777v1)
- **How far we got:** read in full. Open copy on arXiv, no access barrier. Read the complete arXiv HTML full text (v1) as plain text (abstract, intro, data, descriptive section, identification, results, discussion, references, Appendix B theory, Appendix C tables). Then read the arXiv PDF v1 page images for pp. 1-26 and 39-45 to get page numbers and to see the tables and figures as typeset (pp. 27-38, the rest of the references and the theory appendix, were read in the HTML only). I wrote no files; the WebFetch tool itself cached a copy of the PDF at the local fetch cache (webfetch-1794411371350-6n016n.pdf). A web search also surfaced an EPRI-hosted brief (esca.epri.com, "2026-jun-have-data-centers-raised-your-electric-bill" PDF) that I did not open; nothing here relies on it.
- **Funding or affiliation:** No funding, acknowledgments, conflict-of-interest or data-availability statement appears anywhere in the paper. The title-page footnote gives affiliations only: Watten and Blanford at EPRI, Bistline at Watershed (p. 1). The paper cites several EPRI reports and uses EPRI's US-REGEN regions. Context not from the paper (general knowledge about the organization, so treat as background, not a verified fact from this document): EPRI is a nonprofit research institute whose funding comes largely from electric utility members, which have a commercial interest in load growth.
- **Unit:** Causal analysis: state-year panel, 480 observations in the main sample (arithmetically 48 states x 10 years; the paper says CONUS but never states the state count, and its region list in footnote 7 includes DC, so whether DC or a state is dropped is unclear). 288 observations in the 2019-24 subsample, 432 in first differences. Separate descriptive analysis: utility-year panel of 366 large investor-owned utilities from FERC Form 1 (414-440 observations per regression).
- **Years:** 2015-2024 for the IV analysis (data center additions 2015-2024; subsample 2019-2024). Utility cost regressions 2015-2024 (Figure 1 note). Table 1 note says its sample spans 2014-2024, which is inconsistent with its 480 observations.
- **Outcome:** Log residential revenue, with log residential sales as a regressor, so the coefficient is read as the change in average all-in residential price at fixed residential sales (Eq. 2 p. 14; Table 2 note p. 20). Source: EIA Form 861 retail revenue and sales "as cleaned and compiled by Selvans et al. (2026)", i.e. the PUDL data release, restricted to bundled (energy and wires) service providers only, which the paper says "accounts for 93% of residential sales during this period" (pp. 7-8). Prices are nominal. Commercial and industrial results use log average price as the outcome (footnote 6) and appear only as coefficient plots (Figures C2, C3, pp. 42 and 45).
- **Data center data:** S&P Global Market Intelligence "Datacenter knowledgebase" (cited as 2026, accessed 2025-12-01), described as compiled from public permitting records, site visits, briefings and surveys; gives the year each facility became operational or was expanded and nominal IT capacity (p. 8). This is a proprietary subscription product, not public. Total additions 2015-2024 in CONUS: 21.3 GW, between 1.1 and 6.2 GW per year (p. 8). Instrument data: the 1947 interstate highway plan geography, downloaded from the NBER website "as used in Brinkman and Lin (2024)" (p. 8), which is public. Controls: Census state population estimates, BEA state GDP (public).
- **Treatment:** Inverse hyperbolic sine of cumulative data center IT capacity added in the state since 2015, measured in kilowatts (Table 2 note p. 20; footnote 5 p. 14). In the 2019-24 column the base year is 2019. Pre-2015 stock is excluded because capacity is missing for "a non-trivial share" of pre-2015 facilities (p. 8). Crypto mining and hosting facilities are excluded. It is nominal IT capacity, not metered load; the paper does not observe data center electricity use (footnote 4 p. 13 assumes 50% utilization, used only to scale Figure 2). The text under Equation 2 (p. 14) says additions "since 2018", contradicting the Table 2 note.
- **Method:** Two-stage least squares on a state-year panel. Instrument, exactly as stated: "the total length of the 1947 Eisenhower Interstate Highway Plan within each state" (p. 3; restated p. 15 as "the total length of the original interstate highway plan"). It is a single time-invariant number per state; units and any transformation are not reported (Table C2 just labels it "Instrument"). Claimed relevance: long-haul fiber follows interstate corridors, and data centers follow fiber (pp. 15-16, Figure 3 p. 19). Claimed exclusion: the 1947 plan affects today's electricity prices only through population, economic activity and communications infrastructure, and the first two are blocked by controlling for log population and log GDP (p. 15). Because the instrument does not vary over time it is collinear with state fixed effects, so the IV models drop state fixed effects and use year fixed effects plus fixed effects for 16 EPRI US-REGEN regions (Eq. 3-4 p. 16; region list footnote 7 p. 17). Controls: log residential sales, log population, log GDP. C&I sales deliberately omitted. Standard errors heteroskedasticity-robust and clustered by state. Because first-stage F is 7.869, the authors report Anderson-Rubin weak-instrument-robust confidence intervals and p-values. OLS comparisons: year FE only ("between"), year plus state FE ("within state"), year plus region FE ("within region"). Robustness: first differences, neighbor-state spillovers, dropping California, dropping 2020, adding IHS of wind/solar/gas/storage capacity additions.

**Headline findings**

- [primary] Preferred IV estimate (year + region FE): coefficient on IHS of cumulative data center capacity, outcome log residential revenue conditional on log residential sales. Starred at p<0.05 on the conventional clustered standard error. **-0.035 (SE 0.014), N = 480** (Table 2, column 4 (IV, Within region), PDF p. 20)
- [primary] Authors' verbal interpretation of that coefficient: a doubling of data center capacity lowers residential retail prices, at fixed residential demand. **3.5% fall; Anderson-Rubin interval -12.1% to -1.4%** (Section III.B, PDF p. 21)
- [primary] Authors' second phrasing of the same coefficient (not a separate estimate): for every 10% increase in data center capacity, average residential retail prices fell. **approximately 0.4%** (Introduction, PDF p. 3 (cites Table 2))
- [primary] First-stage F statistic in the preferred specification. The text rounds it to 7.8 and acknowledges it is below 10. **7.869 (text: 'F = 7.8')** (Table 2 col. 4, p. 20; text pp. 3 and 17)
- [primary] Weak-instrument-robust (Anderson-Rubin) 95% CI and p-value for the preferred estimate. **[-0.121, -0.014]; p = 0.000** (Table 2 col. 4, p. 20)
- [primary] First-stage coefficient on the highway-plan instrument, preferred specification. Table shows two stars (p<0.01), while the text on p. 16 says p<0.001. **1.297 (SE 0.462), R2 0.728, N = 480** (Table C2, column 'Within', PDF p. 43)
- [primary] IV estimate restricted to 2019-2024 (base year 2019). **-0.017 (SE 0.006); AR CI [-0.034, -0.006]; AR p = 0.001; first-stage F 21.642; N = 288** (Table 2, column 5, p. 20)
- [primary] OLS estimates are an order of magnitude smaller than the IV estimate. With state fixed effects the authors say they 'could not reject no effect'. **Between -0.010 (0.003); within state -0.003 (0.001); within region -0.001 (0.002)** (Table 2, columns 1-3, p. 20; text p. 17)
- [primary] First-difference estimates (the only specifications that use year-to-year changes with a strong first stage) are zero for both OLS and IV. **OLS 0.000 (0.000); IV 0.000 (0.001); AR CI [-0.002, 0.002]; AR p = 0.872; first-stage F 96.896; N = 432** (Table C3, columns 1-2, PDF p. 44; text p. 21)
- [primary] Neighbor-state spillover IV: positive, insignificant, and the instrument fails. **0.007 (SE 0.012); first-stage F 0.663; AR CI [-Inf, Inf]; AR p = 0.439** (Table C3, column 4, p. 44; text pp. 4, 21-22)
- [primary] Authors' back-of-envelope: the average residential customer lived in a state where data center capacity 'grew 160%' from 2019 to 2024, which 'caused their rates to fall by 6%'. The 1.6 is a customer-weighted mean difference in IHS units. **160% growth; 6% rate decline** (Introduction p. 3 and footnote 1 (pp. 3-4))
- [primary] Data center capacity additions in the S&P data, CONUS, 2015-2024; overall capacity rose 3.5 times 2019-2024. **21.3 GW total; 1.1 to 6.2 GW per year; 3.5x** (Section I.B p. 8; footnote 1 p. 4)
- [primary] Coverage of the outcome data: bundled-service providers only. **93% of residential sales** (Section I.B p. 7)
- [primary] Descriptive cross-class result: within states, higher C&I sales are associated with lower residential revenue at fixed residential sales. **-0.101 (SE 0.035), state + year FE, N = 480** (Table 1, column 2, PDF p. 11)
- [primary] Commercial and industrial price effects are much smaller than residential. Read off coefficient plots, so approximate: preferred-spec industrial about -0.25% and commercial about -0.05% per 'doubling', both with intervals touching or spanning zero. No table of these numbers is given. **approx. -0.25% (industrial), approx. -0.05% (commercial); I am uncertain about these, they are eyeballed from figures** (Figures C2 and C3, PDF pp. 42 and 45; text p. 22)

**Heterogeneity reported:** Limited. (1) Time: 2019-24 subsample effect is about half the full-sample effect (-0.017 vs -0.035). (2) Customer class: commercial and industrial effects "substantially smaller" than residential, shown only as plots (Figures C2, C3). (3) Spillovers to neighboring states: 0.007, insignificant, instrument has no power (F 0.663). (4) Leave-outs: dropping California or dropping 2020 gives similar point estimates (Figure C1, p. 41). (5) Adding IHS wind/solar/gas/storage capacity additions gives a similar point estimate. Not reported: any split by regulated vs restructured states, by region or RTO, by utility ownership, by state, by data center intensity (for example with and without Virginia), or by hyperscale vs colocation. No state-level effects are reported.

**Limitations the authors state:** Authors' own caveats (Discussion, pp. 22-24, plus scattered): (1) Supply constraints now binding (gas turbine backlog, transformer shortages, solar tariffs, stalled federal wind and solar approvals) could make incremental capacity more expensive than embedded supply and reverse the result; the abstract says so too. (2) The forward-looking reading needs durable demand; if forecast load exceeds realized load, utilities overbuild and fixed costs are spread over fewer kWh, and advance investment can raise average costs temporarily. (3) National-level effects, such as natural gas price effects, are excluded; they lack identifying variation and argue the effect was small. (4) First-stage F below 10 in the preferred specification (p. 17). (5) Dropping state fixed effects means "we must rely more heavily on our controls" (p. 16). (6) Data center power use is not observed, only nominal IT capacity. (7) Pre-2015 capacity is missing for a non-trivial share of facilities. (8) Rate structures and individual bills are not observed, only average revenue per kWh. (9) Load shape is abstracted away. (10) The spillover instrument is "quite weak". (11) FERC Form 1 omits some wholesale generator costs. (12) The utility cost correlations are "consistent with economies of scale and also reverse-causality" (p. 10).

**What a skeptical referee would raise:** 1. The identifying variation is cross-sectional, not over time. The instrument is one fixed number per state, state fixed effects are dropped, and the panel has 48 clusters. The IV therefore asks whether states with more 1947-plan highway mileage, given population, GDP, residential sales and region, have lower residential price levels in 2015-2024. Calling this evidence that data centers "caused" rates "to fall" over the period overstates what a levels comparison across about 48 states can show. Four of the 16 regions are single states (CA, FL, NY, TX; footnote 7), so those states contribute nothing to the within-region estimate. 2. The paper's own change-based specification finds nothing. In first differences the first stage is strong (F 96.9) and the IV estimate is 0.000 with an Anderson-Rubin interval of [-0.002, 0.002] (Table C3, p. 44). With state fixed effects OLS is -0.003. The authors read the zero as a lagged effect, but they test no lags, no long differences and no event study. 3. Exclusion restriction is asserted, not tested. Planned highway mileage within a state tracks land area, terrain, settlement density and mid-century industrial geography, all of which plausibly affect electricity cost levels directly (distribution cost per customer, generation mix, hydro and coal endowments, public vs investor ownership) and are not absorbed by population, GDP and 16 regions. There is no placebo (for example regressing pre-2015 or pre-2000 prices on the instrument), no reduced form shown, no control for area or density, and no overidentification possible with one instrument. 4. Weak instrument. F = 7.869. The Anderson-Rubin interval runs from -12.1% to -1.4%, an order-of-magnitude range. AR is valid under weak instruments only if the exclusion restriction holds, so point 3 is not rescued by it. The text describes the preferred estimate as "significant at the 0.1% level" while the table stars it at p<0.05 on the conventional standard error; the 0.1% refers to the AR p-value. 5. Sensitivity to the control set. In Figure C1 (p. 41) the IV point estimate appears to be near zero with region FE but without the population and GDP controls, and near -9 with those controls but without region FE. I am uncertain about this reading because it comes from a small dot-matrix plot, but if right, the headline depends on a specific control combination. The plotted intervals are explicitly not the AR intervals. 6. Treatment transformation. The regressor is asinh of cumulative kW added since 2015. In kW units a state going from zero to 10 MW moves about 10 asinh units, while Virginia doubling moves 0.69, so the estimate is dominated by the zero vs nonzero margin and by when small states first show any capacity, not by large buildouts. The paper cites Bellemare and Wichman (2020) for unit scaling but not the later critique that IHS coefficients are unit-dependent when zeros are present (Chen and Roth 2024 is not in the reference list). Pre-2015 stock is ignored, so "doubling of capacity" means doubling of post-2015 additions. 7. The headline magnitudes are arithmetically loose. A coefficient of -0.035 on a log-like regressor implies about -0.33% for a 10% increase (the paper says approximately 0.4%) and about -2.4% for a doubling (-0.035 x ln 2), not 3.5%; the 3.5% is the effect of one log point, roughly a 2.7-fold increase. Likewise footnote 1 treats a 1.6 IHS-unit change as "grew 160%"; 1.6 log points is roughly a five-fold increase. So "doubling lowers price 3.5%" should not be quoted without this caveat. 8. Outcome coverage. Bundled-service providers only. In retail-choice states (Texas, much of the Northeast, parts of the Mid-Atlantic and Midwest) residential customers on competitive supply are excluded, and which customers remain on bundled default service changes over time. The 93% figure is national; state-level coverage is not shown. Prices are nominal average revenue per kWh, which says nothing about bill distribution or fixed charges. 9. Measurement. IT nameplate capacity by operational year is not load. Ramp-up, utilization, behind-the-meter supply and announced-but-unbuilt capacity are unobserved. The S&P data are proprietary, so the treatment cannot be audited. 10. Sample period and external validity. The sample ends in 2024, before most of the large AI-era interconnections and before the capacity-price spikes in PJM for delivery years 2025/26 onward. The authors themselves say supply constraints could reverse the sign. The result should not be extended to 2025-2026 conditions. 11. State-level averaging can hide utility-level and zone-level effects (for example Dominion zone within Virginia or PJM capacity costs shared across states); the spillover test that would address cross-state incidence has a first-stage F of 0.66 and an unbounded interval. 12. Internal inconsistencies suggest an early draft: "since 2018" vs "since 2015" for the treatment base year (p. 14 vs p. 20); first-stage significance stated as p<0.001 in text but p<0.01 in Table C2; Table 1 sample labelled 2014-2024 with 480 observations; column header "20219-24"; the text says "0.1% level" for a neighbor coefficient that is not starred that way in Table 1; DC appears in the region list although N implies 48 units. 13. Not peer reviewed, posted under physics.soc-ph, authored at an institute whose work is closely tied to the utility industry, with no disclosure statement. That does not make the result wrong, but it should be cited as a working paper with an interested affiliation.

**What it would take to reproduce:** No code or data repository is posted or mentioned; arXiv offers only the TeX source. What a public-data approximation needs: (a) Outcome and sales: PUDL EIA-861 release (Zenodo), state-year residential revenue and sales, bundled service type only, 2015-2024; public. (b) Controls: Census state population, BEA state GDP; public. (c) Instrument: 1947 interstate highway plan line geometry from NBER (the Brinkman and Lin 2024 replication materials), intersected with state boundaries to get length per state; public, but the paper does not say whether length enters in miles, km, logs or scaled, so the first-stage coefficient (1.297) cannot be matched exactly; the second-stage coefficient is invariant to linear rescaling of the instrument but not to a log transform. (d) Treatment: S&P Global Datacenter KnowledgeBase is proprietary. A public substitute (state-year cumulative MW from permits, utility filings, or an open facility list) will differ in coverage and dating; because the regressor is asinh of kW, the zero vs nonzero margin and the first year a state turns positive will drive results, so treatment substitution is the main obstacle. (e) Specification: 2SLS, year FE plus the 16 US-REGEN region FE listed in footnote 7 (p. 17), controls log residential sales, log population, log GDP, cluster by state, Anderson-Rubin CI. (f) Unknowns to resolve by trial: which 48 units make N = 480; nominal revenue (appears so); whether cumulative capacity starts at zero before 2015. Checks worth running that the paper does not report: the reduced form (price on highway length), the same reduced form on pre-2015 or pre-2005 prices as a placebo, state land area and population density as added controls, log(1+x) and MW-unit versions of the treatment, and dropping Virginia.

**Corrections to what we had heard:** Mostly confirmed, with these corrections and qualifications. (1) Affiliations: not all three authors are EPRI. Watten and Blanford are EPRI; Bistline is listed at Watershed (p. 1). (2) "First-stage F about 7.8": the table value is 7.869 (Table 2, p. 20); the text rounds to 7.8. Confirmed. (3) "Doubling lowers residential price about 3.5%" and "10% increase, 0.4% decline" are both in the paper (p. 21 and p. 3) but they are the same single coefficient (-0.035), not two findings, and they are not mutually consistent under exact log arithmetic (a true doubling implies about 2.4%). (4) The brief's "associated with" understates the paper's claim: the authors state it causally ("caused"). (5) "State data center capacity" is narrower than it sounds: cumulative nominal IT capacity added since 2015 from proprietary S&P data, asinh-transformed, excluding crypto; not total installed capacity and not electricity use. (6) "Residential price" is log residential revenue conditional on log residential sales, bundled-service providers only (93% of residential sales), nominal. (7) State-level IV, 2015-2024: confirmed; 480 state-years, with region rather than state fixed effects in the IV. (8) Instrument identified: total length of the 1947 interstate highway plan within each state, time-invariant, data from NBER as used in Brinkman and Lin (2024). (9) The brief omits the paper's own null results: first-difference IV of 0.000 and state-fixed-effects OLS of -0.003. (10) Date: June 2026 is correct (v1, 18 June 2026); the "August 24, 2026" shown in the arXiv HTML view does not appear in the PDF and there is no v2.

## Cameron Scalera (School of International and Public Affairs, Columbia University; RFF-CMCC European Institute on Economics and the Environment), Valentina Bosetti (Department of Economics, Bocconi University; RFF-CMCC European Institute on Economics and the Environment; CEPR), Filippo Pecci (RFF-CMCC European Institute on Economics and the Environment). 'Large-Load Electricity Customers and Spillovers on Household Prices: Evidence from U.S. Data Centers.' CEPR Discussion Paper DP21854, Climate Change and the Environment programme. Cover page reads 'DP21854 (v. 2)'. Imprint page (PDF p. 2): 'First Published 19 August 2026', 'This Revision 02 November 2026'. The manuscript's own title page is dated November 2, 2026. 82 PDF pages (PDF pp. 1-3 are CEPR cover, imprint and abstract page; main text printed pp. 1-19; bibliography printed pp. 20-24; online appendix printed pp. 25-79). CEPR imprint says Discussion Papers 'often represent preliminary or incomplete work' and should be cited with 'provisional character'; not peer reviewed. JEL classification printed as 'N/A'.

- **Link:** https://cepr.org/publications/dp21854
- **How far we got:** read in full. PDF downloaded by hand by the project owner on 2026-11-11 and read locally. Read the complete text extraction of all 82 PDF pages (cover, imprint, abstract, main text, bibliography skimmed only for the paper's own cross-references, full appendix S1-S2 with Tables S1-S28 and Figures S1-S7 captions and notes). Rendered PDF pages 14 (Figure 2) and 20 (Table 3) were viewed as images to check the table columns; all other tables were read from the text extraction, where the columns were unambiguous. Other figures (Fig. 1, S1-S7) were read by caption and notes only; I did not inspect plotted values. Page numbers below are the paper's printed page numbers; PDF page = printed page + 3 (printed p. 1 = PDF p. 4, appendix printed p. 25 = PDF p. 28). Appendix tables are labelled S1 to S28 but the pages carry continuing printed numbers 25 to 79. No text in the paper reads as an instruction to the reader.
- **Funding or affiliation:** Financial support from the PNRR project GRINS, funded by the European Union-NextGenerationEU (Grant ID PE00000018; CUP B43C22000760006), title-page acknowledgements (PDF p. 3). Acknowledged for comments: Robert Metcalfe, Tatyana Deryugina, Doug Almond, and participants of the World Congress of Environmental and Resource Economists. Affiliations: Columbia SIPA, Bocconi, and the RFF-CMCC European Institute on Economics and the Environment. The paper includes the standard disclaimer that views are the authors' only. No conflict-of-interest statement and no thanks to data vendors (Data Center Map, CoStar) appear. No utility, technology-company or advocacy funding is disclosed.
- **Unit:** Utility-by-State by year (EIA-861 utility-state reporting unit, mapped to a HIFLD polygon). Main residential panel: 12,370 observations, 1,237 utility-states, balanced 2015-2024 (Table 1 Panel A col. 3, p. 13). Industrial: 10,820 obs, 1,082 utility-states; commercial: 12,480 obs, 1,248 utility-states (Table 1 Panels B-C). Land-area models use 12,080 / 10,570 / 12,190 obs because some territories lack acreage (fn. 9, p. 10). The paper says 'approximately 1,200 utility-States' (p. 5). Universe: 3,805 HIFLD utility-state territories, 49 states in the table (contiguous U.S.); only 1,243 have residential price data in all ten years; the other 2,562 are small, mostly nonprofit and are dropped (Table S2, p. 30; S1.3 pp. 30-31). Utility types in the 1,243: 172 investor-owned, 1,071 nonprofit (Table S2). Treatment is continuous, so there is no single 'number treated'. Table S11 (p. 46) splits the 1,237 estimation-sample utility-states into 178 'compliers', 89 'defiers' and 970 never-treated (no data center at any time in 2015-2024); [derived] that implies 267 utility-states with at least one operational data center. Distribution-spending sample: 1,403 obs, 145 utility-states, IOUs only (Table 2 Panel B, p. 15). Number of utilities with ten or more facilities: not stated. The paper says only 319 of 3,805 territories have any operational data center by 2024, only seven have more than 60 (S1.2, p. 29), 'most have less than 10 facilities by 2024', 'a few' have 25 to 100, and only three have more than 100 (S2.8, p. 50). Average stock among compliers is 7.79 facilities and among defiers 3.42 (Table S11).
- **Years:** Retail price panel 2015-2024 (EIA-861), balanced. The last year of retail price data entering the regressions is 2024. 'Post-AI' is defined as 2021 onward; pre-AI period 2015-2020 (p. 7-9; alternatives 2020, 2022, 2023 in Table S16, p. 54). Fiber share is the 2017-2019 mean (FCC Form 477, reported 2014-2021). National data center construction spending (Census VIP) 2015-2024 (Table S3, p. 34). Facility start years are inferred 2015-2024 with facilities coded operational if start year is between 1970 and 2024 (S1.2, p. 27). Heterogeneity modifiers are measured over 2017-2019. There are no treatment cohorts or event-time windows for the main estimate. Data vintage (download date of the Data Center Map, CoStar or EIA files): not stated. The authors note CBP data end in 2023 'at the time of this draft' (fn. 19, p. 69).
- **Outcome:** Average retail price in cents per kWh (levels), computed as EIA-861 revenue divided by sales for each customer class (residential, industrial, commercial) at the utility-state-year level, converted to real 2024 dollars with the CPI (p. 4; S1.3 p. 29). The authors say the form does not report rates and the implied average price follows Houde and Myers (2021), Borenstein and Bushnell (2022), Berha and Mohapatra (2025), MacKay and Mercadal (2026). Whether delivery-only or retail-choice customers are handled (bundled versus delivery service type): not stated. The words 'bundled' and 'delivery' do not appear. Log-price versions are in Table S7 and an inverse-hyperbolic-sine version in Table S15. Other outcomes: summer and winter peak demand in MW (EIA-861 Operational files, utility-level, apportioned to states by sales share, S1.4 p. 32-33) and distribution, generation and transmission spending (FERC Form 1 via PUDL, IOUs only, apportioned by state MWh sales share, S1.4 p. 31). No outcome for kWh sales, usage per customer, customer bills, wholesale prices or water.
- **Data center data:** Primary: Data Center Map (DCM), 'a commercial listing of data center addresses, operating companies, and some information on other facility characteristics' (S1.2 p. 25-26); vendor, edition and download date not stated. Supplemented by (1) Business Insider's compilation of state air-quality permits for backup generators (Beckler et al., 2025), fuzzy-matched on company name and address; (2) CoStar property records (year built, last sale date, land area, 'existing' status), fuzzy-matched, with about 1,300 unmatched facilities searched manually in CoStar. Counts (Table S1, p. 28): 3,844 facilities in the raw sample; 3,021 operational; 2,749 operational with a start year (about 91%); 2,698 with start year and coordinates; 2,244 operational with acreage (about 74%); 505 with MW (about 17%). Hyperscale: 727 of the 3,021, defined by DCM or CoStar label or over 50 MW or over 200 thousand sq ft building or over 45 acres (S1.2 p. 27); thresholds chosen from industry reports; hyperscale mean 60.0 acres, non-hyperscale 6.27 acres. Cryptocurrency mines: not mentioned, so inclusion or exclusion not stated. Colocation versus enterprise versus cloud: not distinguished, only hyperscale versus not. Only external validation reported: none against other inventories; the paper does not compare DCM counts with other vendors' inventories. The authors state they distrust facilities listed as operating before 1970 and that very early dates may reflect building age (p. 27).
- **Treatment:** Continuous, not binary. Main treatment is the stock (cumulative count) of operational data centers inside the utility-state territory in each year, winsorized at the 99.5th percentile within year (p. 5, p. 8 fn. 3; Table 1 notes). A facility is counted from its inferred year of operation onward. The count is a number of facilities, NOT megawatts: MW is available for only about 17% of operational facilities (fn. 2, p. 5; Table S1: 505 of 3,021 operational facilities have MW). The intensive measure is the total land area in acres of operational facilities (CoStar acreage for about 74% of facilities, p. 5). Operating only; announced or under-construction facilities are not used. Start year: priority algorithm combining Business Insider air-quality (backup generator) permit year, Data Center Map operation year and CoStar year built or last sale year; where signals disagree it generally takes the LATER year (S1.2, p. 27, steps 1-4); start years before 2000 are replaced with the max of the assigned year and the CoStar sale year. Linking to utilities: facilities are geocoded, then 'geospatially merge[d] to the HIFLD polygons' and summed by territory and year (S1.2 p. 28). Overlaps: HIFLD polygons overlap in places; the authors 'resolve overlapping polygons' by assigning the intersected area to the SMALLEST polygon, reasoning that small co-ops and municipals are 'islands' inside IOU territory (S1.1, p. 25; p. 5). The result is spatially exclusive territories, so each facility is attributed to exactly one utility-state (not counted for every overlapping utility). This is geometric assignment, not the serving utility from interconnection records; no validation against actual serving utility is reported. Multi-state utilities enter as separate utility-states. Instrumented by a shift-share instrument (see estimator).
- **Method:** Two-stage least squares with a shift-share (Bartik-style) instrument, estimated in levels at the utility-state-year level (eqs. 1.1-1.2, p. 8). First stage: DC_ust on Z = PostAI_t x ln(national private data center construction spending_t, Census VIP) x PreAIFiber_us, where PreAIFiber is the territory's mean 2017-2019 share of national Census blocks with fiber-to-the-end-user (FCC Form 477 technology code 50). PostAI is 1 for 2021 onward, so the instrument is zero before 2021. Fixed effects: utility-state and State-by-year. No time-varying controls in the baseline (authors argue they would be 'bad controls', fn. 4 p. 8; controls and baseline-by-year controls in Tables S8 and S10 change estimates little: residential 0.184 and 0.191 versus 0.182). Standard errors clustered by utility-state (BA and State clustering in Table S20; residential SE 0.034 becomes 0.045 and 0.036). There is no comparison group in the DiD sense and no event window; identification is the exogeneity of the fiber share (Goldsmith-Pinkham et al. 2020). First stage: coefficient 0.849 (SE 0.11) on the instrument, F = 59.43; acres: 16.05 (3.63), F = 19.52 (Table S5, p. 37). OLS is also shown and is about one third as large. Pre-trend evidence: a high-fiber (above 90th percentile, 0.132% of national fiber blocks) versus low-fiber event study on residential price relative to 2020 with 2015-2019 coefficients individually insignificant (Fig. S5, p. 67) and joint F = 1.252, p = 0.282 (Table S24, p. 68); similar for industrial and commercial (Fig. S6). The authors state there ARE pre-trends in the FIRST stage: high-fiber territories were already adding data centers faster before 2021 (S2.17, p. 71-72); adding pre-AI growth-bin by year fixed effects (Table S26, p. 74) raises the residential 2SLS estimate to 0.359 (0.11, F 17.62) with 2 bins and 0.681 (0.26, F 10.03) with 4 bins. Exclusion-restriction checks: instrument does not predict log residential sales or customers or total manufacturing plants but does predict fewer iron and steel, pulp and paper, and basic chemical plants (Table S25, p. 70). Other checks: Rotemberg weights (top four utilities hold over 30%; Table S12), placebo instrument (Table S23), weak-IV robust (Anderson-Rubin, tF) intervals (Table S22), long-haul fiber alternative (Table S17), outlier handling (Table S14), alternative post-AI years (Table S16), alternative fixed effects (Table S21). Heterogeneity uses a double-first-stage interaction model (eqs. S14-S16, p. 78) and the authors say they 'do not use an identification strategy' for the modifiers (p. 16).

**Headline findings**

- [primary] Residential price, shift-share 2SLS, per additional operational data center (cents/kWh, real 2024 dollars) **0.182*** (SE 0.034); 12,370 obs, 1,237 utility-states; dep. var. mean 14.63, SD 3.48; first-stage F 59.43. OLS: 0.0610*** (0.018)** (Table 1 Panel A cols. 1 and 3, p. 13; text p. 10 and p. 2)
- [primary] Residential price, intensive margin, per additional acre of data center land (cents/kWh) **0.00941*** (SE 0.0030); 12,080 obs, 1,208 utility-states; first-stage F 19.52. OLS: 0.00128* (0.00071)** (Table 1 Panel A cols. 2 and 4, p. 13)
- [primary] Industrial price per additional data center; commercial price per additional data center (cents/kWh) **Industrial 0.139*** (0.040), 10,820 obs, F 51.18, mean 9.75; commercial 0.130*** (0.026), 12,480 obs, F 61.45, mean 13.13. OLS industrial 0.0965*** (0.027); OLS commercial 0.0116 (0.020), not significant** (Table 1 Panels B and C, p. 13)
- [primary] Industrial and commercial price per acre (cents/kWh) **Industrial 0.00685** (0.0030); commercial 0.00666*** (0.0022)** (Table 1 Panels B and C col. 4, p. 13)
- [primary] Authors' translation of the residential coefficient into the headline percentage: one standard deviation of the post-2021 data center stock, stated as ten facilities, times 0.182 = 1.82 cents/kWh, described as 13% of the post-2021 mean residential price of 14.01 cents/kWh. This is an extrapolation by multiplication, not a separate estimate. **1.82 cents/kWh; 13%. [derived by me] 1.82 / 14.01 = 12.99%; against the full-sample mean of 14.63 it would be 12.4%** (p. 2-3 (abstract, intro), p. 12 and fn. 14)
- [primary] Same effect from a log-price model: per additional data center and for ten facilities (authors describe ten as an 'un-winsorized standard deviation') **Log price coefficient 0.0108*** (SE 0.0018), described as 1.1% per facility and 11% for ten; OLS 0.00368*** (0.0010); industrial 0.0113*** (0.0033); commercial 0.00991*** (0.0019)** (Table S7 Panels A-C col. 3, p. 40; text S2.4 p. 38)
- [primary] Single average hyperscaler (60 acres) effect: extrapolated from the per-acre coefficient, NOT estimated for hyperscalers directly. A one-SD increase in land area (188 acres, about 3 hyperscalers) is stated as about 12.6%. **0.56 cents/kWh, about 4% of the post-2021 mean (14.01). [derived] 0.00941 x 60 = 0.565 cents; 0.565 / 14.01 = 4.0%; 0.00941 x 188 = 1.77 cents = 12.6%** (p. 3 and p. 12)
- [primary] Residential effect is larger in absolute cents than industrial or commercial; authors say about 30 to 40% higher, but similar relative to each class's own average price **[derived] 0.182/0.139 = 1.31 and 0.182/0.130 = 1.40. Log-price coefficients: residential 0.0108, industrial 0.0113, commercial 0.00991 (similar)** (p. 12 (text); Table S7, p. 40)
- [primary] Summer peak demand per additional data center (hundreds of MW) **0.343** (SE 0.15), i.e. 34.3 MW; 12,420 obs, 1,242 utility-states; dep. var. mean 6.05, SD 19.72; first-stage F 61.35. OLS 0.223*** (0.065). Per acre: 0.0183** (0.0080). Authors state ten facilities would raise summer peak by about 50% relative to the post-2021 mean (post-2021 mean not printed)** (Table 2 Panel A, p. 15; text p. 14)
- [primary] Winter peak demand: no effect **-0.0824 (0.11), not significant; 12,420 obs** (Table S27 Panel B col. 1, p. 76; S2.18 p. 75)
- [primary] Distribution spending per additional data center (FERC Form 1, IOUs only, millions of 2024 dollars); described as suggestive because of the small sample **11.58** (SE 5.09), text says about $11.6 million; 1,403 obs, 145 utility-states; dep. var. mean 245.74; first-stage F 16.91. OLS 11.69** (5.69). Per acre 0.659 (0.43), not significant, F 7.33** (Table 2 Panel B, p. 15; text p. 14-15)
- [primary] Generation and transmission spending: no significant effect **Generation -2.54 (3.17); transmission -3.37 (4.44); with right-tail adjustments distribution rises to 14.70*** (weighted) and 20.30*** (removed)** (Table S28 Panels A-C, p. 77)
- [primary] Heterogeneity: nonprofit (co-op, municipal, State and other non-IOU) utilities. Baseline is the IOU effect. **Num DC 0.215*** (0.042); Num DC x Nonprofit -0.192*** (0.064); text rounds IOU to 0.22 and the nonprofit offset to 0.19. [derived] implied nonprofit effect 0.215 - 0.192 = 0.023 (SE not printed)** (Table 3 col. 1, p. 17; text p. 16)
- [primary] Heterogeneity: energy surplus (2017-2019 reserve margin above median, about 5%) **Num DC 0.244*** (0.053); Num DC x Energy Surplus -0.124** (0.058); 12,130 obs** (Table 3 col. 2, p. 17; text p. 18)
- [primary] Heterogeneity: historically price-sensitive utilities (change in log residential price over change in log summer peak, 2017-2019, above 75th percentile) **Num DC 0.127*** (0.027); Num DC x Price Sensitive 0.199*** (0.066); 11,850 obs. [derived] implied 0.326 for price-sensitive, about 2.6 times the baseline; authors say 'more than double'** (Table 3 col. 3, p. 17; text p. 18)
- [primary] Heterogeneity: competitive retail market (State with Retail Power Marketer share of residential sales above 25%, 2017-2019: Illinois, Ohio, Texas) **Num DC 0.236*** (0.051); Num DC x Competitive Market -0.105* (0.057), significant only at 10%; authors say 0.11 less, a '44% reduced price effect'. [derived] 0.105/0.236 = 44.5%. This column uses Year FE, not State x Year FE** (Table 3 col. 4, p. 17; text p. 18)
- [primary] Heterogeneity: high-renewables states (over 15% solar and wind generation, 14 states): no differential effect **Num DC 0.209*** (0.049); Num DC x High Renewables 0.0325 (0.078). Year FE, not State x Year FE** (Table 3 col. 5, p. 17; S2.19 p. 79)
- [primary] First stage and reduced form **First stage: 0.849*** (0.11) per unit of the instrument, F 59.43; authors say this is 48% of the post-2021 mean stock. Reduced form on residential price 0.155*** (0.028); industrial 0.123*** (0.034); commercial 0.111*** (0.022)** (Table S5, p. 37; Table S6, p. 39)
- [primary] Weak-instrument robust 95% intervals for the residential count coefficient **Cluster-robust [0.116, 0.248]; Anderson-Rubin [0.120, 0.257]; tF [0.111, 0.252]** (Table S22 Panel A, p. 63)
- [primary] Sensitivity of the residential estimate to outlier treatment (the paper's main choice is winsorizing at the 99.5th percentile within year, which changes 8 utilities) **Winsorize at none/99.9th/99.5th/99th: 0.110** (0.044, F 9.60), 0.150*** (0.034), 0.182*** (0.034), 0.236*** (0.044). Removing outlier utilities: 0.110**, 0.184***, 0.226***, 0.240***. Max stock after winsorizing at 99.5th: 62 (unwinsorized max 280)** (Table S14, p. 51; S2.8 p. 50)
- [primary] Sensitivity to fixed effects: Year FE only / Ownership x Year / State x Year (baseline) / Ownership x State x Year **Residential 0.218*** (0.042); 0.137*** (0.038); 0.182*** (0.034); 0.108*** (0.033)** (Table S21 Panel A cols. 1-4, p. 61)
- [primary] Controlling for pre-AI data center growth by year raises the 2SLS estimate but weakens the first stage **Residential 0.359*** (0.11), F 17.62 (2 bins); 0.681*** (0.26), F 10.03 (4 bins). Industrial not significant in the bin specifications (0.151, 0.230)** (Table S26, p. 74)
- [primary] Excluding the four highest Rotemberg-weight utilities (Virginia Electric & Power 0.0932, Southern California Edison 0.0846, Commonwealth Edison 0.0632, Public Service Electric & Gas 0.0609; together over 30% of first-stage variation) **Residential 0.201*** (0.035), F 34.09; 12,330 obs** (Table S12, p. 48; Table S13, p. 49)
- [primary] Placebo instrument (pre-2017 spending times land-area share) **Residential 0.0776 (0.060), not significant, F 16.34; industrial -0.219 (0.29); commercial 0.00433 (0.051)** (Table S23, p. 65)
- [primary] Descriptive facts the authors report for scale: stock of facilities in the estimation sample (winsorized), first-stage dependent variable **Mean 1.17, SD 5.00 facilities (all utility-state-years 2015-2024). Acres mean 15.61, SD 88.85. [derived] first-stage statements imply a post-2021 mean stock of about 1.8 facilities (0.849 is '48% of the post-2021 mean') and about 27.7 acres (16.05 is 58%)** (Table S5, p. 37; S2.2 p. 36-37)
- [primary] Descriptive price trend: national average real residential price fell about 11.9% from 2015 to 2024 while nominal rose about 17%; new data centers per year rose from about 126 (2015-2020) to about 216 (2021-2024) **-11.9% real; +17% nominal; 126 to 216 per year (about 70% increase)** (p. 6-7 (Section 2.2))

**Heterogeneity reported:** All splits are for the residential price and use the count of data centers, estimated by interacting the instrumented stock with a pre-period (2017-2019) modifier (Table 3, p. 17; method S2.19 pp. 78-79); the authors say these are 'mainly descriptive' because there is no identification strategy for the modifier (p. 16). (1) Ownership: IOU 0.215***, nonprofit offset -0.192***, nonprofit set is every non-IOU (co-op, municipal, State, federal, political subdivision), so no separate co-op versus municipal result and no separate public-power result (Table 3 col. 1; S2.12 p. 59-60 says six ownership types). (2) Energy surplus: -0.124** (col. 2). (3) Price sensitivity: +0.199*** (col. 3). (4) Retail competition, State-level proxy for Illinois, Ohio, Texas only: -0.105*, weakest result, defined by Retail Power Marketer sales share, 'less precisely mapped to utility territories' (col. 4; p. 18). (5) High wind and solar states: 0.0325, no difference (col. 5). Not reported: hyperscaler versus colocation split (only the intensive acre margin, Table 1 col. 4, and an IV-complier comparison of hyperscaler counts, Table S11); PJM, ERCOT or other market splits; early versus recent years (post-AI year definition varied only, Table S16: 0.164, 0.182, 0.192, 0.190 for 2020 to 2023); load size relative to utility size; investor-owned versus public effect on industrial and commercial prices. Authors state IV compliers are larger metropolitan territories with slightly higher prices, more total data centers, and about the same number of hyperscalers as defiers (p. 11 fn. and S2.6 pp. 46-47; compliers 38% IOU, defiers 29% IOU, never-treated 7.8% IOU, Table S11).

**Limitations the authors state:** (1) The authors say the heterogeneity analysis is 'mainly descriptive' with no identification strategy for the modifiers (p. 16); the competition estimate is 'only weakly significant' and State-level definition is 'less precise' (p. 18). (2) Distribution-spending evidence is limited to about 145 large IOUs, 'suggestive rather than definitive', first-stage F 16.91 and 7.33 for the acre version (p. 14-15, p. 19; Table 2). They expect grid-spending effects to appear with a lag beyond the sample (p. 19). (3) The first stage has pre-trends: high-fiber territories were already gaining data centers faster before 2021, so the first stage is 'not causal' and estimates may be diluted by smaller pre-AI facilities (S2.17, pp. 71-72). (4) Winsorizing matters: retaining all outliers gives estimates 'meaningfully smaller, and sometimes less than half' (S2.8 p. 50; Table S14). (5) Exclusion restriction: the instrument is correlated with slightly fewer iron-and-steel, pulp-and-paper and chemical plants, which the authors say would make estimates slight underestimates (S2.16 p. 70-71); fiber share is correlated with growth in median income and college attainment (Table S9, S2.5 p. 41-43). (6) Land-area first stage is only 'moderately strong' (F 19.5) and long-haul-fiber versions have F below 10 (S2.2 p. 37; S2.10 p. 55). (7) Capacity in MW is observed for about 17% of facilities (fn. 2, p. 5), which is why count and acres are used. (8) Multi-state utilities are apportioned by sales share for peak demand and spending (S1.4 p. 33). (9) Possible spillovers to other utilities through wholesale markets are mentioned as a reason for clustering at BA and State level, which the authors say leaves results significant (S2.11 p. 59). (10) The authors say the AR and tF intervals leave industrial per-acre only 10% significant (S2.13 p. 62). (11) The competitive-market classification rests on three states (p. 18).

**What a skeptical referee would raise:** Items marked [derived] are my own arithmetic from the paper's printed numbers. 1. Facility-to-utility link is geometric. Facilities are assigned to HIFLD polygons after the authors reassign overlaps to the smallest polygon (S1.1 p. 25). A campus inside a co-op or municipal 'island' would be counted for the small utility even if the IOU serves it, and the IOU's polygon loses that area. This is plausibly related to the headline ownership result (IOU 0.215, nonprofit near zero) because misassignment pushes nonprofit estimates toward whatever the island utility's prices do. The paper does not validate polygon assignment against serving utility. 2. Start dates are inferred, and tend late. The algorithm takes the later of permit year, DCM year and year built, and uses the CoStar last-sale year as a floor (S1.2 p. 27). Facilities with start dates after construction and interconnection, and sales dates that are not operation dates, would cause timing error against price changes. 3. Counts, not load. A facility is one unit whether 5 MW or 500 MW; MW exists for about 17% of facilities (Table S1). The per-facility coefficient averages enterprise rooms and hyperscale campuses; the 4% hyperscaler figure is a linear acre extrapolation. 4. The 13% is a multiplication at the edge of the data. [derived] The post-2021 mean stock is about 1.8 facilities (from p. 37 statements), the winsorized sample SD is 5.00 and the maximum after winsorizing is 62 (Table S14), so ten facilities is about two winsorized SDs; and the unwinsorized estimate is 0.110 versus 0.182 (Table S14), so the 13% depends on winsorizing at the 99.5th percentile. The log-price version gives 11%. 5. Instrument validity. The share is a national fiber-block share that is high in large urban territories (compliers have 423,606 average residential customers versus 26,761 for never-treated, Table S11). A shift that is zero before 2021 and rising after interacts with an urban-density share, so identification is close to a continuous DiD on dense territories after 2021. Anything that raised real prices more in dense IOU territories after 2021 (grid hardening, wildfire costs in California, storm costs, electrification, inflation in delivery costs) violates exclusion; State x year FE absorb only statewide shocks. The exclusion tests cover residential sales, customers and manufacturing plants, not T&D spending, wildfire, or wholesale and capacity costs. Fig. 2C shows the raw high-fiber series dipping and then rising from 2021 while low-fiber keeps falling, consistent with a post-2021 divergence but not specific to data centers. 6. The authors concede the first stage has pre-trends (S2.17). With pre-AI-growth controls the estimate doubles or more (0.359, 0.681) but first-stage F falls to 17.6 and 10.0, and industrial loses significance; the paper does not choose among these and keeps the lower baseline. 7. A few territories carry the instrument: the top four Rotemberg weights are over 30% (Table S12) including Southern California Edison, not an obvious data center hub in Fig. 1. The estimate rises when they are dropped (0.201), which the authors read as attenuation. 8. IOU confounding. IOU share is 38% of compliers versus 7.8% of never-treated (Table S11). Ownership-by-year FE cut the estimate to 0.137 and ownership-by-state-by-year to 0.108 (Table S21), which the authors dismiss as not preferred. 9. No usage or sales outcome and no delivery-only handling. Price is revenue/sales; a rise could reflect lower residential kWh per customer or a change in the mix of bundled and delivery-only customers in retail-choice states. The paper does not say how delivery-only reporters, ERCOT competitive areas, or Illinois and Ohio utilities are treated, and then uses those same three states for the competition split. 10. Selection into the balanced panel. Only 1,243 of 3,805 territories have price data in all ten years; the dropped 2,562 are small, mostly nonprofit (Table S2). Residential price may be missing for retail-choice delivery utilities. 11. Wholesale spillovers. The authors cluster by balancing authority to address cross-utility spillovers but do not test or control for wholesale or capacity prices; State x year FE absorb statewide wholesale shocks but not within-state differences between utilities in how procurement costs are passed through. Controls and comparison units are not region-contaminated by design except through the fiber-share exposure. 12. Heterogeneity is not identified and is unadjusted for multiple testing: five interaction columns, one at a time, with different FE in two. The competition result is 10% significant. 13. Mechanism evidence is thin: distribution spending sample is 145 IOUs with first-stage F 16.9 (and 7.3 for acres), generation and transmission null. Summer peak effect is significant at 5% only. 14. No event-study or dynamic estimate of the effect; results are average over a stock that grows over time. 15. Data-center list is a commercial vendor listing with no cross-validation against other inventories; no code or data posted. 16. Water: the paper has nothing on water rates or water bills.

**What it would take to reproduce:** No data-availability statement, code repository or replication package appears anywhere in the PDF (searched the text for code, replication, GitHub, Dataverse). Public inputs: EIA-861 Sales to Ultimate Customers and Operational files; HIFLD Electric Retail Service Territories shapefile; FCC Form 477 (fiber-to-end-user block data); Census Value of Construction Put in Place; FERC Form 1 via PUDL; CPI; ACS; Census County Business Patterns; EIA-923. Long-haul fiber from the InterTubes academic project (robustness only). Proprietary or access-limited: Data Center Map (commercial listing, edition not stated) and CoStar property records; the Business Insider permit compilation is a journalist dataset whose public availability I cannot tell from the paper. The headline therefore cannot be exactly reproduced without a DCM and CoStar licence. Exact specification in one line: ivreg of real residential price (cents/kWh, revenue/sales, CPI 2024$) on the utility-state count of operational data centers (winsorized at 99.5th percentile within year), instrumented by PostAI(2021+) x ln(national private data center construction spending) x mean 2017-2019 share of national fiber-containing Census blocks, with utility-state and State x year fixed effects, 1,237 balanced utility-states 2015-2024, clustered by utility-state. Calibration targets: Table 1 col. 3 (0.182, SE 0.034, N 12,370, F 59.43), Table S5 (first stage 0.849, mean 1.17), Table S1 (3,021 operational, 2,749 with start year). Unspecified details that could matter: how utility-state IDs are matched to HIFLD polygons beyond the stated ID merge; how facilities outside every polygon are treated; whether utility-state-years with ownership changes are handled; exact definition of the 'post-2021 mean' used as the denominator (14.01).

**Corrections to what we had heard:** 1. Mostly confirmed, with caveats. The abstract (PDF p. 3, p. 1) says 'A standard deviation of ten data centers raises electricity prices by 13%'. Derivation (p. 12): 10 x 0.182 = 1.82 cents/kWh, divided by the post-2021 mean residential price 14.01 cents/kWh = 13% [derived: 12.99%]. The 13% is for ALL electricity prices per the abstract wording but the calculation is on residential prices. The '11%' the first author mentions matches the log-price version: 0.0108 per facility, 11% for ten (Table S7, S2.4 p. 38). The paper calls ten facilities 'one standard deviation' of the post-2021 stock (p. 12) and an 'un-winsorized' SD (p. 38), but the estimation-sample winsorized stock has mean 1.17 and SD 5.00 (Table S5), so ten is about two winsorized SDs; I am uncertain which SD is meant and the post-2021 SD is not printed. 2. Confirmed with a caveat: 'a single hyperscaler facility increases prices by 4%' (abstract; p. 12) is 0.56 cents/kWh = 4% of 14.01, extrapolated from the acre coefficient 0.00941 times a 60-acre average hyperscaler; it is not a hyperscaler-specific estimate. 3.
