Confirmed: 0.182 cents/kWh per additional operational data center, residential, Table 1 Panel A col. 3, p. 13 (SE 0.034, ***), real 2024 dollars. It is a coefficient on the stock of facilities in the territory, from IV. 4. Confirmed: 'about 30-40% higher in absolute amount' (p. 12); [derived] 0.182 versus 0.139 industrial and 0.130 commercial. The authors add that relative to each class's own price, effects are similar (p. 12; Table S7). 5. Confirmed with units now known. 34.3 MW is 0.343 in hundreds of MW, summer peak, **, Table 2 Panel A col. 3, p. 15. The '$11' is $11.58 million (text: about $11.6 million) of distribution spending in millions of 2024 dollars, per additional data center, ** at 5%, IOUs only (about 145 utility-states, 1,403 obs), Table 2 Panel B col. 3. It is a per-utility-state annual spending coefficient, not a per-customer or per-household figure. 6. Confirmed approximately: about 1,200 utility-states (1,237 in the residential regressions; 1,243 balanced of 3,805, Table S2); 2,749 operational facilities with a year of operation (p. 5; Table S1) out of 3,021 operational facilities; 2,698 also have coordinates; panel 2015-2024. 7. Confirmed with context: Illinois, Ohio, Texas (p. 18; S2.19 p. 79); interaction -0.105* (0.057) on a baseline of 0.236, 'equivalent to a 44% reduced price effect', 'only weakly significant' (Table 3 col. 4). That column uses Year FE not State x Year FE. 8. Confirmed: price-sensitive utilities see 'more than double' (p. 18); 0.127 baseline plus 0.199 interaction (Table 3 col. 3). 9. Version: cover says 'DP21854 (v. 2)'; imprint says 'First Published 19 August 2026' and 'This Revision 02 November 2026'; manuscript title page dated November 2, 2026. So 'v2' is correct, revised November 2, 2026. Additional corrections to our understanding: the treatment is a COUNT of facilities (and separately acres), not MW; there IS an instrument (shift-share fiber); the paper is NOT a difference-in-differences with staggered treatment; price is revenue per kWh in levels (cents), not log, in the headline; data come from Data Center Map plus permits and CoStar, not Aterio.

## Kiran Garimella, "Do Data Centers Raise Residential Electricity Prices? Evidence from 24 U.S. States." Self-posted working paper, no series or number; title page reads "Preliminary Draft: June 2026" (PDF metadata creation date 22 June 2026; 84 pages). No journal, arXiv, SSRN or RePEc version found. Note: arXiv 2606.19777, which a search surfaced next to it, is a different paper (Watten, Bistline, Blanford, EPRI).

- **Link:** https://gvrkiran.github.io/content/AI_data_centers_electricity_bills.pdf
- **How far we got:** read in full. Fetched the author's own openly posted PDF at the start URL (HTTP 200, no access barrier, 917 KB). Read pp. 1-12 as rendered pages and pp. 13-84 as extracted text (pdftotext to stdout), including all 14 tables, the limitations section, references and Appendices A-E. The fetch tool auto-saved a copy at the local fetch cache (webfetch-1794411299074-invedp.pdf); I wrote no files myself. Figures were not read numerically (only captions and the numbers quoted in text). Supplementary checks: the author's homepage (no listing of the paper, no repo link visible on the landing page; publications.html not checked), the Rutgers NJ State Policy Lab blog post of 22 June 2026 summarizing it, and the Interconnection.fyi landing, /data-center and /data-center/state/VA pages (read via a summarizing fetch, so those site facts are less firmly verified than the paper's).
- **Funding or affiliation:** The PDF itself carries no affiliation, acknowledgments, funding statement or conflict-of-interest disclosure (primary: I searched the full text). Secondhand, from the Rutgers New Jersey State Policy Lab blog post dated 22 June 2026 that summarizes the paper (https://policylab.rutgers.edu/?p=11153): the author is identified as an assistant professor at the Rutgers School of Communication and Information, and the Policy Lab states it is funded by the New Jersey Office of the Secretary of Higher Education. Whether this specific paper was funded by the Policy Lab is not stated; I am uncertain. No industry funding is indicated anywhere I looked.
- **Unit:** Primary: "state-ZIP" unit by year (22,834 units; 223,409 regression rows). The outcome is not observed at ZIP level: it is PUMA-level ACS PUMS allocated to ZIPs through Geocorr population weights, so ZIPs in one PUMA share nearly the same value (Sec. 3.2, pp. 18-19). Secondary ("more institutionally appropriate" per the author): utility service territory by year, 602 territories, 5,906 observations (Sec. 3.3, pp. 19-20). Treatment groups at ZIP level: 22,133 never-treated, 298 clean-treated (first data center 2014-2024), 403 always-treated (Table 2, p. 20). At territory level: 510 never-treated, 37 clean-treated, 55 always-treated.
- **Years:** 2014-2024 excluding 2020 (no standard 1-year ACS PUMS), so 10 panel years: 2014-2019 and 2021-2024. Bills are nominal dollars (Table 2 note, p. 20). A separate non-causal "nowcast" extends bills to 2025-2026 using EIA-861M rates through March 2026 (Sec. 6, pp. 49-56).
- **Outcome:** Average monthly household electricity EXPENDITURE in dollars (price times quantity), not a rate per kWh. Built from ACS PUMS household variable ELEP (monthly electricity cost) weighted by WGTP, by PUMA, each year 2014-2024 except 2020; allocated to ZIP/ZCTA with Missouri Census Data Center Geocorr PUMA-to-ZCTA population weights (2018 release with 2012-vintage PUMAs for ACS 2014-2021; 2022 release with 2020-vintage PUMAs for ACS 2022-2024) (Eq. 1, p. 19; Appendix A, p. 78). Sample mean $163.83/month (Table 2). HINCP (income) used in a robustness check; TEN (tenure) in descriptives. The paper does not say how ELEP special codes (electricity included in rent, no charge), top-coding or zero bills are handled; I looked and found no mention. Utility-level outcome is the same PUMS-derived bill aggregated to territories, not EIA-861 revenue per kWh. EIA-861M residential revenue/sales is used only in the nowcast section.
- **Data center data:** Interconnection.fyi (https://www.interconnection.fyi), scraped by the author; Table 1 note says "as of March 2026" (p. 16). The paper describes the site as aggregating interconnection-queue filings from utilities and regional grid operators and collects every record with "Operational" status for the 24 states (Sec. 3.1, p. 15; Appendix A, p. 78). Fields the paper says it observes: facility name, city/county/state and often street address, capacity in MW bins, permit status, grid operator, hyperscaler flag (Yes/Likely/No/Unknown), and four dates (application, approval, proposed operational, actual operational) (p. 16). Full scrape n = 2,434; analytic sample 2,277 after dropping post-2024 dates, invalid ZIPs and failed geocodes (Fig. 1 caption, p. 18). About one third of records had no ZIP and were geocoded (p. 17). 5-10% of project pages returned errors and were dropped (p. 78). Date rule: actual operational date, else approval date, else application date; facilities with no dates are assigned 2013, i.e. always-treated (p. 17). Sec. 6.5 (p. 55) states 76% of facilities lack a recorded operational date. 61% lack capacity and are imputed at 5 MW (p. 47). Is it public: partly. The site's data center directory (https://www.interconnection.fyi/data-center, state pages at /data-center/state/XX) is free to browse; when I looked it listed about 4,000 operational records nationally and 602 operational in Virginia (paper's scrape: 596 VA). The state list pages I saw show only facility name, county, status and capacity bin; I did not open individual project pages, so I have NOT verified that the four date fields exist there. The full dataset is offered through the operator's GridTracker product (pricing not stated; appears to be a paid/licensed export). Important caveat: the site's own directory page attributes the data center records to various public records and points to a sister site, datacenter.fyi, for building-level data; I saw nothing on the site saying the data center records come from interconnection-queue filings. Generation interconnection queues list generators, not loads, so the paper's "interconnection queue filings" label for this inventory is, in my reading, the author's characterization and may be inaccurate. I am uncertain about the true provenance of the dates. The site is live and changing, so the March 2026 snapshot cannot be re-pulled exactly. I did not check the site's terms of use on scraping.
- **Treatment:** Main: has_dc(z,t) = 1 if the state-ZIP has at least one operational data center in year t (absorbing; turns on in the facility's operational year as defined above). Intensive margin: log(1 + cumulative count of operational data centers). Dose-response among always-treated: new_dc (count becoming operational in year t) plus log(1 + cum_dc). Robustness: cumulative MW using bin midpoints with 5 MW imputed for the 61% with no capacity; a hyperscaler indicator; a same-utility spillover indicator for never-treated ZIPs in a utility that gains a clean-treated data center. Utility level: has_dc(u,t) = 1 if any ZIP mapped to the territory has an operational data center. Treatment is a count of listed facilities (some records appear to be individual buildings or street addresses), not load actually served.
- **Method:** Staggered-adoption difference-in-differences on observational data; identification rests on parallel trends. (1) TWFE with state-ZIP and national year fixed effects, SEs clustered by state-ZIP (Eq. 2-3, pp. 21-22). (2) Dynamic TWFE event study, window k in [-5,+5], k = -1 omitted, 298 clean-treated vs 22,133 never-treated (Eq. 4, p. 23). (3) Matched DiD: 5 nearest never-treated neighbors, with replacement, matched on a single covariate, the 2014-2016 mean bill (pp. 24-25). (4) Dose-response within 403 always-treated ZIPs (Eq. 5). (5) Same TWFE and event study at the utility-territory level, unweighted and household-weighted, clustered by territory (Eq. 6, p. 26). Extras: income control, market-structure subsamples, 200-draw placebo, same-utility spillover, and a cohort-size-weighted ATT(g,t) difference-in-means that the author says is not a formal Callaway-Sant'Anna estimator and has no inference (pp. 23, 45-49, 83-84). No state-by-year or region-by-year fixed effects, no weather controls, no instrument.

**Headline findings**

- [primary] Baseline ZIP-level TWFE, extensive margin (has_dc): data center presence associated with higher monthly bill; about 1.3% of the $163.83 sample mean **+$2.093/month, SE 1.207, p = 0.083, N = 223,409, within-R2 0.0006** (Table 3 col. 1, p. 35; Table 8, p. 40; abstract p. 1)
- [primary] ZIP-level TWFE, intensive margin, log(1 + cumulative data centers) **+$0.132, SE 1.320, p = 0.921, N = 223,409** (Table 3 col. 2, p. 35; Table 8, p. 40)
- [primary] Matched DiD (5 nearest neighbors on 2014-2016 mean bill), extensive margin **+$0.901/month, SE 1.231, p = 0.464, N = 16,455** (Table 6 col. 1, p. 38; Table 8, p. 40)
- [primary] Matched DiD with both terms entered: presence positive, log count negative; author attributes to collinearity and prefers column 1 **has_dc +$7.658 (SE 3.205, p = 0.017); log(1+cum_dc) -$8.501 (SE 3.782, p = 0.025)** (Table 6 col. 2 and text, p. 38)
- [primary] ZIP-level event study: pre-period coefficients small and insignificant; post coefficients grow and are significant only at years 4 and 5 **k=-5: 0.62; k=-4: -0.37; k=-3: -0.06; k=-2: 0.41; k=0: 0.67; k=1: 1.83; k=2: 1.44; k=3: 2.23; k=4: 5.57 [1.61, 9.53]; k=5: 5.22 [0.61, 9.82] (k=5 SE 2.349, p = 0.026)** (Table 4, p. 36; Table 8, p. 40)
- [primary] Utility-territory TWFE, extensive margin, unweighted (602 territories) **+$0.352, SE 1.681, p = 0.834, N = 5,906** (Table 9, p. 42)
- [primary] Utility-territory TWFE, extensive margin, household-weighted **-$1.997, SE 3.863, p = 0.605, N = 5,906** (Table 9, p. 42; abstract p. 1)
- [primary] Utility-territory TWFE, intensive margin **unweighted -$0.420 (SE 1.560, p = 0.788); weighted -$4.645 (SE 3.095, p = 0.134)** (Table 9, p. 42)
- [primary] Utility-territory event study (37 clean-treated, 510 never-treated): only k=0 significant, pre-period point estimates all positive, no monotonic build-up **k=-5: 2.26; k=-4: 0.59; k=-3: 2.15; k=-2: 2.34 [-0.60, 5.27]; k=0: 3.57 [0.41, 6.72]; k=1: 2.03; k=2: 1.57; k=3: 2.67; k=4: 4.51 [-1.88, 10.89]; k=5: 1.43 [-4.90, 7.76]** (Table 10, p. 43)
- [primary] Dose-response among 403 always-treated ZIPs: negative; author attributes to mean reversion, not a protective effect **new_dc -$0.480 (SE 0.384, p = 0.212); log(1+cum_dc) -$22.300 (SE 3.878, p < 0.001); N = 4,006** (Table 7, p. 39)
- [primary] Dose-response among 55 always-treated utility territories (text only, no table) **new_dc -$0.31 (p < 0.001); log cumulative -$10.11 (p = 0.007)** (Sec. 5.3.4, p. 44)
- [primary] Adding time-varying log household income: presence coefficient shrinks and loses significance **+$1.835, SE 1.188, p = 0.122; within-R2 rises to 0.162** (Table 11 row (ii), p. 46; text p. 47)
- [primary] Treatment measured in cumulative MW (bin midpoints; 5 MW imputed for 61% with no capacity): sign flips negative **-$0.011 per MW, SE 0.003, p = 0.001; log(1+MW) -$0.611, SE 0.392** (Table 11 row (i), p. 46; text p. 47)
- [primary] Market-structure subsamples: none significant, and all three are below the pooled +$2.09 **Regulated -$0.931 (SE 1.195); Restructured +$0.946 (SE 1.567); Hybrid (MI only) -$1.539 (SE 1.533)** (Table 11 row (iii), p. 46)
- [primary] Hyperscaler heterogeneity: no differential effect **has_dc +$2.424 (SE 1.295) / has_hyperscaler -$1.661 (SE 2.735, p = 0.54)** (Table 11 row (iv), p. 46; text p. 47)
- [primary] Placebo, 200 random assignments of 298 never-treated ZIPs **mean $0.019, s.d. $0.991, 5th-95th percentile [-1.50, 1.77]; 2% of draws at or above $2.09** (Table 11 row (v), p. 46; text p. 48)
- [primary] Same-utility spillover test: own-presence positive but insignificant; spillover to other ZIPs in the same utility negative and significant **own +$1.935 (SE 1.207, p = 0.109); spillover -$1.371 (SE 0.177, p < 0.001)** (Table 11 row (vi), p. 46; text p. 48)
- [primary] Cohort DiD ATT diagnostic (no standard errors reported) **average post-treatment +$2.436/month; by event time e=0: -0.09, e=3: 2.09, e=4: 5.97, e=5: 4.68, e=6: 7.45, e=9: 14.77; lead e=-10: 7.99** (Table 11 row (vii), p. 46; Appendix E Table 14, p. 84)
- [primary] Sample construction counts **22,834 state-ZIP units, 24 states, 223,409 obs; 2,277 analytic data centers in 23 states (2,434 scraped); TX 651, VA 552, CA 438, OR 268; 701 units (3.1%) ever have a data center** (Tables 1-2, pp. 16, 20; Fig. 1 caption p. 18; Table 13, p. 79)
- [primary] Nowcast (descriptive, not causal): projected population-weighted mean bill and gradient by data center count **$199 (2024) to $209 (2026), +5.0%; pass-through lambda = 0.73; ZIP-level correlation of projected increase with data center count -0.03; +5.8% in ZIPs with none vs +1.6% in ZIPs with six or more; held-out 2024 MAE $16.7 vs $15.6 for simple persistence** (Sec. 6.1-6.4, pp. 50-55; Table 12, p. 54)
- [primary] Utility-level rate DiD on EIA-861M 2020-2025, which the author declines to report as causal (identified off two transitions) **about -1.6 cents/kWh, customer-weighted; only 2 of 186 reporting territories gain a first data center in 2021-2025** (Sec. 6.5, pp. 55-56)

**Heterogeneity reported:** By market structure (regulated -$0.93, restructured +$0.95, Michigan hybrid -$1.54; none significant; Table 11, p. 46). By hyperscaler flag (no differential; flag only available for within-panel facilities). By event time (ZIP level: near zero in years 0-3, about +$5 at years 4-5; utility level: no build-up). By treatment margin (presence positive, count and MW flat or negative). By level of aggregation (ZIP +$2.09 vs utility +$0.35 unweighted, -$2.00 weighted). By state only descriptively (Appendix D figures; no state-specific estimates). No heterogeneity by income, tenure, ISO/RTO region (e.g. PJM vs others), utility ownership type, or facility size beyond the hyperscaler flag. Nothing on water prices; water appears only as a future-research paragraph (p. 68).

**Limitations the authors state:** The author is candid (Sec. 3.5 p. 21; Sec. 7.3 pp. 62-66; Sec. 6.6 p. 56): (1) inventory is an undercount, probably half or fewer of operational U.S. data centers, so some "never-treated" ZIPs host unobserved facilities; (2) panel ends 2024, before the 500 MW-1 GW hyperscale wave, so nulls should not be extrapolated; (3) 2020 missing; (4) operational dates imputed from approval or application dates for a substantial fraction, which can distort leads and lags; (5) presence/count treatment rather than load; (6) few covariates, no weather or housing controls; (7) PUMA-to-ZIP smoothing is non-classical measurement error that attenuates ZIP-level effects and can compromise matching if controls share a PUMA with treated units; (8) ZIP is not the unit at which rates are set (SUTVA), and regional wholesale-market effects (PJM, ERCOT) are not absorbed by national year effects; (9) pooled heterogeneous regulatory regimes; (10) only 298 clean-treated ZIPs and 37 clean-treated territories, so wide intervals that cannot rule out meaningful positive effects; (11) TWFE event studies subject to staggered-adoption contamination; (12) outcome is expenditure, so a rate increase offset by lower consumption would be missed; (13) ZIP-level SEs clustered by state-ZIP even though the author says utility territory would be the theoretically right level (Sec. 4.6, p. 27); (14) utility crosswalk is a 2019 snapshot. The author's own summary is that the results are absence of evidence, not evidence of absence (p. 72).

**What a skeptical referee would raise:** 1. The outcome has no real ZIP-level variation. ELEP is observed by PUMA (about 100,000 people) and spread to ZIPs by population share. The 22,834 "units" and 223,409 observations therefore overstate the independent information by a large factor, and state-ZIP clustering treats mechanically duplicated outcomes as independent. Standard errors on the ZIP results, including the p = 0.083 headline, are very likely too small; clustering by PUMA or utility was not shown. The 200-draw placebo does not fix this because it randomizes at ZIP level too. 2. PUMA boundaries were redrawn starting with the 2022 ACS, and the paper switches crosswalks at that point. That creates a ZIP-specific break in the outcome series between 2021 and 2022 that neither ZIP nor year fixed effects absorb, and it lands on the peak facility-entry years (2022: 180, 2023: 244 new facilities, p. 17). No check is reported. 3. Treatment provenance and timing are weak. The inventory is a third-party web directory scraped once (March 2026); the site itself attributes its data center records to various public records, not queue filings. Sec. 6.5 says 76% of facilities lack a recorded operational date, and undated ones are assigned 2013; dated ones may use application or approval dates. The date rule also has an internal inconsistency: Sec. 3.1 says undated "Existing" facilities are set to 2013, while Sec. 3.1.1 says the 2005 spike reflects that imputation. The 298 clean-treated ZIPs that identify everything may be partly artifacts of which records happen to carry a date. 4. Counts look like buildings or listings, not campuses (the directory includes bare street-address entries; Oregon shows 268 and Texas 651 "operational data centers"). 61% have no capacity and are set to 5 MW, so the MW result (-$0.011/MW) is mostly imputation plus a few large Loudoun-type ZIPs. 5. Survivorship: only currently "Operational" listings are scraped, and 5-10% of project pages errored and were dropped as stale. 6. Only national year fixed effects. With treatment concentrated in TX, VA, CA and OR (over 80% of facilities) and bills moving very differently by state after 2021, the coefficient can pick up state or regional trends. The subsample results are a warning: all three market-structure estimates (-0.93, +0.95, -1.54) sit below the pooled +2.09, which suggests the pooled number partly reflects between-group trend differences. No state-by-year specification is reported; the author says it would absorb too much variation. 7. The utility-level analysis, called primary, is thin. Outcome is the same smoothed PUMS bill, not a utility's actual residential revenue per kWh from EIA-861, which is public annually and would be the natural rate measure. Each ZIP is forced to one utility (IOU preferred) from a 2019 snapshot. The big data-center utilities (Dominion, Oncor-area retailers, PG&E, PGE) are always-treated, so the has_dc estimate comes from 37 territories, probably small ones. Binary presence at territory level ignores load share. Pre-period event-study coefficients are all positive (2.15 to 2.34). In Texas and other retail-choice states the wires utility in the crosswalk does not set the residential price. 8. Outcome choice. Household expenditure in nominal dollars mixes price, consumption, weather, home size, heating fuel and household composition. Data-center ZIPs are growing suburbs where new, larger, all-electric homes change bills independent of rates. Only income is controlled, and it already knocks the estimate to p = 0.122. Handling of ELEP codes for electricity included in rent or no charge, and top-coding, is not described. 9. Matching is on one variable (2014-2016 mean bill), which is post-treatment for 2014-2016 entrants, and matched controls can share a PUMA with the treated ZIP, giving near-identical outcomes by construction. 10. Multiple estimates, selective emphasis either way. The one consistently positive pattern (about +$5 at years 4-5, cohort ATT rising to +$6 to +$15 at long horizons) is discounted as thin; the nulls are underpowered. The paper cannot distinguish "no effect" from "effect of a few dollars," which the author concedes. 11. Draft quality signals. Leftover text contradicts current results: Sec. 5.3.5 (p. 44) says the ZIP event study showed significant pre-treatment divergence, while Table 4 shows none; text gives within-R2 of 0.0001 but Table 3 prints 0.0006; Sec. 7.3 lists MW treatment and market-structure subsamples as future work although Sec. 5.4 reports them; references to an "earlier unwindowed specification" and an "earlier same-3-digit-ZIP spillover interpretation" show specifications were revised after seeing results. Not peer reviewed, single author whose field is information science rather than energy economics. None of this makes the null wrong, but the numbers should be cited as preliminary. 12. Scope: electricity bills only, pre-2025 only, 24 states chosen by the author. It says nothing about water prices.

**What it would take to reproduce:** Nothing is posted. The only availability statement is in Appendix A (p. 78): "Collection scripts are available on request." No GitHub repo, data archive, or replication package is linked in the PDF, on the Rutgers blog post, or on the author's landing page (I did not check his publications sub-page). Regression code and the assembled panel are not mentioned at all, only collection scripts. Public inputs we could rebuild: (a) ACS 1-year PUMS household files 2014-2019 and 2021-2024 for 24 states, variables ELEP, WGTP, PUMA, HINCP, TEN (Census); (b) Geocorr PUMA-to-ZCTA population allocation factors, 2018 and 2022 releases (https://mcdc.missouri.edu/applications/geocorr.html); (c) NREL OpenEI "U.S. Electric Utility Companies and Rates: Look-up by ZIP Code (2019)" (https://data.openei.org/submissions/4042); (d) EIA-861M for the nowcast (https://www.eia.gov/electricity/data/eia861m/). The outcome side is fully reproducible in principle; undocumented choices (ELEP special codes, top-codes, which ZCTA-to-ZIP convention, how border ZIPs are split) will cause small differences. The blocker is the facility inventory. It is a March 2026 scrape of Interconnection.fyi's data center directory (https://www.interconnection.fyi/data-center and /data-center/state/XX). The site is live and has changed (Virginia operational count 602 when I looked vs 596 in the paper's scrape), list pages show only name, county, status and capacity bin, and the dates the paper relies on would have to come from individual project pages (which I did not open) or from the operator's GridTracker export, which appears to be a paid product. To reproduce the +$2.09 one would need the author's snapshot with his geocodes and imputed operational years; ask him for it by email. Before any automated collection from that site, a person should read its terms of use; I did not, and I did not scrape it. A practical alternative for an independent study: use an inventory with documented provenance and dates, and use EIA-861 annual residential revenue, sales and customers by utility as the rate outcome, which avoids the PUMA smoothing problem and matches the level at which rates are set. With that design the paper's utility-level numbers (+$0.35 unweighted, -$2.00 weighted) are the comparison point, not the ZIP-level +$2.09.

**Corrections to what we had heard:** 1. "From interconnection-queue filings": the paper's actual source is a scrape of the website Interconnection.fyi (March 2026), which the paper describes as an aggregator of queue filings. The site's own data center directory credits various public records and I found no statement there that those records are queue filings. Treat the queue-filing label as the author's description, not an established fact. 2. 2,277 is the analytic sample; the scrape was 2,434 (Fig. 1 caption, p. 18). Facilities are in 23 states; Connecticut has zero. 3. "2014-2024" is 10 years, not 11: 2020 is excluded. 4. "ZIP panel" overstates the geography: units are state-ZIP pairs and the bill is PUMA-level PUMS data allocated to ZIPs, not ZIP-level observations. 5. The utility-territory -$2.00 (p = 0.605) is the household-WEIGHTED estimate; the unweighted estimate is +$0.35 (p = 0.834). Both are in Table 9 (p. 42). 6. Matched DiD +$0.90 has p = 0.464 (the brief said only "n.s."). A second matched column gives +$7.66 (p = 0.017) for presence with a -$8.50 count term. 7. The brief omits results that cut the other way from a clean null: ZIP event study +$5.57 and +$5.22 at years 4 and 5 (both significant at 5%), and a cohort ATT averaging +$2.44. It also omits the income-controlled +$1.84 (p = 0.122) and the MW specification at -$0.011/MW (p = 0.001). 8. Dating: the PDF says June 2026; the Rutgers blog post cites the same URL as a March 2026 working paper, so the file has been revised in place. The version I read is the 22 June 2026 build; I cannot rule out a later silent revision after today. 9. Data/code: not posted. Only collection scripts are offered, and only on request.

## Daniel Yue and Yiyang Zeng (both Scheller College of Business, Georgia Institute of Technology), "The Local Economic Effects of Data Center Entry." Working paper, SSRN abstract 6497238, DOI 10.2139/ssrn.6497238. SSRN metadata (archived snapshot of 2026-09-04): Date Written March 30, 2026; Posted April 3, 2026; 52 pages; no revision date shown. The copy I read is dated "March 2026" on its title page (PDF created 2026-03-27, 52 pages). Zeng's site lists it as "Under review". I could not confirm whether SSRN now carries a later revision, because SSRN returned 403.

- **Link:** https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=6497238 (403 to automated access; download by hand to confirm the current version). Full text actually read from an openly hosted third-party copy: https://centralina.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/GeorgiaTech_DataCenter_March2026_IBTS.pdf
- **How far we got:** read in full. SSRN returned HTTP 403 and I did not try to get around it. Neither author's site hosts a PDF: danielyue.github.io and yiyang-zeng.com/research.html link only to SSRN, and the Georgia Tech news pages (hg.gatech.edu/node/691073, Scheller news) do the same. The Wayback Machine holds the SSRN abstract page (web.archive.org/web/20260904070124/...), which I used for metadata, but no archived PDF. A web search found a 52-page PDF hosted openly by Centralina Regional Council (a North Carolina regional council of governments), which I read in full: text, Tables 1-8, A1 and B1, and Figures 3, 4, 5 and 17. Provenance caveats: - The copy is hosted by a third party, not by the authors or Georgia Tech. - Its creation date (2026-03-27) is three days before SSRN's "Date Written" (2026-03-30). The page count matches (52) and the abstract matches SSRN's word for word, but small differences from the SSRN file are possible. - Someone should download the SSRN PDF by hand and spot-check Tables 3 and 8 against the numbers below. Disclosure against the no-files rule: I saved temporary working copies (the PDF, extracted text and figure crops) in the session scratchpad only, at a local scratch folder (yz_pdf/paper.pdf) and alongside it. No project files were created or changed. Page references are the paper's printed page numbers; the PDF page is the printed page plus 1.
- **Funding or affiliation:** Both authors are at the Scheller College of Business, Georgia Tech. The copy I read has no acknowledgments, funding statement or disclosure section. The facility data come from a commercial vendor (Aterio); the paper does not say whether access was purchased or donated. I am uncertain whether the SSRN version adds a disclosure.
- **Unit:** County-quarter panel of U.S. counties. Treatment is assigned at the county level; electricity prices are utility-level values blended up to counties.
- **Years:** Economic outcomes: 2000Q2-2024Q4, 99 quarters (Sec. 3.2, p. 10; Table 1 notes, p. 25). Figure headers say "[2000Q2-2025Q1]". Electricity prices: Table 1 Panel B and its notes say EIA-861M, quarterly, 2010-2024 (p. 25), but the electricity event-study figure header also says 2000Q2-2025Q1 (Fig. 17, p. 44), so the true electricity window is ambiguous. Event-study plots show 12 quarters before to 12 quarters after activation.
- **Outcome:** Electricity: the paper says it uses "Energy Information Administration (EIA) Form 861M, which reports monthly retail electricity prices by utility" (Sec. 3.1.3, p. 9). Outcomes are log residential, commercial, industrial and total price in $/MWh (Table 1 Panel B; Table 8). County mapping, as the paper describes it: - Where a county is served by several utilities, the authors "compute sales-weighted average prices across all serving utilities using EIA service territory files" (pp. 9-10). - "Spillover exposure" is the number of unique other counties sharing at least one utility with the county. The median county shares utilities with 63 others, and only 23 counties (0.7%) have zero exposure (p. 10). What the paper does not say: - whether price is revenue divided by sales (I assume so, since that is what 861M supports, but it is not stated); - how the monthly EIA-861M sample of utilities is reconciled with the annual EIA-861 territory file; - whether the weights are utility-total sales or county-specific sales (EIA does not publish county-level sales, to my knowledge); - how utilities absent from the monthly form are handled. Economic outcomes: - BLS QCEW total private-sector employment, total wages and establishments (logs); - Census SAIPE median household income; - BLS LAUS unemployment rate; - Census Building Permits Survey (Sec. 3.1.2, p. 9). There is no water price or water use outcome anywhere in the paper.
- **Data center data:** Aterio, a commercial data provider, so not public. The paper describes it as aggregating "utility filings, permit records, satellite imagery, and industry sources". For each facility it reports latitude and longitude, county FIPS, activation date, operational status (active, announced, under construction, canceled), facility type, operator name, utility connections and estimated MW capacity (Sec. 3.1.1, p. 8). - Activation dates come straight from Aterio. The paper reports no validation of those dates against press releases, permits or utility records. - "Big Tech" means Amazon, Google, Microsoft, Meta, Apple or Oracle. - Counties are matched to outcomes by FIPS code. - Metro status uses OMB CBSA delineations; rural-urban codes are from USDA ERS; population is from Census PEP.
- **Treatment:** Treatment is the quarter of activation of the FIRST data center in a county, using the activation date in the Aterio data (Sec. 3.1.1, p. 8; Sec. 3.2, p. 10). It is a binary, absorbing, first-entry treatment that takes no account of facility size. - All facility types count: hyperscale, colocation and enterprise (p. 4). - Median first-entry nameplate capacity is 10 MW, and over 55% of first entries are 10 MW or below (Fig. 4, p. 31). Table 1 gives treated-county capacity as median 12.5 MW, mean 106.7 MW (p. 25). - The authors estimate the median first entry at about 44 GWh a year, roughly 1.2% of the median serving utility's sales of about 3,600 GWh (Sec. 3.3.4, p. 13). - Controls are never-treated counties in the main results; not-yet-treated counties are a robustness check (Appendix B). - No announcement or construction-start date is used, and subsequent entries are not modeled except in an isolated-versus-clustered split.
- **Method:** Estimator: Callaway and Sant'Anna (2021) staggered difference-in-differences, with group-time ATTs aggregated to an overall ATT and to event-time ATTs (Sec. 3.3, pp. 11-12). - Main control group: never-treated counties. - Covariates: log population, log land area, percent white, percent aged 20-39, percent aged 40-64, percent male. - Reference period: the quarter before activation (e = -1). - Standard errors: county-clustered bootstrap, 1,000 reps (figure headers). - "Certain counties are automatically dropped from the estimation to ensure balanced panels" (p. 12), with no further explanation. Identification rests on parallel trends between treated counties (78% metro) and never-treated counties (2% metro), conditional on those covariates. The support offered is flat pre-period coefficients over 12 quarters. Electricity: the full-sample event study is described as noisy, with no clear effect (p. 18; Fig. 17a). The authors then split treated counties at the median of spillover exposure "among treated counties in our restricted sample" (64 or fewer versus more than 64 shared counties) and run the estimator separately in each half against never-treated controls (Sec. 3.3.4; Table 8). The 5% result is the low-spillover half. They read a positive effect in low-spillover counties and null or negative effects in high-spillover counties as a sign of attenuation from shared utilities, not as a formal test. Robustness: - Adjacent-county controls (Table A1, p. 45): 280 treated, 803 adjacent controls, employment ATT 0.0317. - Not-yet-treated controls (Table B1, p. 47): employment ATT 0.0340. - Extra splits by MW, population and rural-urban code appear as figures only (Appendix C). - None of these robustness checks is reported for electricity prices.

**Headline findings**

- [primary] Total electricity price, LOW-spillover counties only (64 or fewer shared counties): the source of the 'about 5%' claim. Significant at the 5% level. **ATT 0.0501 log points (SE 0.0225), N treated = 168** (Table 8, p. 28; text Sec. 4.7, p. 18)
- [primary] Residential price, low-spillover counties. Significant only at the 10% level. **ATT 0.0358 (SE 0.0189), N treated = 168** (Table 8, p. 28)
- [primary] Commercial price, low-spillover counties. Significant only at the 10% level. **ATT 0.0361 (SE 0.0205), N treated = 168** (Table 8, p. 28)
- [primary] Industrial price, low-spillover counties. Significant at the 5% level. **ATT 0.0664 (SE 0.0283), N treated = 168** (Table 8, p. 28)
- [primary] HIGH-spillover counties (more than 64 shared counties): every point estimate is negative, and total price is negative at the 10% level. **Total -0.0539 (SE 0.0296); residential -0.0241 (0.0173); commercial -0.0290 (0.0211); industrial -0.0008 (0.0299); N treated = 131** (Table 8, p. 28)
- [primary] Full-sample electricity price: no pooled ATT is tabulated. The authors say they 'do not detect a clear treatment effect in the full sample'. Plotted post-period points sit between about -0.005 and +0.013 with confidence intervals spanning zero; the figure header reads Treated = 94 counties, Control = 2,065. **No pooled estimate reported; described as null** (Sec. 4.7, p. 18; Fig. 17a, p. 44)
- [primary] Low-spillover event study for total price rises gradually after activation, reaching roughly 0.05-0.06 by quarters 9-12 (read off the plot, approximate). Pre-period points are mostly within about plus or minus 0.02. **About 0.05-0.06 at quarters 9-12 (visual read)** (Fig. 17b, p. 44)
- [primary] Employment, baseline with never-treated controls. **ATT 0.0345 (SE 0.0100), 95% CI 0.0149 to 0.0542; table note says 301 treated counties** (Table 3, p. 26)
- [primary] Total wages, baseline. **ATT 0.0496 (SE 0.0116), 95% CI 0.0268 to 0.0723** (Table 3, p. 26)
- [primary] Establishments, median household income, unemployment rate and building permits, baseline. The unemployment estimate is insignificant even though the abstract says entry reduces unemployment rates. **Establishments 0.0467 (0.0113); log median household income 0.0189 (0.0049); unemployment rate -0.0316 pp (0.1578); log building permits 0.1613 (0.0462)** (Table 3, p. 26)
- [primary] Metro versus non-metro counties. **Employment: metro 0.0406 (0.0104), N = 208; non-metro 0.0009 (0.0315), N = 93. Wages: metro 0.0549 (0.0120); non-metro 0.0170 (0.0371). Non-metro unemployment rate -0.7313 pp (0.3333)** (Table 5, p. 27)
- [primary] Entry cohort: effects are near zero for the 2020-and-later cohort, which is 212 of the 301 treated counties. **Employment: 2009 and earlier 0.0329 (0.0143), N = 36; 2010-2019 0.0499 (0.0149), N = 53; 2020+ 0.0073 (0.0072), N = 212** (Table 6, p. 27)
- [primary] The employment and wage event studies reach only about half the headline size within the plotted 12 quarters, so the 3.5% and 5.0% figures reflect longer horizons in earlier cohorts. **Employment about 0.017 and wages about 0.02 at quarter 12 (visual read); figure header reads Treated = 107, Control = 2,598** (Fig. 5, p. 32)
- [primary] Sample and scale: treated versus control counties, and the size of the first-entry load. **447 treated and 2,846 control counties; 78.1% of treated are metro versus 1.7% of controls; median first entry 10 MW, about 44 GWh a year, about 1.2% of the median utility's load** (Table 1, p. 25; Sec. 3.3.4, p. 13; Fig. 4, p. 31)
- [secondhand] Press claim that in the first three years employment rose 0.9%, wages 1.1% and establishments 1.0%. These numbers do not appear in the text or tables of the copy I read; they may be averages of the event-study coefficients. They come from the authors' article in The Conversation and its reprints. **0.9% / 1.1% / 1.0%** (Not in paper; The Conversation article, seen via search summaries only)

**Heterogeneity reported:** - Metro status (Table 5): gains are concentrated in metro counties; non-metro employment and wage effects are about zero. The non-metro unemployment rate falls 0.73 pp. - Operator (Table 4): Big Tech wages 0.0715 versus 0.0468 for others. Employment is 0.0399 (SE 0.0242, not significant) versus 0.0358. No formal test of the difference is reported. - Entry cohort (Table 6): 2009-and-earlier and 2010-2019 cohorts are positive; the 2020+ cohort is about zero. - Clustering (Table 7): counties with one or more additional data centers within 5 years (N = 88) versus isolated entries (N = 118): employment 0.0427 versus 0.0357, wages 0.0771 versus 0.0602. - Electricity (Table 8): split by spillover exposure only. There is no electricity split by facility MW, by load share of the utility, by utility ownership type, by regulated versus restructured state, by RTO, or by cohort. - MW size, county population and rural-urban code splits are shown only as figures (Appendix C) and only for economic outcomes.

**Limitations the authors state:** Sec. 5.3, pp. 21-22: 1. The authors cannot observe occupation or skill mix, so the mechanism is untested. 2. Electricity identification remains contaminated even in low-spillover counties. They argue the true effect may be larger, and that a first entry is only about 1% of utility sales, so cumulative effects of later entries may be larger. 3. Event-study graphs cover only three years after activation. 4. Location may respond to expected growth. The authors write that they "cannot fully rule out selection on unobservable growth potential." Elsewhere: - The clustered-versus-isolated difference may be selection (p. 17). - Blending prices across multiple utilities adds noise (p. 10). - The weak 2020+ effects may reflect short post-periods (p. 16).

**What a skeptical referee would raise:** 1. The 5% is a subsample result, not the main estimate. The full-sample effect is null. The headline comes from a median split with no stated pre-specification, and the other half shows a marginally significant NEGATIVE total-price effect (-5.4%, p<0.10). Attenuation from shared utilities predicts shrinkage toward zero, not a sign flip. Opposite-signed effects of similar size in the two halves look more like different underlying price trends by utility type (small municipal or co-op territories versus large investor-owned ones) than like a causal effect plus measurement error. Roughly averaging the halves gives about zero, which matches the pooled null. 2. The residential number is weak on its own terms. Residential 3.6% and commercial 3.6% are significant only at 10%, with a rough 95% interval of -0.1% to +7.3% for residential. Four price outcomes times two subsamples were tested with no multiple-testing adjustment. 3. Implausible magnitude. The authors show the median first entry adds about 1.2% to utility load. A 5% rise in the all-sector average price from a roughly 1% load shock is hard to rationalize through rate-base or fuel channels. It suggests confounding, later entries, or composition effects. 4. Standard errors are likely too small for electricity. Price varies at the utility level, yet clustering is by county. Many counties share one utility's price series, so utility or state clustering is the natural choice. 5. Contaminated controls. Never-treated counties that share a utility with a treated county receive the same price change. Controls are not restricted to utilities with no data-center exposure. 6. The price measure is thin on detail. - The paper cites EIA-861M (monthly). Outside the paper, my understanding is that the monthly form covers a sample of larger utilities with the rest estimated, so small municipal and co-op utilities, which are exactly the low-spillover territories, may be poorly covered. I am uncertain about this and it should be checked against EIA documentation. - If price is revenue per kWh, a new large customer on a low rate mechanically moves the commercial, industrial and total averages, and total price shifts with sector mix. Residential is the cleanest series and has the weakest result. - The service-territory file lists counties served but not county sales, so "sales-weighted" county prices probably use utility-wide weights. 7. Treated and control counties are not comparable. Treated counties are 78% metro and about 8 times larger; controls are 2% metro. Covariates are only population, land area and demographics. There is no matching, no state-by-time or region-by-time effects, and no metro-only control group. Twelve quarters of flat pre-trends is limited reassurance over a 25-year panel. 8. The headline depends on aggregation weights. 212 of 301 treated counties are 2020+ entrants with effects near zero. The 3.5% employment ATT is carried by 89 earlier-cohort counties with long post-periods, while the event study shows about 1.7% at three years. 9. Treatment timing. Activation dates are from a proprietary vendor and unvalidated. Construction runs 18-36 months before activation, so the pre-period is partly treated; this biases economic effects but matters less for prices. Any facility counts, including roughly 10 MW enterprise sites, which is far from the hyperscale policy question. 10. Sample counts do not reconcile: 447 treated (Table 1), 442 (Fig. 2), 301 (Table 3), 107 (Fig. 5 header), 206 (Table 7), 299 (Table 8), 94 (Fig. 17a header), 146 (Fig. 4). The dropping rule is one unexplained sentence. The electricity window is given as 2010-2024 in Table 1 but 2000Q2-2025Q1 in the figure header. If prices start in 2010, the 36 pre-2010 entrants have no pre-period, yet 299 of 301 counties appear in Table 8. 11. No electricity robustness. Adjacent-county and not-yet-treated checks are reported only for economic outcomes. There is no dose-response by MW or load share, no alternative spillover cutoffs, and no placebo. 12. Text and tables disagree in places. The abstract says entry reduces unemployment rates; the baseline is -0.03 pp and insignificant. "Big Tech generates larger effects" rests on untested differences. 13. Nothing on water.

**What it would take to reproduce:** The copy I read mentions no posted code, data or replication package, and the authors' sites link none. The binding constraint is Aterio, which is proprietary. A public-data approximation would need a substitute list of facility activation dates and MW by county. Any substitute changes the treated set, so only a rough match to 0.050 should be expected. Public inputs for the electricity result: 1. EIA-861M (formerly EIA-826) monthly sales and revenue by utility, state and sector, used to compute revenue per MWh and aggregate to quarters. 2. EIA-861 annual Service Territory file (utility by county) to map utilities to counties and to count unique other counties sharing any utility. The paper does not say which year's territory file was used. 3. County prices as sales-weighted means across serving utilities, presumably using utility-level sales as weights. 4. County covariates: Census PEP population and age, sex and race shares, plus land area; OMB CBSA delineations. 5. Callaway-Sant'Anna estimation on a county-quarter panel with never-treated controls, covariates, base period -1 and a 1,000-rep county-clustered bootstrap. Figure titles read "CSDID (With Controls)", which suggests Stata csdid or the R did package. 6. Split treated counties at 64 shared counties (64 or fewer versus more than 64) and estimate each half against all never-treated controls. Targets: 168 and 131 treated counties, 2,805 controls; total price 0.0501 and -0.0539. Ambiguities to resolve before any reproduction: - the true electricity sample window (2010-2024 or 2000-2025); - the rule that drops counties for "balanced panels" (447 becomes 301 becomes 299); - how utilities missing from the monthly form are handled; - whether log price is of the blended level; - whether the aggregation is the "simple" overall ATT. Economic outcomes (QCEW, SAIPE, LAUS, Building Permits Survey) are all public. A useful independent check would re-estimate with utility-level clustering and with controls limited to counties whose utilities serve no treated county.

**Corrections to what we had heard:** 1. "Retail electricity prices up about 5%" is not the paper's main estimate. The full-sample effect is null (Sec. 4.7, p. 18). The 5.0% (ATT 0.0501, SE 0.0225) applies only to the low-spillover half of treated counties (168 counties). The high-spillover half shows -5.4% (p<0.10) (Table 8, p. 28). 2. The 3.6% / 3.6% / 6.6% split is confirmed in Table 8 (0.0358, 0.0361, 0.0664), also low-spillover only. Residential and commercial are significant only at the 10% level; industrial at 5%. 3. Employment +3.5% and wages +5% are confirmed (Table 3: 0.0345 and 0.0496). These are overall ATTs across all post-treatment quarters. The three-year event-study effects are about half that. The 0.9% / 1.1% / 1.0% three-year figures quoted in the press are not in the text or tables of the paper. 4. "First opening" is more precisely the first data center ACTIVATION per Aterio, of any type or size (median first entry 10 MW), not hyperscale openings. 5. The price source is EIA Form 861M (monthly), not the annual EIA-861 detailed file. Service-territory files are used for the county mapping. 6. The estimator is Callaway-Sant'Anna with never-treated controls, which is consistent with "county staggered DiD". 7. Dates: SSRN shows written March 30, 2026 and posted April 3, 2026 ("about March 2026" is close enough). 8. Zeng is also at Georgia Tech Scheller. The brief listed an affiliation only for Yue. 9. The paper contains no water outcome, so it cannot inform the water-price half of the study question.

## Owen Kay, Robert Reaser and Reid Taylor (author order randomized), "Processing Power: The Effect of Data Centers on Wholesale Electricity Markets," Federal Reserve Bank of Dallas, Research Department Working Paper No. 2606. First version dated March 17, 2026 (landing page shows March 20, 2026); latest version "Revised: October 9, 2026" (file wp2606r1.pdf, PDF created Oct 9, 2026, 105 PDF pages: 58 pages of text, references, Appendices A-H). DOI of the revision: https://doi.org/10.24149/wp2606r1. JEL L94, P18, Q41, Q42, Q48. Working paper, not peer reviewed ("preliminary drafts circulated for professional comment").

- **Link:** https://www.dallasfed.org/~/media/documents/research/papers/2026/wp2606r1.pdf
- **How far we got:** read in full. Direct fetch of the Dallas Fed's own openly posted PDF of the October 2026 revision (no access barrier, no workaround needed). Text extracted locally with pdftotext to stdout (nothing written by me; the fetch tool itself cached the PDF at the local fetch cache (webfetch-1794411300206-9df8zo.pdf)). Read all 105 PDF pages: main text, references, and Appendices A-H. Tables 1, 3, 4 and A8 re-read in layout mode to confirm column alignment. Figures 2, 10 and 15-18 viewed as page images because the retail results (Figs 15-18) are choropleth maps with no numeric labels. Landing page https://www.dallasfed.org/research/papers/2026/wp2606 checked: it links only the original (wp2606.pdf) and revised (wp2606r1.pdf) PDFs. I did not read the March original, so I cannot say what changed between versions. Page cites below use the paper's printed page number; PDF page = printed page + 2.
- **Funding or affiliation:** Federal Reserve Bank of Dallas working paper. Authors: Owen Kay (Williams College), Robert Reaser (University of California, Davis), Reid Taylor (Federal Reserve Bank of Dallas). No external funding disclosed. Standard disclaimer that views are the authors' and not those of the Dallas Fed or Federal Reserve System. Acknowledges comments from Bushnell, Hausman, Kilian, Muehlegger, Rapson, and a discussion by Mar Reguant; presented at NBER EEE Spring Program, Electricity Camp in the Rockies, CAISO, Dallas Fed, UC Davis, UC Santa Barbara. Relies on commercial data from S&P Global, Bloomberg/DC Byte and YES Energy; no industry sponsorship stated.
- **Unit:** Simulation, not a regression on outcomes. Model unit: thermal generating unit x hour (3,382 units in 2025; 149,429,424 unit-hours in the 2021-2025 fit table), solved jointly across 23 regions (the 22 contiguous-US EPA eGRID subregions, with SRVC split into SRVC-N = Dominion zone/northern Virginia plus 18 NE North Carolina counties, and SRVC-S) linked by 42 capacity-constrained interfaces. Results are reported at region x month, region x year, and load-weighted national level. There is no household, utility, or retail-rate unit of analysis.
- **Years:** Ex post 2021-2025 (observed load and supply); forecast 2026-2028 (constructed load and supply). Fixed-cost-share calibration for the retail section pools 2021-2024 (Texas 2022-2024) and is held fixed for 2025 and 2028.
- **Outcome:** Primary outcome: the model-generated regional "wholesale price," defined as the shadow value (lambda) on each region's hourly power-balance constraint in a least-cost dispatch, in 2024 dollars per MWh. The paper says this "should be interpreted as a benchmark for the competitive wholesale price" (p.20): it is built from unit marginal costs, not offers, and excludes start-up costs, scarcity pricing, intra-regional congestion and capacity markets (capacity markets explicitly out of scope, fn 4 p.6). In vertically integrated regions it is a system-lambda analogue, not a price anyone pays. National figures are load-weighted by no-data-center demand. Secondary outcomes: average variable cost of thermal generation (Fig. 7), payments/production costs/rents (Table 3), CO2 (Fig. 8). Observed prices enter only for validation and calibration: real-time LMPs at 93 nodes in the seven ISO/RTOs (hurdle rates, App. C.5; fit, Tables A6-A7). Retail: there is NO retail or residential price outcome. EIA-861 appears only as a calibration input: (a) all-sector average retail price = revenue per MWh across residential, commercial, industrial and transportation customers, by subregion, 2024 values used for 2025 (p.44, fn 43), for the demand-elasticity robustness check; (b) state average retail rate = total revenue from sales to ultimate customers / total sales (App. G.1, p.100) for the fixed-cost share s = 1 - (load-weighted wholesale hub price / EIA-861 average retail rate) in ISO/RTO states. In non-market states s = 1 - (fuel + purchased power)/operating revenue from FERC Form 1 via Catalyst Cooperative PUDL, used only if Form 1 filers serve at least half of retail load. The paper does not use EIA-861 residential average revenue per kWh as an outcome anywhere. No water outcome of any kind.
- **Data center data:** S&P Global 451 Research Data Center KnowledgeBase (DCKB): facility-level location, data center type, net IT power, interconnection (utility) capacity, PUE, operational dates, with separate records per building on a campus (Section 3.4 p.15; App. C.3). Proprietary subscription product, not public. Utilization rates and training/market shares by type come from Bloomberg/DC Byte, also proprietary, and the values are not printed in the paper. Intraday shape from EPRI (2026) "Powering Intelligence 2026," p.14. Planned projects 2026-2028 come from the same DCKB, haircut to 60% (mid) or 30% (low) of announced capacity. One inconsistency: Appendix Table A1 (county characteristics, p.65) says data center counties are those "with at least one non-cancelled data center in the Cleanview sample," a different source from DCKB; Cleanview is also cited (fn 69) for the claim that about 25% of planned capacity will connect behind the meter. Supply-side forecast data partly proprietary too (YES Energy interconnection probabilities; S&P SNL node locations).
- **Treatment:** The "treatment" is the entire modeled data center load, removed in a counterfactual: effect = model shadow price with data center load minus model shadow price with ALL data center load removed in the same period (Eq. 9, p.21; "All effects are reported relative to a no-data-center counterfactual in the same time period," p.17). This includes the roughly 134 TWh of data centers already operating in 2021 (p.45), and the facility list includes crypto mines (App. C.3, p.79). It is therefore the effect of the whole data center stock versus zero, not the effect of AI-era growth. Data center load inputs (Section 3.4, Table 1 p.17, Fig. 2 p.18, App. C.3 pp.78-80, App. F.1 p.95): - Maximum potential load per facility = net IT power (MW) x PUE, from S&P Global 451 Research Data Center KnowledgeBase (DCKB). PUE "typically ranges from 1.3 to 1.5." For planned facilities net IT is imputed: grid load = rho x utility power, rho = mean of Net IT/(Utility Power/PUE) by type from 2024-2025 vintages (fn 23). - Utilization: Bloomberg/DC Byte average annual utilization rates and market shares by data center type. A share of hyperscale, cloud and neo-cloud capacity equal to the training market share runs at 100% every hour; the remainder runs at type-specific rates. The paper does NOT print the utilization rates or the training share. - Four scenarios. High: 100% of reported capacity, 100% every hour, 100% of announced planned capacity. Mid-Range-Peak (preferred): Bloomberg/DC Byte utilization, load-following intraday shape after EPRI (2026) (highest 11AM-3PM, lowest 10PM-4AM; hyperscale +10%, colocation +30%, other types +50% above their rate at peak, symmetric below overnight), 60% of announced planned capacity. Mid-Range-Constant: same daily energy, flat profile, 60% of announced. Low: 50% of Mid-Range-Constant, flat, 30% of announced. - New-facility ramp to full capacity, linear by month: crypto mines 3 months; hyperscale, cloud, neo-cloud, wholesale, reseller 9; telco and system integrators 12; retail and hosting 24. - Implied national consumption (Fig. 2): mid-range 134 TWh in 2021, 319 TWh in 2025, 521 TWh in 2028; High 979 TWh in 2028; Low 261 TWh in 2028. - Regional "Own DC share," 2024-2025 (Fig. 10, p.40; the term is not defined in the figure notes, I am uncertain whether the denominator is total or non-data-center load): SRVC-N 32.6%, MROW 13.3%, NWPP 13.2%, ERCT 12.0%, AZNM 10.4%, SRSO 6.8%, NYUP 6.2%, SRVC-S 5.9%, SRTV 5.9%, RFCW 5.8%, RMPA 5.3%, SPSO 3.4%, CAMX 3.2%, RFCE 2.5%, NYCW 1.3%, NEWE 1.0%, SPNO 1.0%, SRMV 1.0%, RFCM 0.6%, FRCC 0.5%, SRMW 0.3%, NYLI 0.2%, MROE 0.2%.
- **Method:** Structural short-run economic dispatch model (linear program), solved day by day with a six-hour look-ahead, jointly across 23 regions (Section 4, Eqs. 2-8, pp.18-20). Extends Hausman (2025, AER) and Ham, Kay and Hausman (2025, RFF) by making inter-regional trade endogenous. - Supply: EPA CEMS hourly unit data; marginal cost = heat rate x fuel cost + variable O&M + permit price (Eq. 1); capacity = 99th percentile of observed output, derated for planned outages plus stochastic unit-day forced outages; ramp limits and ramp costs for coal and combined cycle; one aggregate battery per region. - Residual demand: EIA-930 hourly demand minus solar, wind, hydro, nuclear, geothermal, biomass, cogeneration (EIA-923/930), minus observed Canadian net imports. Hydro and all non-thermal output are held fixed across counterfactuals. - Trade: 42 interfaces with limits from NERC's 2024 Interregional Transfer Capability Study, supplemented by the 99th percentile of EIA-930 interchange; regional import caps; season-by-interface hurdle rates (mean $7.15/MWh, about 92% congestion component) estimated from congestion and loss components of observed LMP gaps. - Counterfactual: re-solve with data center load removed; fuel prices, non-thermal generation, generator availability and (in the baseline) all other demand and the entire generation fleet held fixed. The authors call it "a short-run partial-equilibrium exercise" (p.20). - Forecast: non-data-center load grown at EIA AEO 2021 regional rates with month-block-bootstrapped 2021-2025 load shapes; supply from EIA-860m plus YES Energy probability-derated additions; fuel paths from AEO 2025; supply additions treated as exogenous. There is no statistical identification of the price effect: no control group, no standard errors or intervals on any headline number. Uncertainty is conveyed only by the four load scenarios and robustness runs. Credibility rests on model fit: unit-hour generation correlation 0.634 (0.784 monthly, 0.867 annual; Table A5); ISO/RTO price correlation 0.678 hourly, 0.840 monthly, 0.929 annual after winsorizing at 1/99 and dropping Winter Storm Uri (0.408 hourly unwinsorized; Table A7). The one regression in the paper is a validation exercise (Eq. 11, Table 2, p.25): IV of regional hourly thermal output on residual demand, instrumented with observed demand, with subregion x date, subregion x month x hour-of-day and interconnection x hour fixed effects, SEs clustered by date; own thermal units meet 0.64 of a local shock in the data versus 0.62 in the model. Retail section (Section 7, App. G): an accounting identity for a utility recovering fixed and variable cost through one volumetric rate, calibrated with model outputs. Not estimated from retail data.

**Headline findings**

- [primary] National load-weighted wholesale price effect of all data center load in 2025, Mid-Range-Peak (preferred) scenario, versus a no-data-center counterfactual. 2021-2025 period mean is 5.9%. **+$2.66/MWh, +6.9% (2025); 5.9% mean 2021-2025** (Table 4 Panel A, epsilon=0 row, p.46; Appendix Table A2 p.65; abstract; intro pp.2-3)
- [primary] Northern Virginia (SRVC-N) wholesale price effect, Mid-Range-Peak. The abstract and intro say 'by 2025' / 'As of 2025', but the figure that carries the number is labeled a 2024-2025 average. **+$5.22/MWh, +15.0%** (Figure 4 (titled 2024-2025) p.29; text pp.27-28; intro p.3)
- [primary] Northern Virginia is not the largest regional effect. Pacific Northwest (NWPP) is larger; California (CAMX) is third in dollars despite little data center load, attributed to foregone imports. Smallest is Florida (FRCC). **NWPP +$5.68 (15.4%); CAMX +$3.70 (8.6%); MROW +$3.45 (12.1%); ERCT +$3.15 (12.6%); FRCC +$0.42 (1.0%), all 2024-2025 Mid-Range-Peak** (Figure 4 p.29; text p.28)
- [primary] National effect in 2028 by scenario. Low scenario 5.2%; High scenario (all announced capacity at 100% utilization) is described by the authors as a highly unlikely upper bound. **Mid-Range-Peak +$4.01/MWh, +12.3% (period mean 2026-2028 10.0%); Low +5.2%; High +$17.88/MWh, +55.2%** (p.27; Table 4 Panel B p.46)
- [primary] Regional effects in 2028, Mid-Range-Peak. Data center component exceeds 10% of the wholesale price in 10 of 23 regions and 5% in 19. High-scenario SRVC-N figure of $416 (1,176%) is called 'an implausible magnitude' by the authors; 96.7% of forecast hours there have no market-clearing solution (fn 34). **SRVC-N +$9.76 (27.6%); NWPP +$7.77 (25.9%); CAMX +$6.96 (17.2%); ERCT +$6.10 (28.0%); RFCW +$5.11 (15.7%); FRCC +$0.34 (0.8%)** (Figure 5 p.30; text pp.29-31)
- [primary] Range of monthly national effects 2023-2025 across scenarios; peak month was September 2022 during the gas price spike. **Mid: $1.22-$3.94/MWh (4.2%-10.1%); High up to $5.79 (15.5%); Low minimum $0.69 (2.3%); Sept 2022 mid-range peak $6.37** (p.26, Figure 3)
- [primary] Decomposition of the increase in spot-valued generator payments, Mid-Range-Peak, 2021-2028. Inframarginal rents are 50.6% of the payment increase by my division (paper says 'roughly 50%'). Ex post 2021-2025: payments +$96.5B, inframarginal +$51.3B (share 0.531). Forecast 2026-2028: +$90.2B, +$43.2B (share 0.479). About one third of the payment increase ($64.3B of $186.7B) is in non-restructured regions where no wholesale price is actually paid. **Payments +$186.7B; production costs +$87.9B; inframarginal rents +$94.5B; marginal rents +$4.3B (2024 dollars)** (Table 3 p.34; text pp.34-36; rent shares p.46)
- [primary] Data center load inputs behind every result (national electricity consumption assumed). **Mid-range: 134 TWh (2021), 319 TWh (2025), 521 TWh (2028). High: 979 TWh (2028). Low: 261 TWh (2028).** (Figure 2 p.18; text p.17; Table 1 p.17)
- [primary] Condition for load growth to lower retail rates. Under Assumption 1 (data center load adds NO fixed system cost; all costs recovered through one uniform volumetric rate across classes), the offset ratio is OR = ((1-s)/s) x ((1+d)/eta), where s = fixed-cost share of the retail rate, d = percent increase in demand from data centers, eta = short-run supply price elasticity (= d/v, v = percent rise in average variable cost; wholesale price in market regions, average generation cost in vertically integrated regions). Break-even fixed-cost share s* = (1+d)/(1+d+eta). 'Load growth can therefore only put downward pressure on retail rates if s > s*' (equivalently OR < 1). It is a necessary condition conditional on Assumption 1, which the authors say 'is unlikely to hold in reality.' **OR = ((1-s)/s)((1+d)/eta); s* = (1+d)/(1+d+eta); rates can fall only if s > s*** (Section 7, Assumption 1 p.51, Eq. 14 p.52, Eq. 15 p.53; derivation Appendix G pp.98-99)
- [primary] Retail results are shown only as maps (no table of eta, d, s* or OR by region). Local-shock version (eta from a 5% demand perturbation in one region at a time, ignoring spillovers): 'in many parts of the country' fixed-cost spreading could outweigh the variable-cost increase; ERCOT is the only region with OR > 1 in both 2025 and 2028 (the 2028 map also shades northern Virginia above 1). Global version (own-region d, price change from all data centers): 'considerably more regions' have OR > 1; SRMW (central MISO) would need fixed costs to be 'nearly all of the retail bill.' TVA region (SRTV) excluded. **Qualitative only; ERCOT OR > 1 in both years under local shock** (Figures 15-18 pp.53-57; text pp.54-56)
- [primary] Calibrated fixed-cost share s (all sectors) by region, the one retail input that is tabulated. Lowest: ERCT, SRVC-N, SPSO. Highest: NEWE, SPNO, CAMX, NY. Industrial-class s is much lower where available (ERCT 0.165, SRVC-N 0.297, NY 0.325). **ERCT 0.440; SRVC-N 0.465; SPSO 0.506; SRMV 0.585; NWPP 0.587; RFCW 0.599; RMPA 0.614; AZNM 0.629; SRMW 0.631; RFCE 0.652; MROW 0.656; RFCM 0.681; SRVC-S 0.684; SRSO 0.690; FRCC 0.692; MROE 0.703; NY (all three) 0.721; CAMX 0.729; SPNO 0.736; NEWE 0.739; SRTV missing** (Appendix Table A8 p.102; method App. G.1 pp.100-101)
- [primary] Robustness to price-responsive non-data-center demand (full wholesale-to-retail pass-through assumed). Central case epsilon = -0.3. **2025 national effect: 6.9% (eps 0), 6.6% (-0.1), 6.2% (-0.3), 5.0% (-1.0). 2028: 12.3%, 11.8%, 11.0%, 8.8%. Crowd-out share at -0.3: 10.5% ex post, 6.9% forecast** (Table 4 p.46; Section 6.3 pp.44-47)
- [primary] Holding inter-regional trade fixed at observed levels (the Hausman 2025 approach) barely moves the national number but roughly triples the northern Virginia effect and understates California. **National 2025: 7.8% ($2.99) trade-fixed vs 6.9% ($2.66) baseline; 2021-25 mean 5.7% vs 5.9%** (Appendix Table A2 p.65; Figure 10 p.40; Section 6.1 p.39)
- [primary] Supply-side robustness in the forecast period. If 25% of new supply is treated as a response to data centers, the baseline overstates the effect by at most 2.44 percentage points. Reversing planned (mostly coal) retirements changes mid-range effects by at most 1.84 points. Slower renewable build-out raises 2028 effects, most in ERCT. **Endogenous entry: at most -2.44 pp; no retirements: at most 1.84 pp; ERCT 2028: 29.4% (full build) -> 42.4% (50% solar, no new wind) -> 48.0% (25% solar, no new wind)** (Section 6.2 pp.41-43; Figure 11 p.41; App. Figs A7, A9)
- [primary] Model fit and how local shocks are served. NWPP, the region with the largest estimated effect, is the worst-fit region on this test (model sign is wrong). **Share of a local demand shock met by own thermal units: 0.64 data vs 0.62 model overall; NWPP 0.15 vs -0.14; ERCT 0.76 vs 0.93; SRVC-N 0.31 vs 0.24. Price correlation hourly 0.678 winsorized (0.408 raw); modeled price SD 22.81 vs observed 36.01 (winsorized)** (Table 2 p.25; Tables A6-A7 pp.92-93; Section 4.3 pp.22-24)
- [primary] Incremental grid CO2 attributed to data centers. Range is across scenarios; valued at a social cost of carbon of $224 per metric ton. **8.9 to 28.1 million tons CO2 per month by end of 2025 (about $24B-$75B per year); cumulative 784.0 Mt 2021-2025 and 937.4 Mt 2026-2028, Mid-Range-Peak** (Section 5.3 pp.36-37; Table 4 p.46)
- [primary] Timing of the effect: about 75% of the added wholesale payments fall outside the top price decile of hours, so peak-hour curtailment addresses only part of it. **about 75% outside the highest price decile, 2026-2028, Mid-Range-Peak** (p.31; Figure 6 p.32)
- [primary] Cost-minimizing siting counterfactual: ERCOT hosts 19.9% of realized 2024-2025 compute versus about 44% under a short-run cost-minimizing planner; northern Virginia 14.6% versus 1.7%. National monthly price up to $2/MWh lower 2021-2025, up to $8 in the forecast. **ERCT 19.9% actual vs ~44% optimal; SRVC-N 14.6% vs 1.7%** (Section 6.4 pp.48-50; Figures 13-14)
- [secondhand] Background share of US electricity used by data centers, as cited by the paper from LBNL (Shehabi et al. 2024); I did not open the LBNL report in this task. **1.9% (2018), 4.4% (2023), up to 12% by 2028** (p.8)

**Heterogeneity reported:** By region (23 eGRID-based regions, Figs 4, 5, 10, 11, 12): 2024-2025 effects range from 1.0% (FRCC) to 15.4% (NWPP); 2028 from 0.8% (FRCC) to 28.0% (ERCT) and 27.6% (SRVC-N). Spillovers: regions with little data center load (CAMX, SRMW, RFCE/RFCM/RFCW, NYCW/NYLI) get sizable effects through foregone imports. By scenario: Low, Mid-Range-Peak, Mid-Range-Constant, High. By time: monthly series with summer and winter peaks and a September 2022 gas-price peak. By market structure: restructured vs non-restructured (Table 3). By price decile of hours (Fig. 6). By fuel on the margin (coal share of incremental generation, Fig. 9). By demand elasticity (Table 4, Fig. 12). Fixed-cost share s reported for all sectors and for industrial (Table A8). Not reported: anything by customer class for prices (no residential-specific result), by utility, by state, or by ownership type; no numeric table of supply elasticity eta, break-even share s*, or offset ratio by region.

**Limitations the authors state:** Stated by the authors: (1) Short-run partial equilibrium; supply additions and retirements exogenous (tested only with a 25% endogenous-entry case). (2) Prices are a competitive benchmark from marginal costs; no strategic bidding, start-up costs, scarcity pricing or intra-regional constraints; fit is conservative in the upper deciles. (3) Capacity markets are outside scope (fn 4). (4) Data center utilization is unobserved; AI training vs inference not observed; behind-the-meter generation not observed; planned net IT capacity imputed. (5) Payments are valued at spot prices and are not realized cash flows; PPAs, hedges and cost-of-service regulation attenuate and delay pass-through; "the incidence of such changes to payments and rents is outside the scope" (p.36). (6) In non-restructured regions lambda has no explicit mapping to a wholesale price and rents should flow back to ratepayers. (7) Assumption 1 in the retail section (no added fixed cost) "is unlikely to hold in reality," so the retail analysis likely understates rate effects; costs assumed recovered uniformly across classes; if data centers pay wholesale directly there is no fixed-cost spreading; not everything outside energy cost is a spreadable fixed cost (fn 50). (8) FERC Form 1 excludes public power and co-ops; TVA region dropped. (9) Baseline holds other demand fixed (addressed in Section 6.3); forecast method does not account for data centers crowding out other load growth (fn 68). (10) AVC excludes nuclear fuel and renewable maintenance. (11) Model over-trades relative to observed flows (1.21x on the 11 interfaces that map to EIA-930). (12) Hydro scheduling constraints not modeled (fn 61); unit degradation ignored. (13) The planner siting exercise is an upper bound. (14) Emissions exclude behind-the-meter and backup generation.

**What a skeptical referee would raise:** What a skeptical referee would raise, most important first. 1. It does not measure what our study asks. There is no residential or retail price outcome and nothing on water. The headline numbers are simulated shadow prices of a dispatch model. The retail section is a calibrated accounting identity shown only as maps, under an assumption the authors themselves call unlikely. Citing "6.9%" as an effect on household bills would be wrong; energy is roughly 26% to 56% of the all-sector retail rate by the paper's own Table A8 (1 minus s), and pass-through is not estimated. 2. The counterfactual is "zero data centers," not "no AI boom." All data center load is removed, including about 134 TWh already operating in 2021 and crypto mines, while the generating fleet is held fixed. A grid that had never served that load would have been built differently, so a fixed-fleet counterfactual overstates the price effect of the legacy stock. The paper offers no decomposition into pre-existing load versus post-2021 growth. The 25% endogenous-entry test applies only to 2026-2028 additions. 3. The treatment is assumed, not observed, and its key parameters are unpublished. Load = proprietary DCKB capacity x PUE x Bloomberg/DC Byte utilization rates that the paper never prints. The implied 319 TWh for 2025 is the single most important input and cannot be audited. I am uncertain about this, but from memory of the LBNL 2024 report (176 TWh in 2023; 325-580 TWh by 2028) the mid-range path looks toward the high end of published estimates; I did not verify that in this task. Because supply is convex, any overstatement of load is amplified in prices. The 60% and 30% haircuts on planned capacity and the 50% Low scenario are round-number judgment calls with no empirical basis given. 4. No statistical uncertainty on any headline number. No standard errors, no intervals, no report of how many stochastic outage or bootstrap draws were used or how results vary across draws. The Low-to-High scenario band is not a confidence interval. 5. Fit is weakest where the effects are largest. In NWPP (largest ex post effect, 15.4%) the model's own-thermal response to a local shock is -0.14 versus 0.15 in the data (Table 2). Hydro is treated as fixed non-dispatchable supply, which in a hydro-dominated region forces every incremental MWh onto thermal units or imports. Hourly price correlation is 0.408 before winsorizing; modeled prices have about two thirds the observed dispersion. The model matches levels of prices; the paper's claim depends on the slope of supply, which is validated only indirectly through Table 2. 6. Capacity markets are excluded. In PJM, the channel most visibly reaching bills from data center load has been capacity auction prices. Leaving that out biases the paper's "wholesale effect" down for PJM regions, while points 2 and 3 bias it up; the net direction is unknown. 7. "Half is a transfer" is a spot-valuation construct. About one third of the payment increase is in vertically integrated regions with no wholesale price, where the authors say the rents should flow back to ratepayers. In restructured regions hedges and PPAs alter incidence. The paper says incidence is out of scope, yet the abstract and text describe a transfer "from existing customers." The share is 0.531 ex post and 0.479 in the forecast, so "roughly half" is fair as arithmetic only. 8. The retail "sufficient statistics" are fragile. s = 1 - wholesale/retail treats taxes, capacity, ancillary services, public-purpose charges and retail margins as spreadable fixed cost, which overstates the spreading benefit; statewide values are pasted onto subregions (all three NY regions share one s; Pennsylvania enters two regions); Form 1 covers investor-owned utilities only. The "global" eta divides own-region d by a price change caused by all regions' data centers, so regions with almost no data center load mechanically get eta near zero and s* near one. That is a spillover result, not evidence about what happens when load is added locally. No region-level numbers are published, so the maps cannot be checked. 9. Internal inconsistencies suggest the revision is not fully cleaned: abstract says northern Virginia "by 2025" but the number is a 2024-2025 average; Figure 17's note describes the global elasticity although the figure is the local one; Figures 17-18 notes describe s as a "share of total costs comprised of capital expenditure costs," which is not the formula in App. G.1; Table A1 cites a "Cleanview sample" rather than DCKB; App. C.3 calls the High scenario "Full-Capacity"; p.46 calls epsilon = -0.1 the "upper bound"; the conclusion claims a transmission-expansion result that I could not find in this version; "Own DC share" in Fig. 10 is undefined. 10. Forecast-period inputs are layered assumptions: AEO 2021 growth for non-data-center load, proprietary YES Energy completion probabilities, bootstrapped weather, AEO 2025 fuel paths. The 2028 numbers are scenario outputs. 11. Working paper, not peer reviewed; one author is at the issuing institution. No replication package.

**What it would take to reproduce:** No code or data is posted. The paper has no data-availability statement and the Dallas Fed landing page links only the two PDFs. Exact reproduction is not possible from public data because three inputs are proprietary: S&P 451 DCKB (facility capacity, PUE, dates), Bloomberg/DC Byte (utilization and training shares, values not printed), and YES Energy (forecast supply), plus S&P SNL node coordinates. Public pieces: EPA CEMS and eGRID; EIA-923, 930, 860, 860m, 861; AEO 2021 and 2025; NERC 2024 ITCS; ISO/RTO LMPs; RGGI/CCA/EPA permit prices; FERC Form 1 via PUDL. The closest public code base is the replication package for Hausman (2025, AER) "Power Flows," which this model extends; I did not check whether that package is posted. What we could approximate without rebuilding the dispatch model: (a) The retail break-even test. The formulas are fully stated: s* = (1+d)/(1+d+eta), OR = ((1-s)/s)((1+d)/eta). Table A8 gives s for 22 regions. A "global" eta can be backed out as d/v using the regional percent price effects in Fig. 4 (2024-2025) or Fig. 5 (2028) for v and Fig. 10's "Own DC share" for d. Caveats: d's denominator is undefined in the paper, Fig. 10 shares are 2024-2025 only, and the paper uses 2025 and 2028 single-year values. My own back-of-envelope, NOT from the paper: if SRVC-N's 32.6% is a share of total load, d is about 0.48, eta about 3.2, s* about 0.32 against s = 0.465, so OR < 1; for ERCT d is about 0.14, eta about 1.1, s* about 0.51 against s = 0.440, so OR > 1. Both match the colors on the paper's global map (Fig. 16/18), which supports that reading but does not confirm it. (b) The s calibration itself is reproducible from EIA-861 state revenue and sales, public ISO hub prices, and PUDL Form 1 (fuel + purchased power over operating revenue), pooled 2021-2024. (c) The national 6.9% cannot be reproduced without a dispatch model and a load series. A public stand-in for load would be LBNL or EPRI state-level data center consumption estimates, which would change the answer. To request from the authors: region-by-year table of d, eta (local and global), s*, and OR; the utilization rates and training share by type; regional data center TWh by year; number of outage and bootstrap draws.

**Corrections to what we had heard:** The brief's numbers all appear in the primary document, but several need qualification. 1. "+15.0% northern Virginia by 2025": the abstract says this, but the supporting figure (Fig. 4, p.29) is a 2024-2025 average, Mid-Range-Peak. Northern Virginia is also not the largest effect; NWPP (Pacific Northwest) is 15.4%. 2. "+6.9% nationally by 2025" is the 2025 value under the preferred Mid-Range-Peak scenario ($2.66/MWh), relative to removing ALL data center load including the pre-2021 stock and crypto mines. The 2021-2025 mean is 5.9%. It is a simulated competitive benchmark price, not an observed price change. 3. "12.3% by 2028" is the Mid-Range-Peak scenario with planned capacity cut to 60% of announced; Low is 5.2% and High is 55.2%. 4. "About half is a transfer to inframarginal generators": $94.5B of $186.7B over 2021-2028 (50.6%), valued at spot prices rather than realized cash flows; 0.531 ex post, 0.479 forecast. About a third of the total is in regions with no wholesale market, where the authors say it is not a persistent windfall. 5.
