"Conditions under which load growth lowers retail rates": the paper gives a break-even condition (s > s* = (1+d)/(1+d+eta)) that holds only if data centers add zero fixed system cost and rates are uniform and volumetric. It does not show that rates fall anywhere; results are maps only, and with spillovers many regions fail the condition. The paper frames this as a rebuttal to the fixed-cost-spreading argument, not support for it. 6. "23 regions" are the 22 contiguous-US eGRID subregions with SRVC split in two, not 23 ISO/RTO markets. 7. The start URL and dates are correct: revision dated October 9, 2026; DOI suffix wp2606r1. Author first names: Owen Kay, Robert Reaser, Reid Taylor, order randomized. 8. For the study's purposes: the paper contains no residential price estimate and nothing on water.

## Fernando E. Alvarez, David Argente, Joyce Chow, Diana Van Patten. "Data Centers and Local Economies in the Age of AI: A Shift–Share Approach." NBER Working Paper No. 35194. Issued May 2026; latest version on the NBER site is dated "May 2026, Revised September 2026" (title page). JEL D8, O3. DOI 10.3386/w35194. The first version also circulated as Becker Friedman Institute Working Paper No. 2026-67 (May 2026; BFI page dated May 12, 2026) and on SSRN (abstract 6753441, not opened). The September 2026 revision is a near-total rewrite of the May version: new instrument, longer sample, water outcome added, and a much weaker electricity result.

- **Link:** https://www.nber.org/system/files/working_papers/w35194/w35194.pdf (landing page https://www.nber.org/papers/w35194). May 2026 first version: https://bfi.uchicago.edu/wp-content/uploads/2026/05/BFI_WP_2026-67.pdf (NBER also lists it at https://www.nber.org/system/files/working_papers/w35194/revisions/w35194.rev0.pdf, not opened).
- **How far we got:** read in full. Both PDFs were served openly with no access block, no login and no workaround. (1) Latest version: the NBER PDF URL above returned the September 2026 revision; I read all 56 PDF pages (cover, abstract, main text pp. 2-26 including references, and the 29-page Online Appendix A-H). (2) First version: the BFI institutional PDF; I read all 21 PDF pages (numbered pp. 2-20). I wrote no files. The fetch tool cached its own copies automatically at the local fetch cache (webfetch-1794411340639-i4yrue.pdf) (NBER Sept 2026 revision) and the local fetch cache (webfetch-1794411317941-naahqx.pdf) (BFI May 2026). Pages were read as rendered images, so every number below was read off the page by eye; small table digits (especially 5-decimal entries in Appendix D and F) carry a small transcription risk and should be spot-checked before publication. I did not open the Bahar and Wright paper, so how they cite this study is not verified here.
- **Funding or affiliation:** Alvarez: University of Chicago and NBER. Argente and Van Patten: Yale School of Management and NBER. Chow: Yale University. No funding or acknowledgments statement appears in either version read. The NBER title page notes that at least one co-author has disclosed additional relationships of potential relevance; the NBER landing page lists disclosures for Alvarez (central-bank visiting and consulting roles and litigation consulting, per the page summary; secondhand via page fetch, not in the PDF). No industry or utility funding is disclosed in the paper. DCKB access is through a Yale subscription. First version was also issued in the Becker Friedman Institute working paper series. Not peer reviewed.
- **Unit:** U.S. county. Two designs in the September 2026 revision: (a) pooled annual county-by-year panel with county and state-by-year fixed effects; (b) county-level long differences from 1995 to each endpoint year with state fixed effects. Preferred sample is restricted to counties with observed 1870 population and boundaries stable by 1870 plus complete controls: 1,577 counties in the pooled sample and 1,547 in the long-difference sample (1,498 counties for electricity; 45,079 county-years for the pooled electricity regression). That is roughly half of U.S. counties. May 2026 version: county long differences from 1995 to 2005/2010/2015/2020 on about 3,130 counties (2,954 to 2,997 for electricity).
- **Years:** September 2026 revision: electricity outcome 1995-2023 (EIA source through 2023); pooled panel 1995-2023; long-difference endpoints 2000 through 2023; pre-period placebo changes for electricity 1990-1994; early-level balance uses logged electricity price in 1990. Other outcomes: CBP and FHFA through 2023, IRS through 2022, USGS public-supply water 2000-2020. May 2026 version: long differences 1995 to 2005, 2010, 2015, 2020.
- **Outcome:** Electricity price is a county-level average retail price constructed by the authors from EIA Form 861: utility revenue, electricity sales and service-territory information, aggregated to counties (Sept 2026, Section 2, p. 5; reference list cites EIA-861 detailed data files). It enters in natural logs, so the coefficient is read as an approximate elasticity with respect to cumulative data-center revenue (Table 1 and Table 2 notes). The September version does not state which customer sector is used. The May 2026 version is explicit (p. 6): county price is built by summing revenue and MWh across the utilities serving a county and dividing revenue by sales, and the main electricity outcome is the TOTAL (all-sector) price. So this is all-sector average revenue per unit sold, NOT a residential price series and not a bill. I am uncertain whether the September revision kept the all-sector definition; nothing in it says otherwise. No residential, commercial or industrial split is reported in either version. Neither version explains how a multi-county utility's revenue and sales are allocated across the counties it serves, nor how the pre-2001 years (1990-1995) were mapped to counties. Water (not the focus here): USGS public-supply withdrawals, HUC12 reanalysis allocated to counties by area weights, 2000-2020.
- **Data center data:** 451 Research Datacenter Knowledge Base (DCKB), a proprietary facility-level database maintained by S&P Global Market Intelligence, accessed through a Yale subscription (reference list, p. 26 of the Sept version). NOT public. It holds facility coordinates, ownership, operating dates, capacity measures including utility power, facility type (wholesale, hyperscale, powered shell, retail and others) and currently reported annualized revenue (Sept 2026, Section 2, p. 5). U.S. facilities are mapped to counties by coordinates. The May version says DCKB covers more than 12,960 facilities across over 2,700 providers and 131 countries (May, p. 4). The Sept version reports 316 U.S. counties with a first mapped build by 2023 (Figure H.1 notes) and uses 1,457 foreign scale-oriented facilities in 54 countries for the shift (footnote 9). Top states by facility count in DCKB: Virginia 373, then Texas and California (Figure 1). Build year is DCKB's reported build year. A 2026 PeeringDB snapshot is used only as a robustness cross-check (Appendix H.3): 146 PeeringDB facilities could not be matched to DCKB.
- **Treatment:** Continuous, not an entry dummy. Treatment D_ct is the inverse hyperbolic sine (IHS) of cumulative annualized data-center revenue, in millions of dollars, attached to facilities built in county c from 1995 through year t (Appendix A.1, eq. 6; Table 1 notes). Important measurement detail: each facility's CURRENTLY reported annualized revenue is assigned to its reported build year and cumulated, so the series is today's reported attributes back-cast onto build cohorts, not realized historical revenue (footnote 2, p. 5; Figure 2 notes, p. 8). Long differences use the 1995-to-endpoint change in D. Mean 1995-2023 treatment change is about 0.24 IHS points (24.3 percent) in the pooled sample and 0.238 in the long-difference sample (pp. 12, 16); Appendix D reports mean changes of 0.217 and 0.214. The instrument is weighted toward "scale-oriented" facilities (wholesale, powered shell, hyperscale). The paper has no megawatt-based treatment in the regressions, although DCKB has utility-power capacity. May 2026 version: change in log (IHS) cumulative annualized revenue 1995 to horizon, with alternates using cumulative facility counts and ever-data-center indicators (Table 6).
- **Method:** September 2026 revision (current): 2SLS with historical-geography instruments. Two county exposure measures: (1) proximity to terrain-predicted least-cost transcontinental routes between six terminal pairs from the 1855 Pacific Railroad survey map, computed on NOAA ETOPO 2022 elevation; (2) an interaction of concentrated 1870 rail coverage (share of county within 10 km vs 50 km of the 1870 network) with access-node geometry (internal endpoints and junctions relative to boundary crossings). Pooled design: the two are averaged into one share s_c and multiplied by a leave-U.S.-out shift G_t (IHS cumulative count of foreign wholesale, powered-shell and hyperscale facilities built since 1995), with county and state-by-year fixed effects and state-clustered errors (eqs. 2-4). Long-difference design: the two measures enter separately as instruments for the 1995-to-endpoint change in treatment, with state fixed effects and "Early" or "Early + terrain" controls (log 1870 population, land area, water proximity, ruggedness, temperature, lower-order rail terms, 1986 CBP and 1989 IRS levels; plus elevation, dispersion, slope) (eq. 5; Appendix A.4). The authors say identification rests mainly on exogenous shares (Goldsmith-Pinkham et al. logic). Weak-IV-robust Anderson-Rubin inference is used when first-stage F is below 10, which is the case for electricity. First stage is weak: long-difference joint F is 1.8 to 2.1 for 2000-2005 endpoints, 4.3 to 4.7 for 2010, and only reaches about 11.6 to 13.7 by 2020; partial R-squared is 0.010 to 0.011 (Table B.1). Validation: 48 early-level joint balance tests, none rejecting at 5 percent (Appendix F); pre-1995 placebo changes shown in Figures 6-7. May 2026 version (superseded): 2SLS long differences with two shift-share instruments, negative log distance to the nearest InterTubes long-haul fiber node times China data-center revenue growth, and the 1980 county share of urban college population times rest-of-world revenue growth; no fixed effects or controls in the baseline; robust standard errors; first-stage F 786 to 907. The September version demotes this design to a "descriptive benchmark" (Appendix C) because its exposures predict 11 of 12 early outcomes in every balance panel (44 rejections in 48 tests).

**Headline findings**

- [primary] LATEST VERSION, pooled annual shift-share 2SLS: electricity-price elasticity with respect to cumulative data-center revenue is positive and not statistically distinguishable from zero. Outcome is log county average retail price from EIA-861, 1995-2023. **Estimate 0.042, SE 0.071, conventional p 0.556, Anderson-Rubin p 0.550; first-stage F 8.79 (flagged weak); N 45,079 county-years. Implied effect 0.9% at the mean sample treatment change, 0.4% for the median positive revenue increment.** (Sept 2026 revision, Table 1, p. 13)
- [primary] LATEST VERSION, preferred 1995-2023 long-difference 2SLS (Early + terrain controls, state FE): positive, small, not statistically significant. **Estimate 0.048, SE 0.063, Anderson-Rubin p 0.606; first-stage F 9.39 (flagged weak); N 1,498 counties. Implied effect 1.0% at mean treatment change, 0.5% at the median positive increment. Authors translate this as a 10% increase in cumulative data-center revenue implying 0.5% higher electricity prices.** (Sept 2026 revision, Table 2, p. 16; footnote 15, p. 19; Figure 7 panel header (F=9.4, N=1,498), p. 18; text pp. 3-4 and p. 23)
- [primary] LATEST VERSION, electricity-price coefficients by endpoint (Early + terrain controls): slightly negative early, positive later, never significant at any endpoint. **2000: -0.010 (0.040), AR p 0.783, F 2.13. 2005: -0.002 (0.074), AR p 0.709, F 2.37. 2010: 0.021 (0.100), AR p 0.921, F 4.57. 2015: 0.018 (0.075), AR p 0.786, F 7.76. 2020: 0.056 (0.062), AR p 0.185, F 9.10.** (Sept 2026 revision, Appendix Table E.2, p. 18 of Online Appendix)
- [primary] LATEST VERSION, same by-endpoint profile with Early controls only. **2000: -0.009 (0.045), AR p 0.861, F 2.19. 2005: -0.010 (0.085), AR p 0.754, F 2.47. 2010: 0.022 (0.109), AR p 0.977, F 4.91. 2015: 0.024 (0.080), AR p 0.880, F 9.92. 2020: 0.061 (0.069), p 0.382, F 11.01.** (Sept 2026 revision, Appendix Table E.1, p. 17 of Online Appendix)
- [primary] LATEST VERSION, OLS counterparts for electricity price are essentially zero and slightly NEGATIVE. **Pooled OLS: -0.00205 (0.00423), p 0.631, implied 0.0%, N 45,079. Long-difference OLS 1995-2023: -0.00309 (0.00424), p 0.471, implied -0.1%, N 1,498.** (Sept 2026 revision, Appendix Tables D.1 and D.2, p. 16 of Online Appendix)
- [primary] LATEST VERSION, the May paper's own fiber-node / 1980 urban-college instruments re-run in the pooled panel with county and state-by-year FE give an electricity effect of about zero, with a negative sign on the preferred sample. **Panel A (full support): 0.003 (0.017), F 29.69, conventional p 0.881, AR p 0.712. Panel B (preferred-design common support): -0.014 (0.012), F 27.26, conventional p 0.241, AR p 0.467.** (Sept 2026 revision, Appendix Table C.1, p. 9 of Online Appendix)
- [primary] LATEST VERSION, robustness for electricity: point estimates stay positive and insignificant after adding 1995 digital-economy controls or dropping counties with unmatched PeeringDB facilities; first stages weaken further. **Digital controls: pooled 0.113 (F 3.13, AR p .520); long difference 0.078 (F 2.55, AR p .555). PeeringDB exclusion: pooled 0.058 (F 7.07, AR p .592); long difference 0.059 (F 9.35, AR p .444).** (Sept 2026 revision, Appendix Tables H.2 and H.3, pp. 26 and 28 of Online Appendix)
- [primary] LATEST VERSION, authors' own wording on electricity: pooled and long-difference electricity-price coefficients are described as "positive but imprecisely estimated" and as emerging later; the abstract and conclusion nonetheless state that data centers put pressure on electricity prices. **No additional number; characterization only.** (Sept 2026 revision, abstract; p. 3; p. 12 (pooled results); p. 19 (House and electricity prices); p. 23 (conclusion))
- [primary] LATEST VERSION, balance check on the electricity outcome: the terrain-route instrument individually predicts the 1990 logged electricity price level in one of four panels, although no joint test rejects at 5 percent. **1860 cohort with terrain controls: terrain b 0.02933, SE 0.01329, p 0.034; joint F 2.78, joint p 0.076. Preferred 1870 cohort with terrain controls: terrain b 0.01806, SE 0.01197, p 0.140; joint p 0.281.** (Sept 2026 revision, Appendix Tables F.2 and F.4, pp. 19-20 of Online Appendix)
- [primary] MAY 2026 VERSION (superseded; likely the version later papers cite): electricity price effect positive and significant at 1 percent at every horizon. Outcome is the long difference from 1995 in the county all-sector (total) price from EIA-861. **2005: 0.007 (0.002). 2010: 0.008 (0.002). 2015: 0.008 (0.001). 2020: 0.009 (0.001). All ***. Electricity N: 2,986, 2,997, 2,975, 2,954. First-stage F 786 to 907.** (BFI WP 2026-67 (May 2026), Table 4, p. 13; text pp. 12-13)
- [primary] MAY 2026 VERSION: the electricity result disappears once state fixed effects are added and errors are clustered by state, in the authors' own robustness table. **1995-2020 electricity price: baseline 0.009 (0.001)***; with state fixed effects 0.001 (0.002), not significant. N 2,954. First-stage statistic falls from 906.95 to 46.25.** (BFI WP 2026-67 (May 2026), Table 7 and Section 9 text, p. 16)
- [primary] MAY 2026 VERSION: alternative treatment definitions for electricity, 1995-2020. **Log DC revenue 0.009 (0.001)***; log DC facilities 0.015 (0.002)***; ever-DC indicator 0.039 (0.005)***. N 2,954.** (BFI WP 2026-67 (May 2026), Table 6, p. 15)
- [primary] MAY 2026 VERSION: summary statistic for the electricity outcome, which suggests the outcome is closer to a level change in dollars per kWh than a percent change (my inference, see critique). **"% change electricity price" 1995-2020: mean 0.038, SD 0.022, N 2,954; table notes say percent-change rows are IHS differences.** (BFI WP 2026-67 (May 2026), Table 1, p. 11)
- [primary] Data-center data source and treatment sparsity. **DCKB: more than 12,960 facilities, over 2,700 providers, 131 countries (May, p. 4). Share of counties with any data-center presence by 2020: 7.6% (May, Table 1-2). 316 U.S. counties with a first mapped build by 2023; median first-entry year 2010 (Sept, Figure H.1).** (May 2026 p. 4 and Tables 1-2, pp. 10-11; Sept 2026 Online Appendix p. 23)

**Heterogeneity reported:** Very little for electricity. (1) Timing only: coefficients are near zero or slightly negative for 2000-2005 endpoints and positive for later endpoints (largest at 2020: 0.056 to 0.061; 2023: 0.048), none significant (Tables E.1, E.2; Figures 6-7). (2) Facility type, indirectly: the preferred instrument is weighted to scale-oriented (wholesale, powered-shell, hyperscale) growth, while the alternative fiber/college design, described as closer to traditional enterprise facilities, gives about zero for electricity (0.003 and -0.014, Table C.1). There is no split by customer class (residential vs commercial vs industrial), by utility ownership type, by regulated vs restructured state, by ISO/RTO, by region, or by facility size in MW. Nothing in the paper speaks to residential rates or bills specifically.

**Limitations the authors state:** From the September 2026 revision: (a) electricity-price and public-supply water specifications are weakly identified and rely on Anderson-Rubin inference (p. 12; Table 1 and 2 notes); (b) electricity coefficients are imprecise under weak-IV-robust inference (p. 19, footnote 15); (c) post-period intervals are wide, especially before the early 2010s, because the first stage is weak at short horizons (p. 16; Table B.1); (d) the analysis is limited to county-level effects, which the authors call a serious limitation because effects probably differ at short distances (p. 23); (e) reduced-form estimates capture the combined local-equilibrium response and do not separate direct demand from fiscal-infrastructure channels (p. 22); (f) the authors suspect their employment and activity effects are too high relative to most geography models (p. 22); (g) no welfare evaluation (p. 23); (h) treatment uses currently reported facility attributes assigned to build cohorts, not realized historical flows (Figure 2 notes); (i) robustness exercises with added controls or exclusions weaken the first stage, so they inform signs rather than magnitudes (Appendix H); (j) the 50 km hub-exclusion test is uninterpretable because relevance collapses (Table H.4). The May version states no limitations section; its only caveats are the SIC-to-NAICS break and the loss of significance with state fixed effects.

**What a skeptical referee would raise:** 1. The headline electricity claim is not supported at conventional levels in the current version. Elasticity 0.042 to 0.048 with standard errors of 0.063 to 0.071 and Anderson-Rubin p-values of 0.55 to 0.61. A rough conventional 90 percent interval on the long-difference estimate is about -0.06 to +0.15 (my arithmetic from the reported SE, not the paper's). The abstract and conclusion still describe pressure on electricity prices and quote a 0.5 percent effect per 10 percent revenue increase without the uncertainty attached. 2. Wrong price for a residential-bill question. Per the May text, the outcome is all-sector average revenue per MWh. Adding a very large customer on a low industrial or large-power tariff mechanically lowers a county's all-sector average even if every existing customer's rate is unchanged or rising. The sign of this composition effect is negative, so the series cannot be used to infer residential rate effects in either direction. The September revision does not restate the sector, so even this is not fully pinned down. 3. The county price is not really a county price. EIA-861 reports revenue and sales at the utility (utility-state) level. Aggregating utilities serving a county assigns each utility's system-wide totals to every county it touches. The allocation rule for multi-county utilities is not described. Most identifying variation after state fixed effects is therefore differences in which utilities serve a county, not county-specific price changes. Regulated rates are set at utility and state level, which is why state fixed effects erased the May result (0.009 to 0.001). 4. Version instability. The May version reported highly significant effects with first-stage F near 900 and no fixed effects or controls; within four months the authors replaced the instrument, and their own appendix shows the old exposures fail 44 of 48 balance tests and deliver about zero (0.003; -0.014 on the preferred sample) for electricity. Anyone citing "Alvarez et al. find higher electricity prices" is citing a result the authors have since downgraded. 5. Exclusion restriction for electricity specifically. Rail corridors and gentle-terrain routes also host transmission lines, pipelines and heavy industry, and determine historical utility build-out. The instrument could move electricity costs through channels other than data centers. Consistent with this worry, the terrain instrument individually predicts 1990 log electricity price in one balance panel (b 0.029, p 0.034, Table F.2), though joint tests do not reject at 5 percent. For the preferred design, pre-1995 electricity placebos are shown only as figure points (1990-1994), not tabulated. 6. Weak instruments and implausible magnitudes elsewhere. Partial R-squared about 0.01; first-stage F below 10 for electricity. IV employment effects are roughly ten times OLS, and the authors themselves suspect they are too high. That weakens confidence in the whole system of estimates, including the price ones. OLS for electricity is slightly negative and tightly estimated around zero (-0.002 to -0.003, SE 0.004). 7. Treatment measurement. Cumulative "currently reported annualized revenue" back-cast to build year is a noisy proxy for load. It is unclear how DCKB assigns annualized revenue to self-built hyperscale campuses that sell nothing externally (I am uncertain about this; the paper does not say). MW capacity exists in DCKB but is not used as the treatment. The IHS of revenue in millions makes the elasticity sensitive to units for small values. 8. Units in the May version. Table 1 shows a mean 1995-2020 "% change" in electricity price of 0.038, which is far too small for a 25-year nominal log change and looks like an IHS difference of a price in dollars per kWh, that is, roughly a 3.8 cent level change. If so, the May coefficient of 0.009 is about 0.9 cents per kWh per log point of cumulative revenue, not 0.9 percent. This is my inference from the summary table, not something the paper states. 9. Sample and external validity. The preferred sample keeps only counties with 1870 population and stable 1870 boundaries (about 1,550 of roughly 3,100), which probably drops much of the West and Plains, including some major hyperscale markets (I did not see a list of excluded counties). The local average treatment effect applies to counties whose data-center growth is explained by nineteenth-century rail geometry. 10. Timing. Data end in 2023, before most AI-era load growth, the 2024-2026 capacity-price spikes and recent rate cases. Even at the point estimate, the implied effect at the mean treatment change is about 1 percent over 28 years. 11. No heterogeneity by customer class, market structure or region, and no multiple-testing adjustment across ten outcomes and many endpoints in the main tables. 12. Not peer reviewed; proprietary treatment data; no replication package.

**What it would take to reproduce:** No code or data are posted (the NBER page lists no data appendix; the paper gives no repository). The treatment data (S&P Global / 451 Research DCKB) are proprietary and cannot be redistributed, so the headline numbers cannot be reproduced exactly from public data. What an approximate public reproduction would need: (1) Outcome: EIA-861 annual detailed files (sales, revenue, customers by sector by utility-state) plus the EIA-861 Service Territory file to map utilities to counties; compute county price as summed revenue divided by summed sales over utilities serving the county, in logs. I am uncertain how the authors handled 1990-2000, since my understanding (not checked here) is that the public service-territory county list starts around 2001; the paper does not say. The allocation rule for multi-county utilities must be assumed. Building the residential-only analogue from the same files is straightforward and would be the more relevant series. (2) Treatment substitute: a public facility list with coordinates and opening years (no revenue), so the treatment would have to be facility counts or MW rather than cumulative annualized revenue; the May version's Table 6 shows facility-count results for comparison (0.015 for electricity, pre-state-FE). (3) Instruments, all public in principle: 1870 railroad network from Atack et al. / Donaldson and Hornbeck, 1890 county polygons (NHGIS), the 1855 Pacific Railroad survey map from the Library of Congress (six terminal pairs listed in Appendix A.2), NOAA ETOPO 2022 elevation, an A-star least-cost routine with edge cost d(1+400 g^2), 5-km grid coverage shares at 10 and 50 km, endpoint/junction and boundary-crossing counts (eqs. 7-15). The foreign shift (1,457 scale-oriented facilities in 54 countries) comes from DCKB and is not public; values are plotted in Figure A.1 (6.153 in 2013, 7.977 in 2023). (4) Controls: 1986 CBP, 1989 IRS SOI, 1870 population, land area, water proximity, ruggedness, temperature, elevation and slope. Simplest partial check available from public data: the OLS and state-fixed-effect patterns (electricity effect about zero) using EIA-861 and any public facility list. The May-version alternative instruments use InterTubes node locations (Durairajan et al. 2015), the NBER county distance database and 1980 Census urban-college shares, all public, but the shifts again come from DCKB.

**Corrections to what we had heard:** 1. The brief cites the paper as May 2026. The current NBER version is "May 2026, Revised September 2026" and is a different paper in substance: the identification changed from fiber-node distance and 1980 urban-college share (with China and rest-of-world revenue shifts) to nineteenth-century rail and terrain-route geometry (with a leave-U.S.-out scale-oriented facility shift); the sample now runs to 2023 instead of 2020; public-supply water withdrawals were added; data-processing employment was dropped as an outcome. 2. On the Bahar and Wright reading ("finds higher electricity prices"): that is accurate for the May 2026 version only (0.007 to 0.009, significant at 1 percent, Table 4), and even there the effect vanished with state fixed effects (0.001, SE 0.002, Table 7). In the September 2026 revision the electricity result is a positive point estimate that is not statistically distinguishable from zero: elasticity 0.042 (pooled, AR p 0.550) and 0.048 (1995-2023 long difference, AR p 0.606); OLS is slightly negative. The accurate summary of the current paper is "small positive, statistically insignificant, weakly identified," although the authors' abstract and conclusion still use language about pressure on electricity prices. I did not open Bahar and Wright, so which version they cite is unverified. 3. The price series is a county-level all-sector average retail price built from EIA-861 revenue divided by sales (explicit in the May version; sector unstated in September). It is not a residential price and not a bill measure. The paper contains no residential-specific estimate. 4. The paper does study water in the current version (public-supply withdrawals, quantity not price): pooled 0.247 (AR p 0.009) and long difference to 2020 0.311 (p 0.065), Tables 1-2. It has no water price or water rate outcome. 5. Title punctuation is "Shift–Share" with an en dash in the NBER version.

## (b) PRIMARY, read in full: Dany Bahar (Brown University) and Greg C. Wright (UC Merced, corresponding author), "The Local Economic Impact of Data Centers," author-posted working paper, no series or number, dated August 7, 2026 (PDF metadata creation date also Aug 7, 2026), 35 PDF pages. Formatted like a journal submission (CRediT statement, competing-interest declaration); not peer reviewed as far as the document shows. (a) Dany Bahar and Greg Wright, "New evidence on data center employment effects," Brookings Institution article, originally published May 4, 2026, updated August 10, 2026. The Brookings piece says it summarizes a DIFFERENT paper by the same authors, "Data Centers and Local Labor Markets," which it says is "available from the authors" (no link).

- **Link:** https://gregcwright-econ.github.io/files/DataCenters_LocalImpact_2026.pdf (working paper); https://www.brookings.edu/articles/new-evidence-on-data-center-employment-effects/ (Brookings brief)
- **How far we got:** read in full. Working paper: the PDF is openly posted on Wright's GitHub Pages site but is NOT linked from his homepage (the homepage lists the paper as "work in progress" with no PDF). I found the direct file URL through a web search index, fetched it with the standard fetch tool (HTTP 200, no access controls), and read the text of all 35 pages (main text, tables, Appendices A-D, data availability statement; figures read via captions and alt text only, not the plotted points). Brookings article: fetched from brookings.edu with the standard fetch tool, which returns the page through an automated text extractor rather than raw HTML; I ran three separate extraction passes including a literal string-presence check, and they agreed with each other. Treat Brookings numbers as primary but machine-extracted. NOT obtained: (1) the earlier draft "Data Centers and Local Labor Markets" dated May 5, 2026 (gregcwright-econ.github.io/files/DataCenters_April2026.pdf) and its addendum (…/files/DataCenters_addendum_May2026.pdf) both now return 404; (2) the May 2026 original of the Brookings page exists in the Wayback Machine (snapshot 20260511171439) but web.archive.org is not reachable from this tool, so a person should open http://web.archive.org/web/20260511171439/https://www.brookings.edu/articles/new-evidence-on-data-center-employment-effects/ by hand to see the pre-revision text. A direct browser read of the Brookings page was blocked by the session's permission system and I did not work around it.
- **Funding or affiliation:** Working paper: states it received no specific grant from public, commercial or not-for-profit funders and declares no competing interests (printed p.22). Declares use of OpenAI ChatGPT and Codex, Anthropic Claude, and Google Gemini for code debugging, robustness checks and editorial revision. Bahar is listed at Brown University; Wright at UC Merced. Brookings page: lists Bahar with the Center for Global Development and Wright as a Brookings nonresident senior fellow; discloses that Amazon, Google, Meta and Microsoft are general, unrestricted donors to Brookings; and says the authors used Claude Code for data analysis, code development and manuscript preparation. Several outcome datasets are proprietary (Dewey-distributed WageScape, LinkUp, RentHub, SafeGraph, Advan; Veridion), accessed under institutional license.
- **Unit:** Electricity analysis (Appendix C): utility-by-state ("each utility within each state as a separate unit"), annual. Not a facility-distance or county design; "near facilities" in the brief overstates the spatial resolution. Rest of the paper: facility-by-distance-ring-by-year (rings 0-1, 1-2, 2-5, 5-10, 10-25, 25-50 km; labor outcomes 0-25 vs 50-150 km), plus county-by-industry-by-year (QCEW) and school-district/county fiscal panels. Brookings brief: county ("labor market") level.
- **Years:** Electricity: EIA Form 861, 2012-2024; treated cohorts first treated 2015-2021, followed to 2024; effect averaged over event years 0-5. Nighttime lights: VIIRS April 2012 - December 2025. Rents, spending, foot traffic: monthly 2020-2023. School and county finance: 2012-2023. Brookings brief: BLS county data 2003-2024.
- **Outcome:** Log average residential electricity price, computed by the authors as residential revenue divided by megawatt-hours sold, from U.S. EIA Form 861, 2012-2024, restricted to bundled-service utilities (per the Figure C.1 note). This is average revenue per unit, not a tariff rate or a typical bill. The paper does not say which EIA-861 file maps utilities to counties (presumably the Service Territory file; my inference, not stated). No water outcome anywhere in the paper; water appears only as a passing background mention in the Brookings piece.
- **Data center data:** Starting point is the public IM3 Open Source Data Center Atlas (Pacific Northwest National Laboratory), which the paper says identifies 1,242 facilities from OpenStreetMap footprints tagged as data centers, with coordinates and, where available, square footage and operator. The authors add operating dates and missing facilities from operator press releases, state economic-development announcements, the Good Jobs First Subsidy Tracker, trade publications, targeted searches of state records, specialty wholesale and AI operators, and REIT filings; the Data Availability Statement additionally names DatacenterMap and Cloudscene (commercial directories) as inputs. Result: 1,494-record master registry, of which 341 are classified hyperscale by operator identity. DATING (Section 2.1): announcement, groundbreaking, construction, planned-completion and operating dates are kept as separate events. Operating dates from operators or public agencies are "high" confidence; less detailed sources are "medium" when the year agrees with an independent registry or satellite evidence; announcement-based projections, planned-completion dates and unresolved conflicts are "low" and excluded (315 high/medium, 19 low, 7 outside 2012-2024). For 87 hyperscale sites, construction start is recovered from Landsat and Sentinel-2 imagery (vegetation-index decline and/or built-up-index rise) and cross-checked against FAA obstruction-evaluation filings (64 matched within 500 m, 119 within 1 km). Near misses (84) come from trade publications, regulatory filings and operator announcements and are dated by announcement. The Brookings brief describes the dating differently: opening dates derived from OpenStreetMap building edit histories, state incentive registries and press reports. PUBLIC OR NOT: the IM3 atlas is public. The authors' compiled registry, dates and near-miss list are NOT posted as of 2026-11-11 as far as I could find. The Data Availability Statement says they "will be deposited" in a public repository (future tense, no URL or DOI). The electricity analysis uses the facility's operating/opening year, and the paper does not list which 52 utility-states are treated.
- **Treatment:** Electricity (Appendix C, printed p.30 / PDF p.31): a utility-state is treated in the year the first HYPERSCALE facility in the authors' registry opens in a county that utility serves. Binary, first-event only; no scaling by MW, by facility load relative to utility load, or by whether that utility actually serves the facility. Hyperscale sample = 52 treated utility-states, none of which also hosts a colocation facility. A separate colocation sample has 60 treated utility-states. "Hyperscale" is defined by operator identity (Amazon, Microsoft, Google, Meta, Apple, Oracle, plus a hand-curated set of large specialty and AI campuses); the authors explicitly impose no megawatt threshold because capacity is not consistently observed (Section 2, printed p.3).
- **Method:** Electricity: cohort-by-event-time event study in the Callaway-Sant'Anna style. Two comparison groups: (i) roughly 2,000 (figure note: 2,040) utility-states that host no registry facility, with state-by-year means removed; (ii) 54 utilities serving counties with an announced hyperscale project that was never built ("near miss"), with year means removed. Reference period is the year before opening; inference from 1,999 state-cluster bootstrap draws; a balanced-panel variant keeps utility-states observed in at least 12 of 13 years. The text is internally loose: it introduces the estimator as using "not-yet-treated controls" and then reports results "against never-treated utilities" in the next sentence. Identification rests on parallel trends between host and non-host utilities within a state (joint pre-trend p = 0.97). The authors acknowledge data centers select utilities with cheap power and spare capacity. Main paper: within-site ring comparisons and built-versus-near-miss comparisons, with construction dated from satellite-detected land clearing; the authors searched for an instrument (substation density, fiber, 1980 college share, climate, generation timing, a shift-share) and report that none has a credible exclusion restriction (Appendix B).

**Headline findings**

- [primary] Residential electricity price, hyperscale host utility-states vs never-treated utility-states, mean effect over event years 0-5. Joint pre-trend p = 0.97. **-1.0% (p = 0.42)** (Working paper Appendix C, printed p.31 (PDF p.32))
- [primary] Same comparison on the balanced panel (utility-states observed in at least 12 of 13 years) **-1.5% (p = 0.17)** (Appendix C, printed p.31 (PDF p.32))
- [primary] Hyperscale host utilities vs near-miss utilities (announced, never built), full sample mean effect; and year-five estimate **-0.9% (p = 0.37); year five -1.9% (p = 0.27)** (Appendix C, printed p.31 (PDF p.32))
- [primary] Colocation host utility-states, mean effect on residential price **+0.7% (p = 0.54)** (Appendix C, printed p.31 (PDF p.32))
- [primary] Electricity sample sizes: treated hyperscale utility-states (first treated 2015-2021); never-treated controls; near-miss control utilities; colocation treated utility-states **52; 2,040 (text says roughly 2,000); 54; 60** (Appendix C text printed pp.30-31 and Figure C.1 note (PDF pp.31-32))
- [primary] Authors' summary wording of the electricity result: residential electricity prices do not rise (introduction); rates do not rise for the cloud-era cohorts in the sample (interpretation); the estimates give no evidence that residential rates rise in the host utility over this horizon (appendix). No numeric confidence interval or minimum detectable effect is reported for electricity; Figure C.1 shows 95% intervals on an axis running from -4 to +4 log points. **qualitative; no CI reported in text** (Section 1 printed p.2 (PDF p.3); Section 7 printed p.21 (PDF p.22); Appendix C printed p.31 (PDF p.32))
- [primary] Facility registry size: IM3 atlas starting point; master registry after augmentation; hyperscale records; hyperscale with analysis date in 2012-2024; with high- or medium-confidence date; with satellite-detected construction date **1,242; 1,494; 341; 334; 315; 87** (Section 2 and Table 1, printed p.3 (PDF p.4); Section 2.1 printed p.5 (PDF p.6))
- [primary] Announced hyperscale-size projects never built (near misses): total; with coordinates and satellite coverage; isolated and used in capital sample. Isolated construction-dated built sites in the capital design. **84; 52; 49. Built: 43** (Table 1 printed p.3 (PDF p.4); Section 3.1 printed p.6 (PDF p.7))
- [primary] Reliably dated data centers of all types in the continental registry (used only for the descriptive geography appendix) **1,087 (814 metropolitan, 273 exurban)** (Appendix A and Table A.2, printed pp.25, 28 (PDF pp.26, 29))
- [primary] Nighttime lights within 1 km in the construction year: within-site ring design; built vs near miss **+38% (campus-bootstrap 95% interval 15 to 71); +70%, reaching +231% one year later** (Sections 4.1-4.2 printed p.11; Table 4 printed p.14 (PDF pp.12, 15))
- [primary] Advertised salaries (0-25 km vs 50-150 km); job postings; new-firm registrations (VA and TX) **+1.3% [-2.7, +5.5]; +5.2% [-5.3, +16.8]; -0.5% [-2.6, +1.6]** (Table D.1 printed p.32 (PDF p.33); Section 5 printed pp.15-16)
- [primary] County QCEW after hyperscale opening, event years 0-5: data-processing (NAICS 518) employment and establishments; telecommunications employment **+25.9% (bootstrap p about 0.10) and +27.2%; telecom -1.4%** (Table 5 printed p.18 (PDF p.19))
- [primary] Residential asking rents: synthetic-control range; built vs near miss; within-site (treated as upper bound) **+2 to +5%; +4.3% (p = 0.23); +6.6% to +9.4%** (Section 6 printed pp.18-19; Table 3 printed p.10 (PDF pp.19-20, 11))
- [primary] School-district local property-tax revenue after hyperscale opening; does not survive multiple-testing adjustment **+16.6% (p = 0.07, FDR q = 0.21)** (Table 6 printed p.20 (PDF p.21))
- [primary] Brookings brief (Aug 10, 2026 update), facility dataset and controls **approximately 1,500 U.S. facilities plus 52 announced-but-canceled projects; BLS county data 2003-2024** (Brookings article, section 'What the evidence says about jobs' and footnote 1 (machine-extracted))
- [primary] Brookings brief headline results: data-processing employment over the first decade; telecommunications employment; wages; home prices; jobs at a typical treated county; incentives as share of investment in hyperscale vs colocation counties **+56%; +43%; unchanged; +2% to +5%; roughly 100-200 jobs; 2% vs 62%** (Brookings article, sections 'What the evidence says about jobs', 'Not all data centers are the same', 'Takeaways' (machine-extracted))
- [primary] Brookings brief contains NO electricity-price finding from the authors' own research. Electricity appears only as cited background: PJM power supply costs rising from $2.2 billion to $14.7 billion in one year, and national residential rates up about 32% from July 2020 to July 2025. **no own estimate; background figures $2.2B to $14.7B and about 32%** (Brookings article, section 'The trade-offs' (machine-extracted))
- [secondhand] Earlier draft 'Data Centers and Local Labor Markets' dated May 5, 2026 reportedly used approximately 770 facilities; an even earlier excerpt reportedly cited 93 counties; an intermediate Brookings version reportedly had wages up 3%-4% and no significant home-price effect. I could not open any of these versions. **about 770 facilities (not verified)** (Search-engine index snippets of the now-404 PDF and of trade press (siteselection.com, san.com); not seen in any document I read)

**Heterogeneity reported:** Electricity: only hyperscale vs colocation (hyperscale point estimates slightly negative, colocation +0.7%, all insignificant), full vs balanced panel, and never-treated vs near-miss controls. NOT reported for electricity: investor-owned vs public or cooperative utilities, RTO or restructured vs vertically integrated states, facility size or load share, utility size, cohort, or region. Elsewhere in the paper: nighttime-light effects by construction cohort (2018 or earlier +97% vs 2019 or later +33% in the near-miss design, Table 4), by single-building vs multi-building campus, and hyperscale vs colocation (colocation shows no lights response).

**Limitations the authors state:** Electricity-specific (Appendix C, printed p.31): the full-sample and near-miss averages are imprecise; the authors do not observe each utility's cost allocation or contract, so they cannot test the fixed-cost-spreading explanation they float for the negative point estimates; retail rates are only one incidence margin (environmental costs may fall outside the host utility); and the sample covers cloud-era cohorts through 2021 followed to 2024, not the larger post-2022 AI build-out. Section 7 (printed p.21) adds that they do not observe land prices, environmental damages, or the terms of utility and tax agreements, so cannot say whether local welfare rises. General: near-miss sites are explicitly not balanced runner-ups (event year 2024.4 vs 2018.8, 18% vs 100% with a named hyperscale sponsor, Table 2); near-miss coverage thins to 5 or fewer sites after event year 1; QCEW suppression affects 27% to 68% of cells and results are sensitive to imputation; five of ten county series have pre-trends; post-2022 AI campuses are larger and more power-intensive than anything in the sample.

**What a skeptical referee would raise:** Scope and status. The electricity result is a side appendix in an unreviewed draft: one page, one figure, four point estimates with p-values, no coefficient table, no numeric confidence intervals, no minimum detectable effect (the paper reports MDEs for its labor outcomes but not for electricity). "Do not rise" in the introduction is stronger than the appendix supports; the appendix itself calls the averages imprecise. I am uncertain how large an increase the design can rule out; the figure axis (plus or minus 4 log points) suggests small single digits, but that is my reading of an axis, not a reported bound. Treatment is coarse. Binary first-hyperscale-opening in any county the utility serves, with no measure of load. A 30 MW first building in a large investor-owned utility's territory is coded the same as a campus that doubles a small public utility's sales. The utility-to-county link means a utility can be coded treated when a different utility (or a different county in its footprint) actually serves the facility, which biases toward zero. The paper does not say whether multiple utilities sharing a county are all treated, or whether estimates are weighted by customers or sales. Unweighted, roughly 2,000 controls are dominated by small municipals and co-ops. Design absorbs the main channel critics worry about. Never-treated comparisons remove state-by-year means, so any cost that is socialized across a state, a holding company, or an RTO zone (capacity prices, transmission build-out, fuel) is differenced out. The design can only detect a host-utility-specific premium relative to neighbors, not a regional increase shared with the controls. A SUTVA violation of this kind would make a true regional effect look like zero. Sample excludes the cases that drive the public debate. Cohorts are 2015-2021 with data ending 2024: before the 2025/26 PJM capacity-price spike reached bills and before most AI-scale load. Utilities first treated before 2015 (the oldest hyperscale hosts) are not in the 52. Restricting to bundled-service utilities drops or mismeasures customers in retail-choice states, which overlap heavily with PJM and ERCOT. Excluding utility-states that also host colocation removes the dense markets (the paper notes 236 data centers around Ashburn). Rate cases lag load by years, so a 0-5 year window may miss pass-through. Outcome. EIA-861 revenue per MWh is an average-revenue measure that moves with weather, usage per customer, fuel riders and customer mix, not only with rates. No commercial or industrial price series is shown, so cost shifting between classes cannot be assessed. No check by ownership type even though the paper cites Meeks et al. finding effects concentrated at investor-owned utilities. Selection. The authors say data centers pick utilities with cheap power and spare capacity. Flat pre-trends (p = 0.97) help, but utilities with spare capacity may be on a different forward cost path (less need for new build) regardless of the data center, which would bias toward a negative estimate. The near-miss control group is, by the paper's own Table 2, much later (mean event year 2024 vs 2019) and mostly speculative, so near-miss utilities have almost no post-period within data ending 2024; it is unclear what identifies that comparison. Registry discretion. "Hyperscale" is by operator identity plus a hand-curated list with no MW threshold; dating relies on hand-assembled sources with confidence tiers; a table footnote refers to "v9 opening dates" and the public-finance section says one estimate "changes materially when we update the opening dates," so results are sensitive to dating revisions. The text is inconsistent on whether controls are not-yet-treated or never-treated. The two sibling write-ups (Brookings Aug 10 vs this paper Aug 7) report materially different employment numbers, and the brief's numbers have been revised at least once, which argues for treating all magnitudes as provisional. Nothing is replicable yet: registry, treated-utility list and code are promised but not posted.

**What it would take to reproduce:** No code or data are posted as of 2026-11-11 that I could find (searched for a GitHub, Zenodo or openICPSR deposit; none surfaced, but absence in search is not proof). The Data Availability Statement promises a future public deposit containing the registry, operating and construction dates, the near-miss cohort, and all code. To approximate the electricity headline (-1.0%, years 0-5) from public data we would need: (1) EIA Form 861 annual files 2012-2024, residential revenue and MWh sales by utility and state, bundled-service records only, with price = revenue / MWh in logs; (2) a utility-to-county crosswalk, most plausibly the EIA-861 Service Territory file (the paper does not name it); (3) a list of first hyperscale opening year by county. The public IM3 atlas gives locations but not opening dates or a hyperscale flag, so we would have to rebuild dates ourselves from operator and state announcements for the six named operators (Amazon, Microsoft, Google, Meta, Apple, Oracle) plus an unknown hand-curated list of specialty and AI campuses; (4) keep utility-states first treated 2015-2021 that host no colocation facility (target N = 52) and never-treated utility-states with no registry facility (target N about 2,040); (5) residualize on state-by-year means, run a Callaway-Sant'Anna cohort-by-event-time estimator with reference year -1, average event years 0-5, and bootstrap by state (1,999 draws). The near-miss comparison (54 utilities) cannot be reproduced without the authors' list of 84 canceled projects. Exact match is unlikely because the treated list, the colocation exclusion and the weighting are undisclosed; landing within about a point of -1% with a similar-sized treated set would be a reasonable check. Useful extensions the paper does not do: split by ownership type, scale by facility MW over utility sales, extend past 2024, and drop the state-by-year demeaning to see regional effects.

**Corrections to what we had heard:** 1. "About 770 facilities" appears in NEITHER current primary document. The Aug 7 working paper has a 1,494-record registry (1,242 from IM3 before augmentation; 1,087 reliably dated) of which 341 are hyperscale; the Brookings page as updated Aug 10 says approximately 1,500 facilities. The 770 figure appears to come from the superseded May 5, 2026 draft of "Data Centers and Local Labor Markets," whose PDF now returns 404; I could verify it only from search snippets (secondhand). 2. The Brookings brief makes NO finding about electricity prices. It only cites background figures (PJM costs, national rates up about 32%). The "residential electricity prices do not rise" claim lives only in the Aug 7 working paper, and there the entire analysis is a one-page appendix (Appendix C) with one figure and no coefficient table. 3. The electricity result is not "near facilities." It is at the host utility-by-state level: any utility serving a county where a first hyperscale facility opens. 4. The claim is narrower than the secondhand summary: 52 hyperscale utility-states first treated 2015-2021, outcomes through 2024, and the authors themselves limit it to "cloud-era cohorts," excluding the post-2022 AI build-out. Point estimates are negative but statistically indistinguishable from zero. 5. (a) and (b) are different papers with different designs and numbers that do not obviously reconcile: Brookings reports data-processing employment +56% and telecom +43% over a decade with home prices +2% to +5%; the facility-level paper three days earlier reports data-processing +26%, telecom -1.4%, and does not estimate home prices (rents only). Brookings says 52 canceled projects; the paper says 84 near misses, of which 52 have satellite coverage. 6. The Brookings piece was first published May 4, 2026 and its editor's note says earlier employment, wage and home-price findings were revised with an expanded sample, so anything quoted from the May version is superseded. 7. The paper PDF is not linked from gregcwright-econ.github.io; it sits at /files/DataCenters_LocalImpact_2026.pdf and the homepage still labels the paper "work in progress," so it may change or move without notice.

## Vishrut Rana, Christian Kaps, Serguei Netessine. "The Cost of Intelligence: Impacts of AI Data Centers on US Electricity Prices." SSRN 7580459. The PDF title page prints no affiliations, no series name, no date and no version number; it carries only the line "[Updated frequently, click here for most recent version.]" and keywords (AI Infrastructure, Data Centers, Energy Markets, Electric Utilities, Sustainable Operations). The PDF's embedded metadata (not the printed text) shows creation date 12 Oct 2026, and it was produced by LaTeX; I note this only as file metadata. 42 PDF pages (pdftotext yields 43 form-feed segments because of a trailing empty one): 32 pages of text and references, then an appendix with its own numbering (A and B, PDF pp. 36-42). Peer review status: not stated; nothing printed says it is published, and it reads as a working paper (it says "Updated frequently").

- **Link:** https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=7580459
- **How far we got:** read in full. PDF downloaded by hand by the project owner on 2026-11-11 and read locally. I read the text extraction of all 42 PDF pages (abstract, introduction, literature review, model Sec. 3, data Sec. 4, empirical strategy Sec. 5, results Sec. 6, discussion Sec. 7, references, Appendix A and B including Tables A.1-A.4 and B.5-B.7) and viewed rendered PDF pages 22 and 23 (Tables 1-2 and Figures 5-6) to confirm the table columns. Other figures (1-4, 6 detail, A.1-A.4) were read by caption and notes only; I did not inspect any plotted values except Figures 5 and 6 at a glance. Page numbers: main text printed page = PDF page (offset 0). Appendix pages restart at 1 on PDF p. 36, so PDF page = appendix printed page + 35. In the record, appendix items are cited as 'App. p. N' with the PDF page in brackets.
- **Funding or affiliation:** not stated. The printed PDF has no acknowledgments, funding, conflict-of-interest or data-provider statement, and no author affiliations. The first line says 'Updated frequently, click here for most recent version.' Aterio is cited only as the data source; no relationship is disclosed.
- **Unit:** Utility by quarter. Treated units are 42 utilities (385 data centers of at least 10 MW added after 2020 among bundled-service utilities; Sec. 4.2, pp. 15-16; Table 1, p. 22). The residential sample has 41 treated utilities because one utility serves only industrial customers (p. 23; the utility missing from the residential list in Table B.7 is Tennessee Valley Authority, App. p. 7 [PDF 42] vs Table B.6 [PDF 41]). Observation counts: industrial 648, residential 627 (Tables 1-2, pp. 22-23). 648 observations over 42 utilities is about 15.4 per utility, and Eq. 21 says it pools 'post-anchor' gaps (p. 18) with up to 24 post-treatment quarters (p. 19), so the observations appear to be post-anchor treated-utility quarters only. The paper does not state the observation count construction in so many words. Donor pool: utilities without data center additions over the post-anchor horizon; Table A.4 (App. p. 4 [PDF 39]) counts 291 'utilities without DC added in 2020-2025' and 42 with. Geography: the number of states containing the 42 treated utilities is not stated; the full Aterio file connects to 184 utilities in 43 states (Sec. 4.1, p. 12). The 42 utilities are listed by name in Tables B.6 and B.7 (App. pp. 6-7 [PDF 41-42]). FERC cost sample: 22 treated utilities (Table 6, p. 30).
- **Years:** Treatment anchors are the first quarter from 2020Q1 onward in which a utility receives positive new data center capacity (Eq. 15, p. 17). Outcomes run through the end of 2025 (Figs. 5-6 notes, pp. 22-23), so a treated utility contributes up to 24 post-treatment quarters (p. 19). Each synthetic control uses the 16 quarters before the anchor, which can reach back before 2020 (p. 17). Last year of retail price data in the regressions: 2025 (through end of 2025; quarter not further specified). Table A.4 compares 2020 and 2025 average prices (App. p. 4 [PDF 39]). 2022-onward sensitivity uses additions from 2022 (Table 3, p. 25). The Aterio data vintage is cited as 'Aterio (2026)' with no month stated. The EIA data vintage is not stated. Data centers that entered before 2000 are all recorded at a common 2000 boundary (p. 19, fn. 2 p. 12).
- **Outcome:** Prices: EIA Form 861M (monthly: revenue, sales, customer counts by utility and customer category). The authors aggregate to utility-quarter and define price = revenue divided by MWh sold, in $/MWh, separately for industrial and residential (Sec. 4.2, pp. 14-15). The paper says this is realized average revenue per MWh, not a posted rate schedule (p. 15). Nominal versus real: the main regressions' dollars are not stated as nominal or real. The text says nominal prices rose since 2020 while inflation-adjusted prices are flat or fall (p. 3, p. 15), and Figure 4 and Table A.4 (2020 vs 2025 averages of 123.59/133.17 residential and 67.84/101.31 industrial in $/MWh; App. p. 4 [PDF 39]) appear to be nominal, with the inflation-adjusted series shown separately in Fig. A.4 (2010 dollars). I infer, not stated, that the regressions use nominal $/MWh. Levels, not logs. Retail choice: the paper restricts to bundled service territories, stating that 'EIA 861M reports bundled utility (sales of both electricity and delivery services)' and that this 'exclude[s] utilities where alternative electricity suppliers can be externally contracted with and the utility is responsible for only delivery services' (p. 15). How a utility is classified as bundled versus delivery-only is not explained. Other outcomes: total utility cost per MWh from FERC Forms 1 and 3-Q (operating and capital-cost measures divided by sales; p. 16), only for utilities selling over about 1 million MWh a year (22 treated utilities, Table 6 p. 30); and total quarterly electricity sales in MWh in the service region from EIA (Table 7, p. 31). No wholesale price outcome, no bill outcome, no water outcome.
- **Data center data:** Aterio, 'US Data Center Locations and Power Demand' (Aterio 2026; URL in the references, p. 33). Commercial vendor data; the paper does not say whether it is purchased or free, and does not describe how Aterio compiles it. Fields used: location, activation date, operating status, power capacity (MW), mapped electric utility, and owner (Sec. 4.1, pp. 12, 14). The file contains 1,758 active US data centers with 45.2 GW cumulative capacity connected to 184 utilities in 43 states; median 15 MW, mean 25.74 MW, 90th percentile 60 MW (p. 12; Table A.3 App. p. 3 [PDF 38]). Among these, 1,061 are at least 10 MW; 561 are at least 10 MW and post-2020 (mean 48.05 MW, median 45 MW, max 430 MW; Table A.3). Whether capacity is nameplate, estimated or contracted is not stated beyond 'estimated power capacity'. Cryptocurrency mines: not stated either way. The owner table lists Hut8 and Crusoe among the top 20 owners (Table A.2, App. p. 2 [PDF 37]) with no comment; I do not know from the paper whether those entries are crypto sites. Validation of Aterio's dates, capacities or utility mapping: none reported. Virginia Electric & Power Co alone has 7,444.61 MW and 213 facilities in Table A.1 (App. p. 1 [PDF 36]) versus '6.7 GW' in the text (p. 13); the two numbers differ and the paper does not reconcile them (the text may be using a different cut, not stated).
- **Treatment:** Continuous, cumulative and repeated, not binary. For each utility i, A_it is MW of data center capacity that begins operating in quarter t. Two intensity measures (Sec. 5, p. 17): (a) MW, and (b) 'Ratio' = 100 x A_it / Peak_i, where Peak_i is the utility's peak annual power demand in MW 'just before the treatment anchor' (Eq. 16). The treatment variable in the regressions is cumulative post-anchor exposure D_it = sum of A_is from the anchor T_i through t (Eq. 17), i.e. the stock of capacity added since the utility's first post-2020 addition. Only data centers of at least 10 MW activated after 2020 count (p. 13); this leaves 561 eligible, and restricting to bundled service territories leaves 385 across 42 utilities (pp. 15-16). Capacity is Aterio's estimated power capacity (MW); multiple facilities activating in one utility-quarter are summed (pp. 13-14). Pre-2020 capacity is not removed or counted as treatment; it stays in the baseline (p. 19, 'Contextual Interpretation'). Linking facilities to utilities: Aterio's data 'identifies... their supplier electric utilities' and each facility has a 'mapped electric utility' (Sec. 4.1, pp. 12-13). How Aterio does the mapping (service territory polygons, utility filings, or something else) is not stated. Operating versus announced: the 1,758 'active' facilities are used (p. 12); announced or under-construction facilities are not mentioned. Start date: Aterio 'activation date' converted to a utility-quarter (p. 13); precision is not discussed except pre-2000 truncation. 'Cloud service' means Google, Amazon, Meta, Microsoft; every other owner is 'third-party' (pp. 14, 25); Apple, xAI, Crusoe and Hut8 appear in Table A.2 (App. p. 2 [PDF 37]) and so fall into the third-party group by that rule.
- **Method:** Single-anchor synthetic control with cumulative, repeated treatment (Sec. 5, pp. 16-18; cites Abadie et al. 2010, 2015, Abadie 2021), then a pooled regression following De Chaisemartin and d'Haultfoeuille (2026) for non-binary, non-absorbing treatment. Steps: (1) for each treated utility, find the anchor quarter (Eq. 15). (2) Build a synthetic utility from the 16 pre-anchor quarters of the outcome, with non-negative weights summing to one chosen to minimize distance between the treated utility's vector X_i (pre-treatment outcome path 'and any utility characteristics included in the matching procedure') and the weighted donors (Eq. 18, p. 18). Which characteristics, if any, enter X_i is not stated. Weights are fixed after the pre-period (p. 18). (3) Gap R_it = Y_it minus synthetic outcome (Eq. 20). (4) Pool post-anchor gaps across treated utilities and regress R_it = beta D_it + mu_i + lambda_q(t) + e_it (Eq. 21), with treated-utility fixed effects and quarter-of-year (seasonal) fixed effects, not calendar-quarter fixed effects. Columns (1) and (3) of the results tables omit fixed effects (Eq. 22); columns (2) and (4) include them and are called preferred (pp. 18-19). The preferred specification is the Ratio treatment with fixed effects (column 4). Comparison group: donor utilities 'with complete outcome data that receive no positive addition of the selected data center treatment over utility i's post-anchor comparison horizon' (pp. 17-18). Whether donors may have had pre-2020 data centers, and whether donors are restricted to bundled-service utilities, is not stated. Clustering: utility-clustered standard errors (table notes, e.g. p. 22). Standard errors from the synthetic control step itself are not mentioned (the gap is treated as data in the second-stage regression). No event-window regression; Figures 5 and 6 plot the average gap by relative quarter from about -16 to +23 with shaded bands (pp. 22-23). Pre-trend evidence: the pre-period gaps in Figs. 5-6 hover near zero by construction; RMSPE diagnostics are in App. B (median pre-RMSPE 2.810 $/MWh industrial, 2.485 residential; mean 4.498 and 3.504; 90th percentile 12.228 and 6.453; median six positive-weight donors; Table B.5, App. p. 5 [PDF 40]). Stated identifying assumptions (p. 19-20): weighted donors reproduce the counterfactual; no treated-utility-specific shock at the anchor; no spillovers to donors (the authors say wholesale or transmission spillovers to neighbors may violate this, p. 20); linear additive stock effect. No placebo tests, no permutation inference and no leave-one-out are reported. Heterogeneity: Eq. 23 interacts D_it with a binary high/low classifier built from pre-treatment information, using top tercile vs bottom two terciles (pp. 20-21).

**Headline findings**

- [primary] Industrial price, preferred specification (Ratio, with utility and quarter fixed effects): $/MWh per one percentage point of cumulative data center capacity relative to utility peak demand **0.807*** (SE 0.238); 42 treated utilities; 648 observations. Other columns: MW no FE 0.008 (0.005); MW FE 0.010 (0.007); Ratio no FE 0.477*** (0.140). Text rounds to 0.8 with SE 0.2 and says the MW columns are positive but insignificant (p. 21).** (Table 1, col. 4, p. 22; text p. 21)
- [primary] Residential price, preferred specification (Ratio, with fixed effects): $/MWh per one percentage point **0.261* (SE 0.158), significant at 10% only, not at 5%; 41 treated utilities; 627 observations. Other columns: MW no FE 0.005** (0.002); MW FE 0.005** (0.002); Ratio no FE -0.060 (0.174). The text (p. 24) calls column 4 '$0.3/MWh'; the 2022 discussion (p. 25) cites it as 0.28, which does not match the printed 0.261 and is not explained.** (Table 2, col. 4, p. 23; text pp. 24-25)
- [primary] Scaling to the average treated utility: 5.4 $/MWh industrial and 1.7 $/MWh residential **The average treated utility receives approximately 456 MW of additional data center capacity and has peak demand of 6,804 MW (p. 22). The paper does not print the implied ratio; my own arithmetic: 456 / 6,804 = 6.70 percentage points, and 6.70 x 0.807 = 5.41 and 6.70 x 0.261 = 1.75, matching the printed 5.4 and 1.7. So the figures are the preferred coefficient times the average treated utility's cumulative post-2020 addition as a share of peak, not per GW and not a per-facility effect. Whether the 456 MW and 6,804 MW are simple averages with the ratio taken of averages (as implied) rather than the average of utility-level ratios is not stated.** (p. 22 (industrial), p. 24 (residential); abstract p. 1)
- [primary] Industrial effect as share of 2020 level and of observed increase **8.1% of the average industrial price in 2020 and 15.7% of the total observed industrial price increase between 2020 and 2025 (abstract rounds to 15%).** (p. 22; abstract p. 1; p. 3)
- [primary] Residential effect as share of 2020 level and of observed increase **1.4% of the average residential price in 2020 and 4.0% of the total observed residential price increase between 2020 and 2025; the paper adds that on average 96% of the observed residential increase is driven by inflation, fuel and grid costs (p. 3), a statement that is the authors' attribution, not an estimated decomposition.** (p. 24; p. 3; abstract p. 1)
- [primary] How the 'share of observed increase' is computed: not stated. My reconstruction from Table A.4 using the group of 42 utilities with data center additions: residential price 123.59 in 2020 to 166.85 in 2025 (increase 43.26 $/MWh); industrial 67.84 to 102.14 (increase 34.30). 1.7 / 43.26 = 3.9% and 5.4 / 34.30 = 15.7%, both consistent with the printed shares. My reconstruction of 1.4% (1.7 / 123.59 = 1.38%) matches; for industrial, 5.4 / 67.84 = 7.96% versus the printed 8.1%, a small unexplained gap. **Table A.4 values as printed: utilities with DC added 2020-2025 (n=42): avg cumulative DC MW 675.85; residential price 123.59 (2020), 166.85 (2025); industrial 67.84 (2020), 102.14 (2025). Utilities without (n=291): 6.16 MW; residential 133.17, 168.01; industrial 101.31, 108.75. Units and nominal/real basis not labeled.** (Table A.4, App. p. 4 [PDF 39])
- [primary] Restricting additions to 2022 onward: industrial effect smaller, residential effect larger (Ratio, with FE) **Industrial 0.563* (SE 0.307), 41 treated utilities, 418 obs; residential 0.371** (0.176), 40 treated utilities, 404 obs. MW versions: industrial 0.009 (0.007); residential 0.004* (0.002).** (Table 3, p. 25; text pp. 24-25)
- [primary] Residential effect by developer type (cloud = Google, Amazon, Meta, Microsoft; third-party = all others), estimated jointly **Ratio with FE (col. 4): cloud 0.158 (0.160), third-party 1.042** (0.477). Ratio no FE (col. 3): cloud -0.122* (0.068), third-party 0.498** (0.220). MW FE (col. 2): cloud 0.006 (0.008), third-party 0.005 (0.014); neither MW column is significant. Wald test of equality of the two coefficients: p = 0.101 ('marginally significant'; the paper does not say for which column).** (Table 4, p. 26; text p. 26)
- [primary] Residential effect by utility operating characteristic, one at a time and jointly (Eq. 23) **Ratio columns: baseline (low) group slope 0.651 (0.4175) with high-utilization interaction -0.488 (0.434) [col. 5]; 0.232 (0.1497) with high-outage interaction 0.193 (0.447) [col. 6]; 0.790 (0.583) with high-experience interaction -0.604 (0.599) [col. 7]; all three jointly 1.326** (0.575) baseline, interactions -0.675* (0.398) utilization, -0.365 (0.329) outage, -0.521 (0.496) experience [col. 8]. MW columns: baseline 0.006*, 0.0125, 0.018***, 0.028***; high-experience interaction -0.014*** (0.002) alone and -0.015*** (0.003) jointly [cols. 3-4]; high-utilization -0.003 (0.008) alone and -0.005 (0.007) jointly; high-outage -0.007 (0.009) alone and -0.010 (0.009) jointly. Treated utilities 41 (40 in outage and joint columns); observations 627 (622). The paper's high/high/high implied effect: MW -0.002 (SE 0.006, p = 0.75); ratio -0.23 (SE 0.29, p = 0.41).** (Table 5, p. 28; text pp. 27-29)
- [primary] Utility cost per MWh (FERC Form 1 and 3-Q; 22 treated utilities for 2020-2025, 21 for 2022-2025) **2020-2025: MW 0.010 (0.016), Ratio 2.108 (1.662), both insignificant, 377 obs. 2022-2025: MW 0.012 (0.010), Ratio 1.769* (0.967), 219 obs. The introduction states this as 'a significant increase of 1.7 $/MWh in utility costs' for 2022-2025 (p. 4); the table coefficient 1.769 is per percentage point of peak demand and is significant only at 10%, so the introduction's '1.7 $/MWh' is that coefficient, not an average-utility effect (my reading; the paper does not say).** (Table 6, p. 30; p. 4)
- [primary] Electricity sales in the utility service region (MWh per quarter) **2020-2025: MW 291 (493), Ratio 9,446 (9,471), 42 treated, 648 obs. 2022-2025: MW 336 (459), Ratio 10,595 (15,616), 41 treated, 418 obs. All positive and none statistically significant; the authors read the absence of a significant sales effect as weakening a pure volume explanation (p. 31).** (Table 7, p. 31)
- [primary] Synthetic control fit (pre-treatment 16 quarters) **Industrial: mean RMSPE 4.498, median 2.810, 90th percentile 12.228 $/MWh. Residential: mean 3.504, median 2.485, 90th percentile 6.453. Median positive-weight donors 6 in both; median largest donor weight 0.376 (industrial), 0.341 (residential). The paper states residential normalized RMSPE is below one for every treated utility (App. p. 7 [PDF 42]); the New York State Electric & Gas residential value printed is 0.946 and the industrial values above 1 include NYSEG 2.594 (App. p. 6 [PDF 41]).** (Table B.5, App. p. 5 [PDF 40]; Tables B.6-B.7, App. pp. 6-7 [PDF 41-42]; text p. 21, p. 23)
- [primary] Event-time dynamics (figures only, no coefficients printed) **Figure 5 (industrial) and Figure 6 (residential) plot actual-minus-synthetic price gaps by quarter relative to the first post-2020 addition. By my visual read of PDF pages 22-23, pre-anchor gaps are near zero and post-anchor gaps drift upward with widening bands: industrial gap about 10 to 25 $/MWh by relative quarters 12-23 (band wide, one band spanning below zero near quarter 23); residential about 5 to 20 $/MWh over the same period, with bands that include zero in many quarters. These are my visual readings of plotted points, not printed values.** (Figs. 5-6, pp. 22-23)
- [primary] Descriptive pre-period comparison **In 2020, utilities hosting data centers had industrial prices about 37% lower and residential prices about 7% lower than utilities without data centers (text); Table A.4 gives 67.84 vs 101.31 industrial (33% lower by my arithmetic) and 123.59 vs 133.17 residential (7.2% lower by my arithmetic), so the 37% industrial figure does not reproduce from Table A.4 (the paper may be using Fig. 4 with a different set of utilities; not stated).** (pp. 3, 15; Table A.4, App. p. 4 [PDF 39])
- [primary] Sample composition of the facility data **1,758 active US data centers, 45.2 GW, 184 utilities, 43 states (p. 12); 561 eligible (at least 10 MW, post-2020) (p. 13); 385 across 42 utilities after restricting to bundled territories (pp. 15-16). Top owners by MW: Amazon AWS 9,942.40; Microsoft 5,225.20; Google 5,182.81; Facebook 4,597.73 (Table A.2). The paper says a little over half of active capacity is owned by the four cloud firms (p. 14).** (pp. 12-16; Tables A.1-A.3, App. pp. 1-3 [PDF 36-38])
- [primary] Wholesale price, retail bills, monthly bill dollars, usage: none estimated **The paper reports no wholesale outcome, no household bill result, and no electricity usage per customer outcome. Utility-wide sales are the only quantity outcome (Table 7).** (Sections 4-6, pp. 12-31)

**Heterogeneity reported:** (1) Developer type: cloud (Google, Amazon, Meta, Microsoft) versus all other owners, residential only (Table 4, p. 26): preferred third-party 1.042** (0.477) vs cloud 0.158 (0.160); Wald p = 0.101. No industrial-price version is reported. (2) Utility characteristics, residential only (Table 5, p. 28; definitions pp. 20-21): high utilization (average demand over peak demand in the most recent pre-treatment year, top tercile), high outage minutes (IEEE, excluding major event days, top tercile of minutes, i.e. lower reliability), high industrial experience (pre-treatment industrial share of total sales, top tercile). Results are in headline_findings. In the paper's own words the effect is 'concentrated among utilities with low utilization, higher baseline reliability, and low experience serving industrial customers' (p. 27); but the reliability interactions are not statistically significant in any column (p. 29), the utilization interaction is significant only in the joint ratio specification (-0.675*, p. 28), and the industrial-experience interaction is significant in the MW columns (-0.014***) but not in the ratio columns (-0.604, -0.521). The abstract's 'higher infrastructure utilization ... lower residential spillovers' therefore rests on a single 10% coefficient in the ratio specification plus insignificant negative signs elsewhere (the paper itself says 'suggestive evidence', p. 29). (3) Time window: all additions from 2020 vs additions from 2022 onward (Table 3, p. 25); 2022 effect residential larger (0.371** vs 0.261*), industrial smaller (0.563* vs 0.807***). (4) Cost outcome by window (Table 6, p. 30). Not reported: investor-owned versus public and co-op, retail-choice versus vertically integrated, region or market (PJM, ERCOT), data center size relative to utility beyond the Ratio treatment, or reserve margin. Utility ownership types appear in the 42-utility list only by name (public power and co-ops are present, e.g. City of Memphis, Salt River Project, Omaha Public Power District, LADWP, Tennessee Valley Authority, Berkeley Electric Coop; Tables B.6-B.7, App. pp. 6-7 [PDF 41-42]) and no ownership split is estimated. No multiple-testing adjustment is mentioned.

**Limitations the authors state:** Sec. 7, p. 32: (1) synthetic control relies on donors reproducing the untreated path; unobserved shocks coinciding with entry and affecting treated utilities differently may remain in the estimate. (2) The cumulative stock specification assumes a common marginal effect and may miss nonlinearities or facility differences (also p. 20, 'linear and additive stock effect'). (3) 'The number of treated utilities remains limited, which weakens the statistical strength of particularly the heterogeneity analysis.' Also: second identifying assumption, no contemporaneous utility-specific shock (p. 19); third, no spillovers to donors, which the authors say could occur through wholesale procurement or transmission across neighboring regions and which restricting donors to non-treated utilities 'does not eliminate' (p. 20); 'we cannot rule out confoundedness from unobserved common operating cost changes at the treated utility' and the result does not establish intentional cross-subsidy (p. 24); FERC cost sample is narrower (22 utilities) so cost effects are 'suggestive rather than conclusive' (p. 30); sales measure may reflect other demand, behind-the-meter supply, delivery-only arrangements, or imperfect correspondence between rated capacity and realized consumption (p. 31); utilities may already serve pre-2020 data center load and the estimand is incremental expansion relative to that baseline (p. 19); the Wald difference between developer types is only marginally significant (p. 26).

**What a skeptical referee would raise:** 1. Identification. The design uses a single anchor per treated utility (first post-2020 addition), a fixed synthetic control from only 16 pre-quarters, and then a pooled regression of gaps on the cumulative stock (pp. 16-18). No placebo or permutation inference, no leave-one-out and no per-utility effects are reported, so inference rests on 41-42 utility clusters and on pre-fit (median pre-RMSPE 2.8 and 2.5 $/MWh in Table B.5) that is comparable in size to the headline effects (average residential effect 1.7 $/MWh). Pre-fit is weak for a subset: the industrial 90th percentile RMSPE is 12.228 (App. p. 5 [PDF 40]) and several treated utilities have normalized RMSPE above 1 (Table B.6). 2. Treated sample is small and heterogeneous. 42 treated utilities include very large single cases (Virginia Electric & Power, 7,444.61 MW and 213 facilities in Table A.1) mixed with small ones; the average treated utility has 456 MW (p. 22). The linear stock assumption (p. 20) means a few utilities with very large ratios can dominate the slope; no influence or leave-one-out checks are shown. 3. Preferred residential estimate is marginal: 0.261* (SE 0.158), significant at 10% only (Table 2 col. 4), and the no-fixed-effects Ratio column is -0.060 (0.174). The MW columns are significant at 5% but the effect per MW is not the preferred scaling. The authors choose the Ratio fixed-effect column as preferred because it is the one that is significant for industrial prices (p. 21) and 'fixed-effect estimates are our preferred specifications' (p. 19); a referee would ask whether the choice among four columns is multiplicity. 4. Heterogeneity is thinly powered and not corrected for multiple testing: 3 characteristics x 2 scalings x 2 combinations plus developer type; the authors acknowledge limited treated utilities (p. 32). The Wald test on developer type is p = 0.101 and the Table 4 cloud coefficient is -0.122* in one column. The abstract's headline statements on Big Tech and on utilization/experience rest on these. Cloud vs third-party depends on an owner-name rule; xAI, Apple, Crusoe and Hut8 are all classed 'third-party' (Table A.2, App. p. 2 [PDF 37]). 5. Facility-to-utility link.
