Facilities are attached to an Aterio-'mapped electric utility' (pp. 12-13) with no description of the mapping or validation. Utilities with very high data center MW in Table A.1 that are absent from the treated list (Umatilla Electric Coop, Northern Virginia Electric Coop, City of Manassas, Denton County Electric Coop, PUD No. 2 of Grant County, plus Oncor and AEP Texas North) are not explained; the bundled-service filter presumably removed some of them but the paper does not say which filter removed which. 6. Retail choice and delivery-only. The filter keeps 'bundled' utilities (p. 15), but the paper never explains how a utility is classified as bundled in EIA 861M, and the treated list in Table B.6 contains utilities such as Commonwealth Edison, PPL Electric Utilities, Public Service Electric & Gas, Atlantic City Electric, Orange & Rockland, Ohio Edison and Ohio Power. My own recollection, not from the paper and uncertain, is that these operate in retail-choice states where revenue per kWh can reflect delivery-only or default-service customers. A referee would ask how revenue per MWh is built for them. 7. Revenue per MWh versus usage. Price is revenue divided by MWh (p. 14). The paper's own Table 7 shows no significant sales effect, but it does not test customer counts or residential sales per customer, so a rise in revenue per MWh due to falling residential usage is not distinguished from a rate increase. 8. Wholesale contamination of controls. The authors acknowledge possible spillovers to donors through wholesale procurement or transmission (p. 20); donors are not restricted by market or region, so region-wide wholesale cost increases from data center load affect both treated and donor utilities in the same market and bias toward zero or are mixed in; the paper does not show donor weights by region. 9. Timing. Treatment starts at Aterio's activation date; interconnection, rate cases and cost recovery usually precede activation, and there is no anticipation or lag analysis. Pre-2000 dates are truncated (p. 19), a sign that Aterio dates are not uniformly precise. 10. The 'share of observed increase' is not defined in the text (my reconstruction from Table A.4 matches the printed 4.0% and 15.7%); the 'explains 15% / 4%' framing compares a regression effect on a subset of utilities to an unadjusted nominal price increase, and the authors say '96%' of the residential increase is due to inflation and other drivers without estimating them (p. 3). 11. Internal inconsistencies a reader will find: 0.28 vs 0.261 (p. 25 vs p. 23), 37% industrial pre-gap vs 33% from Table A.4, 6.7 GW (text) vs 7,444.61 MW (Table A.1) for Virginia Electric & Power, the introduction's 'significant 1.7 $/MWh' cost effect vs a table coefficient of 1.769* per percentage point (pp. 4 vs 30), and 8.1% vs 7.96% on my arithmetic. 12. Scope. All price results are for utilities with data centers added 2020-2025 only; the large pre-2020 stock (for example in Virginia) stays in the baseline (p. 19), so the paper is about marginal additions on top of existing stocks. Water: the introduction mentions 'concerns around water, noise, and air pollution' (p. 1) as public concerns; the paper has no analysis of water rates or bills.

**What it would take to reproduce:** No code or data availability statement appears in the PDF (searched the text for code, replication, data availability). Data: EIA Form 861M (public), EIA Form 861 utility characteristics including outage minutes and industrial share (public), FERC Forms 1 and 3-Q (public filings), and the Aterio facility file (a vendor product; whether it is free or paid is not stated). Specification in one or two sentences: for each of 42 treated utilities (first post-2020 addition of at least 10 MW, bundled-service only), build a non-negative, sum-to-one weighted synthetic control from utilities with no data center additions over the horizon using the 16 pre-anchor quarters of utility-quarter price in $/MWh (matching covariates, if any, not stated); then regress the post-anchor gap on cumulative data center MW divided by pre-anchor annual peak demand times 100, with treated-utility and quarter-of-year fixed effects and utility-clustered standard errors (Eq. 21, Table 1 col. 4 and Table 2 col. 4). Unresolved details that would move a replication: how Aterio maps a facility to a utility; how bundled-service is classified from 861M; whether donors must be bundled; whether the synthetic-control optimizer includes covariates; how peak demand is measured and from what source; whether prices are nominal; whether all 16 pre-anchor quarters or only post-anchor quarters enter the second stage (the observation counts imply post-anchor only); how the share-of-increase denominators are defined (my reconstruction from Table A.4 is in headline_findings).

**Corrections to what we had heard:** 1. Confirmed, with refinements. Industrial: 5.4 $/MWh for the average treated utility, 15.7% of the observed industrial increase 2020-2025 (abstract rounds to 15%; text p. 22, abstract p. 1). The 5.4 is the Ratio, fixed-effects coefficient 0.807*** (Table 1 col. 4, p. 22) multiplied by the average treated utility's 456 MW addition over its 6,804 MW peak, not a measure of the whole US. The 'increase' it explains is the 2020-2025 increase in the average industrial price of utilities that received data centers, which I reconstruct from Table A.4 (not stated by the authors). The MW-based industrial coefficients are positive but insignificant (Table 1 cols. 1-2). Also: the study is of 42 utilities, not 'the US' in aggregate, and the phrase 'their region's' means the serving utility's territory. 2. Confirmed. Residential: 1.7 $/MWh (0.17 cents/kWh is my conversion), 4.0% of the observed increase (p. 24; abstract p. 1). The abstract says 'significant but small spillovers'; the preferred coefficient is 0.261* (SE 0.158), significant at 10% only (Table 2 col. 4, p. 23). 3. Mostly confirmed, with a caveat: the abstract says Big Tech 'no discernible price increases'; for residential prices the cloud coefficient is 0.158 (0.160) and the third-party coefficient is 1.042** (0.477) (Table 4 col. 4, p. 26), and the paper's own test of the difference is p = 0.101. This split is for residential prices only; no industrial split is shown. 'Big Tech' is Amazon, Google, Meta and Microsoft; everything else including xAI and Apple is classified 'third-party', and the paper calls them 'third-party data center providers' / 'computing infrastructure firms that offer third-party leasing services' (pp. 3-4, 25), while the abstract calls them 'co-location'. The paper's classification is by owner name, not by whether a facility is actually leased to others. 4. Confirmed on direction: higher utilization and more industrial experience are associated with smaller residential effects (Table 5, p. 28; abstract). Higher outage minutes (lower reliability) also have a smaller (insignificant) effect. Significance is weak and mixed (see heterogeneity_reported). 5. Confirmed: 385 data centers, 42 utilities (pp. 15-16; Table 1 p. 22); 41 for residential (p. 23, Table 2). Note the 385 are those added post-2020, at least 10 MW, in bundled territories, out of 561 eligible (p. 13). 6. Confirmed: 1.4% of the average 2020 residential price (p. 24). 7. Confirmed: Table 2 col. 4 shows 0.261 with one star (p < 0.10); note the MW specifications show 0.005** (5% level) in cols. 1-2 (p. 23), but the authors designate the Ratio fixed-effect column as preferred (p. 19, p. 24). 8. Confirmed: residential 0.371** (2022 onward, Table 3 col. 4, p. 25) vs 0.261* in the full window; the paper's text says the full-window figure is 0.28 (p. 25), which does not match the printed 0.261. Industrial goes the other way (0.563* vs 0.807***). 9. Confirmed: utility by quarter (Sec. 4.2 p. 14; Eq. 15-21 pp. 17-18). 10. Not in the paper: there is no date on the PDF. The only printed date-like text is 'Updated frequently'. The PDF file's embedded metadata shows 12 Oct 2026; I found no '30 September 2026' anywhere in the text. The method also differs from what 'what we heard' might imply: it is a synthetic control with a continuous cumulative treatment, not a staggered difference-in-differences.

## TWO linked documents, both read in full. (A) Wiser, R., Barbose, G., Gorman, W., O'Shaughnessy, E., Forrester, S., Donohoo-Vallett, P., Cappers, P., Deason, J. (Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory) and Hledik, R., Lam, L. (The Brattle Group). "Retail Electricity Price Trends and Drivers: Data Update-2026 Edition." LBNL briefing deck (slides, not a paper; no report number shown). First release title slide dated "April 2026" (63 pages, PDF created 2026-03-30); latest version title slide dated "July 2026 - update" (68 pages, PDF created 2026-07-06). (B) Wiser, R., O'Shaughnessy, E., Barbose, G., Cappers, P., Gorman, W. (all LBNL). "Factors influencing recent trends in retail electricity prices in the United States." The Electricity Journal 38(4), 107516, December 2025 (available online 10 October 2025). DOI 10.1016/j.tej.2025.107516. Open access. Related: October 2025 LBNL/Brattle "full summary" deck with the same title as (B) (66 pages), which I searched but did not read end to end.

- **Link:** Deck landing page: https://emp.lbl.gov/publications/retail-electricity-price-trends-and (returns 403 to automated clients). Copies actually read: July update https://web.archive.org/web/20260730125219/https://eta-publications.lbl.gov/sites/default/files/2026-07/retail_price_trends_2026_edition_julyupdate.pdf ; April/March edition https://web.archive.org/web/20261007103917/https://emp.lbl.gov/sites/default/files/2026-03/Retail%20Price%20Trends_2026%20edition.pdf ; journal article (DOE OSTI / eScholarship open copy) https://www.osti.gov/pages/servlets/purl/3000204 (permalink https://escholarship.org/uc/item/1x14m1s7); journal replication package https://web.archive.org/web/20251116034445/https://eta-publications.lbl.gov/sites/default/files/2025-10/pub_0.zip ; 2026 edition data workbook https://web.archive.org/web/20260330195005/https://eta-publications.lbl.gov/sites/default/files/2026-03/retail_price_trends_2026_edition_data_file.xlsx
- **How far we got:** read in full. emp.lbl.gov and eta-publications.lbl.gov both returned HTTP 403 to automated requests (landing page and both PDFs). I did not work around this. Instead: (1) the journal article was read from the open copy on DOE OSTI (osti.gov/pages/servlets/purl/3000204, the eScholarship/UC version of the published Elsevier PDF, 11 pages including cover). (2) Both editions of the 2026 deck, the 2026 data workbook, the journal replication zip (R scripts, inputs, regression output CSVs, variable-methods PDF), and the October 2025 full-summary deck were read from public Internet Archive Wayback Machine snapshots of the LBNL URLs. Text was extracted with pdftotext and the load-growth and PJM slides (July deck PDF pp. 25, 52, 53, 54, 55; journal p. 5) were also rendered and read as images because the key content is in charts. NOT obtained: the journal's Supplemental Information document itself (SI-1 to SI-9, hosted on ScienceDirect), though the posted output CSVs contain the SI regression results including the residential-price model (SI-8); and the July 2026 version of the data workbook (only the March/April workbook is archived). Local read-only copies are in a local scratch folder (lbnl_dl) (wb_jul.pdf, wb_mar.pdf, osti3000204.pdf), a local scratch folder (lbnl_zip/pub) and a local scratch folder (lbnl_xlsx/data.xlsx). A person wanting the canonical files should download by hand from https://emp.lbl.gov/retail-electricity-price-trends-and-drivers and the SI from https://doi.org/10.1016/j.tej.2025.107516.
- **Funding or affiliation:** U.S. Department of Energy under Contract No. DE-AC02-05CH11231. The journal article names the Office of Energy Efficiency and Renewable Energy and states DOE "had no role in study design, data collection, data analysis and interpretation, writing of the article, or decision to submit". The 2026 deck acknowledgement names DOE's "Office of Critical Minerals and Energy Innovation". Authors are LBNL staff; the 2026 deck adds two Brattle Group consultants (Hledik, Lam); Brattle is not on the journal article. Disclosed interests: Wiser is an Editorial Board member of The Electricity Journal and was recused from peer review; O'Shaughnessy "is an independent consultant" with a client list at cleankws.com. No utility or technology-company funding is disclosed. The Electricity Journal is a practitioner-oriented journal; the 2026 deck is not peer reviewed.
- **Unit:** State. Journal regression: 48 contiguous states (AK, HI, DC excluded), one cross-section per 5-year window. 2026 deck scatter charts: 50 states plus DC, one point per state, 2019 to 2025 change. Utility level appears only in one descriptive three-state case study (ND, NM, NE; 2019 to 2024). No utility-territory, county, or facility-level analysis anywhere.
- **Years:** Journal article: core window 2019-2024; robustness windows 2015-2020, 2016-2021, 2017-2022, 2018-2023, plus alternate spans ending 2024 and 2021-2023. Price series shown from 2010. 2026 deck: price data through 2025 (2025 uses preliminary EIA-861M); main comparisons 2025 vs 2024, 2025 vs 2019, 2025 vs 2010; data center/crypto capacity growth measured 2019-2024; early 2026 indicators (Jan-Apr 2026 vs Jan-Apr 2025) in the July update.
- **Outcome:** Change in state-level average retail electricity price in cents/kWh, inflation-adjusted. Journal (p. 2): prices from Form EIA-861 via EIA Electricity Data Browser, "all-in average price, inclusive of volumetric, demand, and fixed customer charges collected by all retail service providers in each state, including delivery-only, energy-only, and bundled" providers, i.e. total revenue divided by total retail sales; deflated with BLS regional CPI to real 2024$. Core dependent variable is the ALL-SECTOR average price change; residential price change is used only as a robustness check (SI-8; variable resi_rate_delta in the posted code). 2026 deck: same construct in real 2025$, EIA-861 annual plus EIA-861M preliminary for 2025; both all-sector and residential shown. This is average revenue per kWh, not a tariff rate and not a bill. No water outcome of any kind.
- **Data center data:** Not public. The deck chart cites "Source: EIA, S&P Global" and the workbook sheet "State load growth vs. prices" names "S&P Global, 451 Research Datacenter Knowledgebase" (a commercial subscription product). Only the resulting four-level state category is published, not MW, facility locations, sizes, or in-service dates. The journal article uses no data center data at all. The journal readme also states "Some of the underlying data are proprietary and cannot be shared in a disaggregated form" (gas hub prices are from ABB Ventyx Velocity Suite).
- **Treatment:** There is no data center treatment variable in the regression. The regressor is "load delta": percentage change in total state retail sales (all sectors, MWh) between start and end year (Table 1, journal p. 3; (S_Y - S_y)/S_y in the variable-methods PDF). Retail sales exclude load met by customer-sited generation (deck slide 47 footnote). Data centers enter only in the 2026 deck as a four-level colour code on two scatter charts: "Data center & cryptocurrency growth relative to state load in 2019", defined in the chart footnote as "increase in MWs from 2019-2024, relative to 2019 state load. Very high = > 45 MW/MWh; High = > 15 MW/MWh; Medium = > 5 MW/MWh" (units as printed; I am uncertain whether MW per TWh was intended). Category counts in the posted workbook: Low 30, Medium 13, High 5 (NV, IA, NE, NM, AZ), Very High 3 (ND, VA, OR). Data centers and cryptocurrency mining are pooled.
- **Method:** Journal: cross-sectional reduced-form OLS, one regression per 5-year window, N = 48. Functional form (p. 2): price delta = a + X*b + (prior 5-year price delta)*g + e. Eight regressors, all standardized: load delta, BTM solar delta, utility-scale wind+solar share delta, RPS growth requirement times regional REC cost, gas exposure (gas share times hub price), reliability (SAIFI) delta, California dummy, prior price trend. "Region-clustered robust standard errors"; significance flag is p < 0.1 only. The posted code confirms lm_robust(..., clusters = region) and the posted data confirm region takes only four values (midwest 12, northeast 9, south 16, west 11). No fixed effects, no panel, no instrument, no event study, no utility-level variation. Identification rests on an assertion: load delta is "defensibly exogenous" because electricity demand is "reasonably price-inelastic in the near to medium term" (p. 3); the authors concede it "may not be strictly exogenous". Shapley decomposition of R-squared (Fig. 1) and predicted-vs-actual (Fig. 2) are descriptive add-ons. 2026 deck: no new estimation. The load-growth evidence is two unfitted bubble scatters (no trend line, no coefficient, no R-squared) plus case studies; the one quantitative claim ("Over 1 cent/kWh reduction ... in highest-growth states") is explicitly "based on earlier statistical analysis", i.e. the 2019-2024 journal regression. The PJM case study reproduces the PJM Independent Market Monitor's counterfactual auction-revenue decomposition; it is not an LBNL estimate.

**Headline findings**

- [primary] Core result: state load growth associated with lower all-sector real retail prices, 2019-2024. Exact text: 'a 10 % increase in load was associated with a 0.6 (+/-0.1) cent/kWh reduction in prices, on average' (+/- is the cluster-robust SE). **-0.6 cents/kWh per +10% load (SE 0.1); standardized coefficient -0.47* (0.09), p<0.1; adj. R2 0.86** (Electricity Journal article sec. 4.4, p. 6; Table 2, p. 4 (2019-2024 column). I reproduced the unit conversion from the posted data: SD of load_pct = 0.0814, so -0.4736 x 0.10/0.0814 = -0.58.)
- [primary] Load coefficient across the five core windows is always negative but significant in only three of five. **2015-2020 -0.33 (0.13) n.s.; 2016-2021 -0.30* (0.06); 2017-2022 -0.23 (0.23) n.s.; 2018-2023 -0.36* (0.10); 2019-2024 -0.47* (0.09)** (Journal Table 2, p. 4; identical values in posted Outputs/reg_results_core.csv)
- [primary] Residential prices: the negative load relationship 'was smaller and lost statistical significance when analyzing residential prices alone (see SI-8)'. Authors attribute this to load growth being 'led by commercial customers' and cost allocation that has 'tended to benefit those large, non-residential customers'. **Residential-price model, 2019-2024: load coefficient -0.22 (SE 0.12), not significant at p<0.1; adj. R2 0.786. Other windows: -0.19, -0.16, +0.03, -0.16, none significant.** (Statement: journal sec. 4.4, p. 6, repeated in Conclusions p. 8. Coefficients: authors' posted replication output Outputs/robust_resi_rates.csv (the SI-8 model, generated by 4_robustness_checks.R). The SI document itself was not opened.)
- [primary] 2026 deck all-sector claim: 'From 2019 to 2025, states with the highest growth generally saw average retail prices decline in real terms'; 'Over 1 cent/kWh reduction in all-sector average prices in highest-growth states, based on earlier statistical analysis'; 'Those states where load declined often experienced price increases'; 'Commercial sector saw most load growth; had largest price reductions'; impact acknowledged as 'bi-directional, as price reductions should also increase load'. **>1 cent/kWh (not re-estimated on 2025 data)** (July 2026 deck slide 51 (PDF p. 52); identical text in April edition slide 47 (PDF p. 48). Chart: 'Load Growth vs. Retail Price Changes: 2019 to 2025', unfitted bubble scatter.)
- [primary] 2026 deck data center statement, all-sector chart: 'Note: Presence of significant data center load does not appear to alter conclusions'. No test, coefficient or subgroup statistic is given; it is a visual read of bubble colours. **none reported** (July deck slide 51 footer (PDF p. 52))
- [primary] 2026 deck residential claim. Slide title: 'Residential customers may have benefited from load growth from 2019 to 2025 in some states, but the relationship is less clear and may change'. Bullets: 'The relationship between residential prices and load growth is not as clear as for all-sector average prices'; residential customers 'in some states appear to have benefitted, or at least not been injured'; 'The presence of significant data center and cryptocurrency growth does not appear to alter these conclusions'; 'Past need not be prelude, illustrated by the PJM example on a later slide'. **none reported (descriptive scatter only)** (July deck slide 52 (PDF p. 53); April edition slide 48 (PDF p. 49))
- [primary] State values behind the two scatters for the three 'Very High' data center/crypto growth states, 2019-2025 real 2025$ change (all-sector / residential) and retail sales growth. **North Dakota -2.91 / -1.12 cents/kWh, load +40.7%; Virginia -0.65 / -0.01, load +22.2%; Oregon +0.37 / +1.45, load +22.2%. Texas (coded Medium): -0.72 / +0.57, load +21.0%.** (Authors' posted 2026 data workbook (April 2026), sheet 'State load growth vs. prices', rows 12, 29, 45, 37. July workbook not obtained; the July chart looks unchanged.)
- [primary] PJM exception, capacity auction decomposition. Slide title: 'Case Study: PJM Capacity Auction demonstrates price increases with load growth'. Data center load growth is credited with a '174% increase in auction revenues'; other factors: summer capacity ratings for CC/CTs 23-118%, demand curve price cap 68%, ELCC adoption 49%. Slide adds 'Capacity prices are one but not the only contributor to retail price increases'. These are PJM IMM figures for the 2025/2026 Base Residual Auction and refer to auction revenues, not retail prices. **174% (data centers); 23-118%; 68%; 49%** (July deck slide 54 (PDF p. 55), sourced to Monitoring Analytics IMM Analysis of the 2025/2026 RPM BRA Parts A-G. October 2025 full summary slide 54 adds that the forecast 'included almost 8 GW of data center load'.)
- [primary] PJM capacity clearing price history (weighted average). **2024/2025: $45.57 nominal ($46.77 in 2025$) per MW-day; 2025/2026: $296.98; 2026/2027: $329.17 nominal ($320.52 in 2025$); 2027/2028: $333.44 nominal ($317.68)** (2026 data workbook sheet 'PJM capacity auction prices'; charted on July deck slide 54 (PDF p. 55))
- [primary] PJM pass-through to wholesale and retail: 'Capacity costs drove average PJM wholesale costs up 0.9 cents/kWh in 2025 vs. 2024; an additional 0.6 cents/kWh impact is expected in 2026'. Footnote: 'separate from capacity, wholesale energy costs increased 1.7 cents/kWh from 2024 to 2025 as fuel prices rose' and the capacity contribution 'did not greatly exceed earlier years'. PJM-state all-sector price increases 2025 vs 2024: DC 3.1, NJ 2.0, MD 1.5, IL 1.2, PA 1.2, IN 0.9, OH 0.8 cents/kWh. **+0.9 cents/kWh capacity (2025), +0.6 expected (2026); +1.7 cents/kWh energy** (July deck slide 24 (PDF p. 25); workbook sheet 'PJM capacity prices' gives capacity cost 0.37 (2024), 1.28 (2025), 1.91 (2026 est.) cents/kWh in 2025$)
- [primary] PJM state retail map: average increase in NOMINAL prices December 2024 to December 2025, shown only in bins (15-25%, 10-15%, 5-10%, <5%); not attributed to data centers and not decomposed. Note says vertically integrated states are 'shielded to some extent'. **bins only; top bin 15-25%** (July deck slide 54 right panel (PDF p. 55), source EIA Electricity Monthly Update Dec. 2025)
- [primary] Journal conclusion on PJM and generalizability: 'spikes in load growth can result in significant, near-term retail price increases. Results from recent capacity auctions in the mid-Atlantic region prove this point'; 'our results cast doubt on the simple view that load growth will necessarily increase prices over the medium- to longer-term'; 'medium- to longer-term effects are uncertain'. **none** (Journal Conclusions, p. 8)
- [primary] ND, NM, NE utility-level case study (2019-2024): utilities with >20% load growth had residential load growth -1%, residential price change -1.3 cents/kWh, C&I load growth +64%, C&I price change -2.7 cents/kWh. Low-growth (0-20%) utilities: residential -1.3, C&I -1.4. Negative-growth utilities: -0.9 and -0.9. Headline: 'residential customers were not harmed'. **-1.3 residential vs -2.7 C&I cents/kWh (real 2025$) in the >20% growth group** (July deck slide 53 (PDF p. 54); workbook sheet 'ND, NM, NE sectoral impacts')
- [primary] National context: all-sector average price 2025 vs 2024 up 5.3% nominal, 2.6% real; 2019-2025 up 29% nominal, 3% real; 2025 is 6% below 2010 in real terms. Residential nominal 2019-2025 +33% vs commercial +26%, industrial +27%; residential real +5.6%. Real all-sector prices fell in 29 states 2019-2025; nominal residential prices rose in all states. **5.3% / 2.6%; 29% / 3%; -6%; 33% / 26% / 27%; 5.6%; 29 states** (July deck slides 6, 7, 8, 12, 13 (PDF pp. 7, 8, 9, 13, 14))
- [primary] Load growth context: retail sales CAGR 0.2%/yr 2010-2019 vs 1.1%/yr 2019-2025; 2024-2025 growth 2.1%; 2019-2025 TWh growth 70% C&I, 30% residential. Highest 2019-2025 state growth: ND, NM, NE, VA, OR, TX. **0.2%/yr; 1.1%/yr; 2.1%; 70/30** (July deck slides 47-48 (PDF pp. 48-49))
- [primary] Forward-looking caveats in the deck: 'Rapid data center buildout plus supply and delivery constraints can increase costs'; IOU generation CapEx rose 22% in 2025 (real), 'driven by load growth' among other things, and 'price impacts are delayed, can grow w/ time'; residential vs C&I price gap has grown; utility-stated benefits from data center deals 'are possible or stated benefits, there is risk that the new tariff structures will be insufficient to fully protect other customers'. **22% generation CapEx growth in 2025** (July deck slides 25, 56, 57, 58 (PDF pp. 26, 57, 58, 59))

**Heterogeneity reported:** Limited. (1) Sector: all-sector vs residential (residential effect roughly half the size and not significant; commercial sector saw most growth and largest price reductions). (2) Time window: five rolling 5-year windows plus alternate spans; load coefficient negative throughout, significant in three of five core windows, and much smaller for the one-year 2023-2024 span (-0.19). (3) Data center/crypto intensity: four colour categories on the deck scatters only, no statistics by category. (4) Market structure is discussed qualitatively (vertically integrated vs restructured; PJM as the counterexample) but the ISO and retail-competition dummies are not interacted with load and are insignificant on their own (2019-2024 ISO coefficient 0.01, SE 0.13). (5) Utility level only for ND, NM, NE. No heterogeneity by utility ownership, rate design, large-load tariff, or facility size in relation to load growth.

**Limitations the authors state:** Deck slide 1 (PDF p. 2): "Is not definitive: analyzes subset of drivers from available data"; does not analyze each state; centered on prices not bills; "Does not predict future price trends". Deck slide 39 note: simple bubble graphics "should be interpreted with care ... because price changes were driven by multiple factors and due to correlated effects". Deck slides 51-52: impact is bi-directional; residential relationship "less clear and may change"; "Past need not be prelude". Deck slide 21: year-over-year driver attribution is "subject to various state ratemaking idiosyncrasies". Journal p. 2-3: only 48 observations "limiting the statistical power"; variables are blunt proxies; cost-recovery timing varies (contemporaneous vs multi-decade vs balancing accounts) and the model "is unable to capture these nuances"; results are "imprecise or inaccurate for individual states"; load delta "may not be strictly exogenous"; RPS and BTM variables are collinear; the prior-trend control could bias coefficients; earlier windows may be distorted by COVID. Journal p. 8: unclear whether relationships extend to "significant, nationwide load growth"; recommends utility-level, customer-class and single-driver studies. Readme: some 2024 data are preliminary EIA estimates; some inputs proprietary.

**What a skeptical referee would raise:** 1. It is not a data center study. Neither document estimates a data center effect. The regressor is total retail sales growth from any cause (oil and gas in the Bakken, population, electrification, crypto, data centers). The only data center content is a four-level colour code from a proprietary source and the sentence that it "does not appear to alter conclusions", with no statistic. Only three states are "Very High" and they point in different directions for residential prices (ND -1.12, VA -0.01, OR +1.45 cents/kWh). 2. Composition effect in the outcome. The all-sector "price" is total revenue divided by total kWh. Adding large, high-load-factor customers on low industrial or commercial rates lowers that average mechanically even if no existing customer's rate changes. The deck's own note that the commercial sector had the most growth and the largest price reductions is consistent with this. The residential series is the relevant one for household bills, and there the journal result is about half the size and not significant (-0.22, SE 0.12). 3. Reverse causality and site selection. Large flexible loads locate where power is cheap and surplus exists (ND, NM, NE are the authors' own examples of "abundant, low-cost energy"). The exogeneity argument cites short-run price inelasticity of existing demand, which does not address cross-state siting of new load. The deck concedes the relationship is bi-directional. 4. The negative slope is partly a contraction story. The high-price-increase states (CA, ME, MA, NY, DC, MD) have falling retail sales tied to behind-the-meter solar, plus wildfire, storm, RPS and transmission costs. The abstract itself frames the finding as "shrinking customer loads". That says little about what happens when a utility adds a gigawatt-scale customer. 5. Inference is fragile. 48 observations, 8 regressors plus a constant, standard errors clustered on only four Census regions (confirmed in the posted data), significance reported only at p < 0.1, and North Dakota is a high-leverage point (+37% load 2019-2024, about 4.1 SD above the mean). The California dummy alone carries much of the fit. 6. Timing. The regression window ends in 2024, before most AI-era capacity came online and before associated generation and transmission capital entered rate base. The deck itself reports IOU generation CapEx up 22% in 2025, partly for anticipated load, with delayed and growing price effects. Data center capacity growth is measured 2019-2024 while prices run to 2025. 7. Geography. Data center load is concentrated in specific utility territories (for example northern Virginia), and costs are allocated by utility and rate class. State averages dilute any local effect and cannot see transmission cost socialization across a utility or RTO zone. 8. Measurement. Data center capacity is in MW (not energy), pooled with crypto mining, binned into four categories, and normalized with units printed as "MW/MWh". Retail sales exclude self-supplied load. Prices are deflated with regional CPI, so "real declines" coexist with nominal residential increases in every state. 9. The PJM case study is borrowed, not estimated. The 174% figure is the IMM's all-else-constant counterfactual for auction revenues, is one of several overlapping factors that do not sum, and is not translated into a retail cents/kWh effect attributable to data centers. The deck's own footnote shows energy costs rose more (1.7 cents/kWh) than capacity costs (0.9 cents/kWh) in 2025, and the retail map is nominal December-to-December changes in bins. 10. Evidence grade. The 2026 edition is a slide deck with unfitted scatters, not a peer-reviewed analysis, and its quantified load claim is carried over from the 2019-2024 regression rather than re-estimated on 2025 data. The list of utility-claimed customer savings (Georgia, DTE, PG&E, Entergy and others) is self-reported by interested parties, which the authors flag. 11. Scope. Nothing on water prices or water utilities.

**What it would take to reproduce:** Unusually replicable for the state-level result. (1) Journal: data and R code are posted (pub.zip linked from the LBNL publication page): Data/inputs.csv (state-year panel 2010-2024 with rate_real_2024, rate_nominal, resi_rate_real, sales_mwh, generation by fuel, BTM, SAIFI, region, etc.), scripts 1_regression_set.R through 4_*.R, and output CSVs for the core and all robustness models. Needs R with tidyverse and estimatr. I did not run the code; I confirmed from the posted regression set (stdlib arithmetic only) that the 2019-2024 standardized load coefficient converts to -0.58 cents/kWh per 10% load, matching the published 0.6. Proprietary pieces (ABB Velocity gas hub prices) are included only in aggregated form. (2) 2026 deck: the posted workbook sheet 'State load growth vs. prices' gives, for 50 states plus DC, the 2019-2025 real price change (all-sector and residential), retail sales % change, state load, and the data center category. Everything except the category can be rebuilt from EIA-861 / EIA-861M (revenue, sales by sector and state) and BLS regional CPI. The data center category cannot be rebuilt from public data (S&P Global 451 Research); a public substitute would be needed. (3) My own descriptive arithmetic on the posted sheets, NOT numbers reported by the authors: bivariate correlation of load growth with price change 2019-2025 is about -0.61 (all-sector) and -0.45 (residential), n = 51; dropping CA, ND, ME and DC gives about -0.51 and -0.34. Mean residential real price change by data center category: Low +1.14, Medium +0.21, High -1.25, Very High +0.11 cents/kWh (n = 30, 13, 5, 3). In the 2019-2024 regression set the bivariate correlations are -0.53 (all-sector) and -0.37 (residential). I counted 28 jurisdictions with negative all-sector change in that sheet versus the deck's "29 states"; I did not resolve the one-state difference. (4) PJM figures trace to public IMM and PJM auction reports.

**Corrections to what we had heard:** 1. Dating: the first 2026 edition's title slide and data workbook say "April 2026", not March (the PDF was created 30 March 2026 and sits in a /2026-03/ folder). The later version's title slide says "July 2026 - update". The July version adds five slides (household expenditure, state and regional electricity burden, regional gas pass-through) and two more utility-claim examples (CenterPoint, Xcel); the load-growth and PJM slide text is word-for-word the same in both editions, with slide numbers shifted (all-sector scatter is slide 47 in April, slide 51 in July). 2. Authorship: "Wiser, Barbose and coauthors ... with Brattle" is right for the 2026 deck (8 LBNL authors plus Hledik and Lam of Brattle). The 2025 Electricity Journal article has five LBNL authors only, in the order Wiser, O'Shaughnessy, Barbose, Cappers, Gorman; Brattle is not an author. Its title is "Factors influencing recent trends in retail electricity prices in the United States", not "Retail Electricity Price Trends and Drivers". 3. "The regression or chart behind each": there is no regression in the 2026 edition. The only regression is the 2019-2024 (and earlier windows) cross-section in the journal article, and it contains no data center variable. The 2026 deck's ">1 cent/kWh" statement is explicitly "based on earlier statistical analysis". 4. "State-level relationship ... data centers": the study's relationship is between total retail sales growth and prices. Data centers appear only as a categorical overlay (pooled with cryptocurrency) on two descriptive charts. 5. "PJM capacity exception": the deck presents PJM as a case study showing load growth plus supply constraints can raise prices, using the PJM market monitor's attribution of auction revenues (174% for data center load). It does not report a retail price effect of data centers in PJM, and it states capacity prices are "one but not the only contributor". 6. Residential vs all-sector: the brief's framing is consistent with the documents, but the precise claim is weaker than commonly summarized. The journal says the residential effect "was smaller and lost statistical significance"; the deck says residential customers "may have benefited ... in some states" or were "at least not ... injured". 7. Funding office differs between the two documents (EERE for the article; Office of Critical Minerals and Energy Innovation for the 2026 deck), both under the same LBNL contract.

## (a) Energy and Environmental Economics, Inc. (E3),  study team (alphabetized) Linus Beer, Nicole Chan, Kushal Patel, Paul Picciano, Shana Ramirez, Isabelle Riu, Aneri Shah. "Understanding the Drivers of Rising Electricity Rates and the Role of Data Centers." White paper, May 2026, 38 PDF pages (numbered 1-37 after the cover). No series number. (b) Monitoring Analytics, LLC (Independent Market Monitor for PJM): "Analysis of the 2025/2026 RPM Base Residual Auction – Part G" (revised, June 3, 2025); "Analysis of the 2026/2027 RPM Base Residual Auction – Part A" (October 1, 2025); "Analysis of the 2027/2028 RPM Base Residual Auction – Part A" (January 5, 2026); "Analysis of the 2028/2029 RPM Base Residual Auction – Part A" (October 30, 2026, the latest).

- **Link:** E3: https://www.ethree.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/05/Understanding-the-Drivers-of-Rising-Electricity-Rates-and-the-Role-of-Data-Centers_E3-2026.pdf | IMM 2025/26 Part G: https://www.monitoringanalytics.com/reports/reports/2025/IMM_Analysis_of_the_20252026_RPM_Base_Residual_Auction_Part_G_20250603_Revised.pdf | IMM 2026/27 Part A: https://www.monitoringanalytics.com/reports/Reports/2025/IMM_Analysis_of_the_20262027_RPM_Base_Residual_Auction_Part_A_20251001.pdf | IMM 2027/28 Part A: https://www.monitoringanalytics.com/reports/Reports/2026/IMM_Analysis_of_the_20272028_RPM_Base_Residual_Auction_Part_A_20260105.pdf | IMM 2028/29 Part A: https://www.monitoringanalytics.com/reports/Reports/2026/IMM_Analysis_of_the_20282029_RPM_Base_Residual_Auction_Part_A_20261030.pdf | IMM index pages: https://www.monitoringanalytics.com/reports/Reports/2025.shtml and /2026.shtml
- **How far we got:** read in part. All five documents were fetched directly from the publishers' own sites (ethree.com, monitoringanalytics.com) with no access barrier, and read page by page as rendered PDF pages. E3: read in full, all 38 pages including Appendix A.1 (study table) and A.2 (tariff table). IMM 2028/29 Part A: read pp. 1-25 (introduction, conclusions, data center load tables, VRR curves, all revenue scenario tables 10-13); the cleared-MW tables after p. 25 not read. IMM 2025/26 Part G: read pp. 1-14 (conclusions, Table 4, Table 7 and the Scenario 87/88 text); remaining pages on Scenarios 89-94 detail not read. IMM 2026/27 Part A: read pp. 1-13 (introduction, conclusions, Tables 1-7); detailed scenario tables 8-13 not read, but the same figures are restated in Table 1 of the two later reports. IMM 2027/28 Part A: read pp. 1-9 (introduction, conclusions, Table 1, Table 2); later tables not read. Marked 'partial' only because of the unread back pages of the IMM reports; every number below tagged 'primary' was seen on the page cited. Note: the fetch tool itself auto-saved the PDFs under the local fetch cache (webfetch-1794411302785-gi7wli.pdf = E3; webfetch-1794411333363-6j6uns.pdf = IMM 25/26 Part G; webfetch-1794411356511-3eudhz.pdf = IMM 26/27 Part A; webfetch-1794411373242-rse8f6.pdf = IMM 27/28 Part A; webfetch-1794411373242-dlbriv.pdf = IMM 28/29 Part A). I wrote no files.
- **Funding or affiliation:** E3: funded by the Data Center Coalition (the data center industry trade association), which reviewed the white paper before publication; E3 states the conclusions are its own (p. 1). Two of the 11 reviewed studies are E3's own work: 'Tailored for scale' (funded by Amazon, per p. 16) and the JLARC Virginia study (first-listed author K. Patel is on this E3 team). IMM: Monitoring Analytics, LLC is the Independent Market Monitor for PJM, a tariff-defined monitoring role. It has an active policy position (new data center load should bring its own generation; it filed a FERC complaint, Docket EL26-30-000, November 25, 2025), and these reports are written partly in support of that position.
- **Unit:** E3: a literature review (11 studies), 4 expert interviews, a tariff inventory (one row per large-load tariff or special contract), and one proprietary PJM-wide decomposition of the RTO capacity clearing price change between two auctions. No household, utility or county panel of its own. IMM: the PJM capacity auction as a whole, one Base Residual Auction per delivery year; outcome is total RPM market revenues in dollars per delivery year, re-cleared under alternative demand scenarios (with locational distribution of data center load across LDAs accounted for).
- **Years:** E3: reviewed studies cover roughly 2019-2025 retail price data (Bates White 2019-2024; LBNL 2019-2025; Georgia Tech 2010-2024 entries); tariff inventory 2018-2026; PJM decomposition compares the 2024/2025 and 2025/2026 auctions. IMM: delivery years 2025/2026 (auction July 17-23, 2024), 2026/2027 (July 9-15, 2025), 2027/2028 (December 4-10, 2025), 2028/2029 (June 30-July 7, 2026). The 2029/2030 BRA is scheduled for December 9-15, 2026 and has no IMM analysis yet.
- **Outcome:** E3: no single outcome variable. It reports other studies' outcomes (state average retail price in cents/kWh from Bates White and LBNL; county electricity prices from the Georgia Tech paper; utility revenue minus marginal cost to serve from its own Amazon-funded study) and, for its own PJM analysis, the RTO capacity clearing price in nominal $/MW-day ($28.92 in 2024/25, $269.92 in 2025/26, p. 23). It does not analyze residential bills directly. IMM: 'total RPM market revenues' for the BRA in dollars per delivery year, based on actual clearing prices, quantities and uplift MW, from the IMM's own re-clearing of the auction with actual (confidential) offers. This is a wholesale capacity cost paid by load, not a residential rate; neither source uses EIA-861.
- **Data center data:** No facility-level data center data in either source. E3's tariff inventory comes from Halcyon (a commercial data vendor), listed in Appendix A.2; E3 says it may not capture all tariffs. The IMM's data center MW come from PJM's annual Long-Term Load Forecast Reports and the large load adjustment requests submitted by utilities to PJM's Load Analysis Subcommittee (public in aggregate; the IMM footnotes link to the pjm.com files). These are utility-submitted forecasts of load, not observed facilities, and the IMM itself calls them highly uncertain.
- **Treatment:** E3 PJM decomposition: the 3.2 GW year-over-year increase in forecast peak load between the 2024/25 and 2025/26 auctions (labelled 'Peak'), which E3 describes as primarily data centers; other factors are supply (retirements outpacing additions), program design (accreditation rules, IRM, FPR, ELCC, VRR), Net CONE, and Dominion leaving the FRR alternative. IMM: removal of PJM-forecast data center MW from the peak load forecast used to build the demand (VRR) curve. Two versions per auction: 'above embedded' = forecast new data center load plus forecast growth at existing data centers; 'embedded and above embedded' (all data center load) = that plus existing data center load at the time of the forecast. MW removed (IMM 2028/29 Part A Table 6, p. 13): 2025/26: 4,654 above embedded / 7,927 all; 2026/27: 7,892 / 11,993; 2027/28: 13,018 / 17,071; 2028/29: 16,436 / 21,470.
- **Method:** Neither source is an econometric study. E3: narrative review plus a 'Shapley-style' attribution. Per footnote 66 (p. 24) E3 reconstructs the auctions with a simplified supply-demand framework (ELCC-adjusted supply meeting the administrative VRR curve) and averages each driver's price impact over all orderings of five drivers; inputs are public PJM data plus 'structured assumptions', and E3 states the result does not reflect unit-level bidding or dynamic market responses. IMM: a deterministic counterfactual re-clearing of the actual auction. The exact counterfactual wording is that the BRA is cleared with a peak load forecast that excludes the stated data center MW 'and everything else had remained the same', i.e. actual supply offers, auction parameters and the VRR curve shape in force are held fixed and only the demand curve shifts. For 2026/27 onward the base case uses the actual (price-capped and price-floored) VRR curve; separate scenarios repeat the exercise on the uncapped 'unrestricted/alternate' curve and on the IMM's own proposed curve. No supply response, no uncertainty bounds; the IMM says results are estimates, not strictly additive, and correct as to direction and order of magnitude.

**Headline findings**

- [primary] E3: number of quantitative studies reviewed is 11, plus interviews with four industry experts. Appendix A.1 lists them: (1) Bates White, Leveraging large loads to improve the U.S. electricity system, Feb 2026, backward-looking; (2) EPRI, Powering Intelligence 2026, Feb 2026, forward; (3) Federal Reserve Bank of Dallas, Data center boom expected to raise electricity component of PCE inflation, Mar 2026, forward; (4) Georgia Institute of Technology (Yue and Zeng), The Local Economic Effects of Data Center Entry, Mar 2026, backward, staggered difference-in-differences; (5) Union of Concerned Scientists, Data Center Power Play, Jan 2026, forward; (6) IMM for PJM, Analysis of the 2026/2027 RPM BRA Part A, 2025, backward; (7) Charles River Associates, Retail rate trends in the US (appendix says Sept 2025; footnote 51 dates it Feb 2, 2026, prepared for Edison Electric Institute); (8) E3, Tailored for scale, Dec 2025, both (Amazon-funded); (9) LBNL, Factors influencing recent trends in retail electricity prices in the United States, Oct 2025, backward; (10) UCS, Connection Costs, Sept 2025, labelled policy brief / qualitative with quantitative evidence; (11) JLARC, Virginia data center study, Dec 2024, backward (E3-authored). **11 studies; 4 interviews** (p. 5 (count); Appendix A.1, pp. 34-35 (list))
- [primary] E3: count of new large load tariffs established 2018-2026, and how many were implemented in 2025-2026. My own row count of Appendix A.2 matches: 38 rows, of which 13 are dated 2026 and 17 are dated 2025. **at least 38 tariffs (2018-2026); 30 in 2025-2026** (Report in Brief, p. 2 and footnote 1; Appendix A.2, pp. 36-37; Figure 3, p. 10)
- [primary] E3 PJM capacity attribution (E3's own proprietary analysis): share of the capacity price increase between the 2024/2025 and 2025/2026 auctions attributable to load growth, with the rest attributed to program parameters (IRM, FPR, ELCC, VRR), supply constraints and Net CONE. Figure 6 waterfall in nominal $/MW-day: 2024/25 price $29; peak load grew 3.2 GW +$121; supply (retirements outpaced additions) +$89; program design +$82; CONE/Net CONE -$24; Dominion no longer under FRR -$26; 2025/26 price $270. **~50% load growth / ~50% other; +$121 of a net +$241 per MW-day** (Executive Summary p. 3; p. 24 text and Figure 6; method in footnote 66)
- [primary] E3: actual RTO clearing prices used in the case study, and its statement that about 93% of the PJM forecast load growth was driven by forecast data center additions (cited to PJM and to IMM Part G). **$28.92/MW-day (2024/25) to $269.92/MW-day (2025/26); 153 GW forecast load, +3.2 GW; ~93%** (Case study box, p. 23, footnotes 53, 60-63)
- [primary] E3 on later auctions (footnote only): peak load increases drove much of the incremental price increase in the 2026/27 auction; the 2027/28 increase is harder to attribute. No percentages given. **no number reported** (footnote 65, p. 24)
- [primary] E3: headline claims from its own prior work that it cites as evidence. Amazon-funded four-utility study: average net surplus per facility, and projected surplus per 100 MW data center by 2030. **$3.4 million per facility; up to $6.1 million per 100 MW by 2030** (Executive Summary p. 3; p. 16)
- [primary] E3's summary of the Georgia Tech difference-in-differences paper (E3's restatement, not checked against the paper here): price increase in low-spillover counties after data center entry, three years before vs after activation, 2010-2024; median entry size 10 MW. **about 5.0% total; 6.6% industrial; 3.6% residential; 3.6% commercial** (p. 17)
- [primary] E3's restatement of UCS Connection Costs: local transmission projects tied to data center connections in seven PJM states in 2024, and related cost; 6 of the 130 described as having no cost to other customers. **130 projects; $4.356 billion** (p. 19)
- [primary] IMM, 2025/2026 BRA, all data center load (Scenario 88): increase in BRA revenues from including 7,927 MW of existing plus forecast data center load. Actual revenues $14,687,047,358; counterfactual $5,354,943,499. The 174.3% is the increase over the counterfactual; the 63.5% is the same dollar amount as a share of actual revenues. This is the source of the secondhand '$9.3B (63%)'. **$9,332,103,858; +174.3% over counterfactual; 63.5% of actual** (IMM 2025/26 Part G (June 3, 2025), pp. 1-2, p. 13 and Table 7)
- [primary] IMM, 2025/2026 BRA, forecast data center load only (Scenario 87): effect of 4,654 MW of above-embedded load. Counterfactual revenues $6,944,087,201. **$7,742,960,157; +111.5% over counterfactual; 52.7% of actual** (IMM 2025/26 Part G, p. 2, p. 12 and Table 7)
- [primary] IMM, 2026/2027 BRA (Scenario 4, all 11,993 MW; Scenario 3, 7,892 MW above embedded, gives the identical figure because the $177.24 price floor binds). Actual revenues $16,124,370,889; counterfactual $8,853,172,918. Share of actual revenues, about 45.1%, is my arithmetic, not a figure I saw printed. **$7,271,197,971; +82.1% over counterfactual** (IMM 2026/27 Part A (Oct 1, 2025), pp. 2-3)
- [primary] IMM, 2026/2027 BRA on the uncapped (unrestricted) VRR curve (Scenario 6 vs Scenario 1): the data center effect absent the Shapiro price collar. Uncapped revenues would have been $19,294,286,100. **$14,189,483,234; +278.0%** (IMM 2026/27 Part A, pp. 2 and 4)
- [primary] IMM, 2027/2028 BRA (Scenario 4, all 17,071 MW). Actual revenues $16,411,578,225; counterfactual $9,913,924,713. Above-embedded only (Scenario 3, 13,018 MW): $6,244,523,827 or +61.4%. Share of actual, about 39.6%, is my arithmetic. This is the Bloomberg '$6.5 billion' figure. **$6,497,653,512; +65.5% over counterfactual** (IMM 2027/28 Part A (Jan 5, 2026), pp. 3, 5-6 and Table 1)
- [primary] IMM cumulative, three auctions (2025/26 + 2026/27 + 2027/28), all data center load. Above-embedded only total: $21,258,681,955. This is the secondhand '$23.1B over three auctions'. **$23,100,955,341** (IMM 2027/28 Part A, p. 3 Table 1, p. 5, p. 8)
- [primary] IMM, 2028/2029 BRA, all data center load (Scenario 4, 21,470 MW). Actual revenues $16,407,949,025; counterfactual $10,140,129,902. This is the secondhand '$6.3B (38%) of $16.4B'. **$6,267,819,123; +61.8% over counterfactual; 38.2% of actual** (IMM 2028/29 Part A (Oct 30, 2026), pp. 3, 5, 7, 21 and Table 11 (p. 24))
- [primary] IMM, 2028/2029 BRA, forecast data center load only (Scenario 3, 16,436 MW above embedded; existing load of 5,034 MW left in). Counterfactual revenues $10,675,483,688. This is the secondhand '$5.73B of $16.4B'. It is the same auction as the $6.3B figure, a narrower definition of data center load. **$5,732,465,337; +53.7% over counterfactual; 34.9% of actual** (IMM 2028/29 Part A, pp. 3, 7, 21 and Table 11 (p. 24))
- [primary] IMM cumulative, four auctions (2025/26 through 2028/29), all data center load. The four actual-revenue totals I read sum to $63,630,945,497, so the share is about 46.2% (my arithmetic; press reported $29.4B of $63.6B, 46%). **$29,368,774,465** (IMM 2028/29 Part A, p. 3 Table 1, p. 5, p. 8)
- [primary] IMM, 2028/2029 BRA on the uncapped (alternate) VRR curve (Scenario 6 vs Scenario 1). Uncapped revenues would have been $29,722,226,243 (the price collar cut actual revenues by $13,314,277,218, 44.8%). **$22,376,528,315; +304.6%** (IMM 2028/29 Part A, pp. 2, 6-8, 22 and Table 12 (p. 24))
- [primary] IMM: capacity cleared short of the reliability requirement, which the IMM attributes to forecast data center load. **208.7 MW UCAP short (2026/27); 6,516.6 MW (2027/28); 6,831.3 MW (2028/29)** (IMM 2028/29 Part A, p. 2 and Table 2 (p. 4))
- [secondhand] Press reports that the IMM first published the $6.3B (38%) figure for 2028/29 in July 2026, before the Part A report. I did not find or open a July 2026 IMM document; only the October 30, 2026 Part A was read. **$6.3B, 38% of $16.4B** (secondary coverage dated July 20-23, 2026)

**Heterogeneity reported:** E3: by region only in narrative form (PJM, ERCOT, Georgia, Arizona, Missouri overviews); it repeats that PJM is the exception to the pattern of load growth coinciding with flat or falling retail prices, and that retail customers in Pennsylvania and Maryland saw the largest increases in part from PJM capacity prices while Dominion customers saw a minor impact (p. 21, citing CRA). No heterogeneity by customer class of its own. IMM: by scenario rather than by geography in the pages read: (i) all data center load vs forecast-only; (ii) actual price-collared VRR curve vs uncapped curve vs IMM-proposed curve. The price floor matters a great deal: it cut the measured data center effect by $3,748,370,053 in 2026/27 (26/27 Part A p. 3), $3,036,924,737 in 2027/28 (27/28 Part A p. 6) and $2,794,431,974 in 2028/29 (28/29 Part A p. 6). The IMM says its scenarios account for the locational (LDA) distribution of data center load, but I did not see LDA-level dollar results in the pages read. Neither source reports residential-class or state-level bill effects.

**Limitations the authors state:** E3 states: few quantitative studies exist and many rely on correlation; the Bates White comparison does not control for confounders (p. 15); its Amazon-site projections evaluate facilities in isolation without modeling system changes (fn 29); its PJM decomposition is a simplified reconstruction with structured assumptions that does not reflect unit-level bidding or dynamic responses (fn 66); the tariff list may not capture all tariffs (p. 10); risk-mitigation tools are largely untested at scale (p. 14); and risk of cost shifting 'is not absent' (p. 2). IMM states: results of individual scenarios are not strictly additive; quantitative results are estimates, correct as to direction and order of magnitude; results are not forecasts of future auctions; without data center load PJM would still have eventually reached tight conditions, more slowly; and the price floor understates the data center effect in 2026/27 onward. The IMM does not state a limitation about holding supply offers fixed.

**What a skeptical referee would raise:** IMM attribution: (1) It is a static re-clearing with supply offers held fixed. Removing 8-21 GW of demand would in reality change retirements, new entry and offer behavior, so the counterfactual revenue is a lower bound on what a no-data-center world would have cost and the dollar attribution is an upper-end figure. (2) The headline 'all data center load' scenario removes existing data centers too (7,927 MW in 2025/26, of which 3,273 MW already existed), which answers 'what if there were no data centers' rather than 'what did growth add'. The forecast-only figures ($7.74B, $7.27B, $6.24B, $5.73B) are the more defensible growth measure. (3) The VRR curve is steep, so results are highly nonlinear in MW: 2026/27 gives the same dollar effect for 7,892 MW and 11,993 MW because the floor binds, and the uncapped-curve versions are roughly two to three and a half times larger. The reported number is as much a function of the administrative price collar as of data centers. (4) 'RPM revenues' are paid by all load, data centers included. The 2027/28 report calls the figure an increase in costs to 'other PJM customers'; the 2028/29 report drops 'other'. The share actually borne by non-data-center customers is smaller than the headline and is not computed. (5) It is wholesale BRA revenue only. It excludes FRR areas and says nothing about pass-through to residential bills, which depends on state default-service procurement, hedging and retail rate design. It cannot be read as a residential bill effect. (6) Data center MW are utility-submitted forecasts the IMM itself calls extremely uncertain; the attribution is to forecast load, much of it not yet built. (7) No uncertainty range, no posted code, not reproducible. (8) The IMM is an advocate for a specific remedy, and the reports attribute essentially all tightness to data centers while setting aside accreditation changes, reliability-requirement changes and retirements that E3 and PJM weight heavily. E3: (1) Industry-funded with sponsor pre-publication review; two of the eleven studies are its own, one of them paid for by Amazon. (2) The central claim of no evidence of cost shifting is about cost-of-service allocation, not about whether data center demand raises the prices everyone pays; the paper tends to treat absence of the first as reassurance on the second. (3) The ~50% PJM split comes from an undisclosed simplified model. Shapley averaging of a nonlinear system is one convention among several, and the split depends on how the five drivers are defined: load is 121 of 241 net (50%) but 121 of 292 gross upward dollars (41%), and 'program design' changes were themselves partly responses to load. (4) It decomposes a year-over-year price change driven by a 3.2 GW forecast increase, while the IMM removes 7,927 MW of level load; the two are answers to different questions and E3 does not reconcile them or report the IMM's dollar figures. (5) The ~93% data-center share E3 applies to the 2025/26 load growth appears to come from the IMM's statement about 2026 large-load adjustments in the 2025 forecast (7,892 of 8,453 MW); I am uncertain it applies to the 3.2 GW in question. (6) The state scatter (Bates White) and LBNL correlations are unconditional and at state level, where data center load is concentrated in a few utilities; E3 concedes this. (7) The one causal study reviewed (Georgia Tech) finds price increases, and E3 spends more space on its limitations than on any other study's. (8) The 38-tariff count mixes general tariffs with about a dozen single-customer contracts (seven ComEd transmission service agreements, Entergy-Meta, DTE-Green Chile, TEP-Humphrey's Peak, an Entergy Arkansas special contract), counts adoption rather than performance, and comes from a commercial database. Figure 3 appears to show about 37 tariffs with minimum demand payments by 2028 while Appendix A.2 shows 34 of 38, a discrepancy I could only read approximately from the chart.

**What it would take to reproduce:** IMM numbers cannot be reproduced exactly from public data: the re-clearing uses actual confidential supply offers and the IMM's auction-clearing model; no code or scenario data is posted, only the PDF reports. What is public: PJM BRA reports and planning parameters (VRR curve points by LDA, reliability requirement, FPR, cleared MW and prices), PJM load forecast reports and large-load adjustment summaries (data center MW by year), and the IMM tables of VRR coordinates. An approximate replication would shift the VRR curve left by data center MW times the forecast pool requirement and intersect it with an estimated supply curve; the result is very sensitive to the assumed supply slope near the clearing point, so expect a wide range rather than the IMM's point values. I am uncertain whether PJM's posted aggregate supply-curve data are detailed enough for this. The simple arithmetic can be reproduced directly: per-auction deltas, shares of actual revenue and the cumulative totals follow from the report tables. E3's ~50% split: no code or data posted; the method is described only in footnote 66 and labelled proprietary. It is in principle reproducible from public PJM parameters with one's own assumptions, but the Shapley averaging and 'structured assumptions' are not specified well enough to recover the $121/$89/$82 split. The tariff count can be re-counted from Appendix A.2 (38 rows), but the underlying Halcyon database is commercial.

**Corrections to what we had heard:** 1. The conflicting '$6.3B or $5.73B of $16.4B' are both from the 2028/2029 auction (held June 30-July 7, 2026) and both are in the IMM's October 30, 2026 Part A report. $6,267,819,123 (38.2% of actual revenue) removes all data center load, 21,470 MW; $5,732,465,337 (34.9%) removes only forecast growth, 16,436 MW. They are not competing estimates. 2. '$16.4B' is ambiguous: actual revenues were $16,411,578,225 in 2027/28 and $16,407,949,025 in 2028/29. The 2027/28 data center figure is $6,497,653,512 (Bloomberg's '$6.5B'), a different number from the $6.3B. 3. '$9.3B (63%) for 2025/26' is right on dollars ($9,332,103,858), but 63.5% is that amount as a share of actual revenue; the IMM's lead statement is a 174.3% increase over the no-data-center counterfactual. It is also not 63% 'of the price increase'. 4. '$23.1B over three auctions' is correct as of the January 5, 2026 report ($23,100,955,341) but is superseded: the four-auction total is $29,368,774,465. 5. E3's 'PJM capacity attribution' is E3's own ~50% figure for the price change between 2024/25 and 2025/26; E3 does not report the IMM's $9.3B anywhere, and the IMM document in E3's list of 11 is the 2026/27 Part A, not the 2025/26 Part G. 6. The 11 studies are confirmed, but one (UCS Connection Costs) is labelled by E3 itself as a policy brief that is qualitative with quantitative evidence, and two are E3's own work. 7. Apparent errors inside the primary documents: in the IMM 2028/29 Part A Table 1 (p. 3) the 'Above Embedded' total is printed as $21,258,681,955, which is the three-auction sum; adding the 2028/29 row gives $26,991,147,292 (my arithmetic, read from the page image). The same report cites a State of the Market report dated November 12, 2026, after its own October 30 date. E3's appendix dates the CRA report September 2025 while its footnote 51 dates it February 2, 2026.

## Angela Ryu and Shon R. Hiatt. "Data Center Entry and U.S. Electricity Prices: From the Wholesale Market to the Power Bill." Cover labelled "Energy Brief", USC Marshall, Zage Business of Energy Initiative. Title page dated May 22, 2026. No series number and no version or revision marker printed. Individual author affiliations are not printed; the only institutional attribution is the closing line on p. 24: "This report was prepared by Shon R. Hiatt and Angela Ryu for the USC Marshall Zage Business of Energy Initiative." 26 PDF pages (unnumbered cover, printed pages 1-24 to the end of Table A4b, unnumbered back cover). Not labelled peer reviewed or a working paper. It calls itself a brief and a report; the disclaimer on p. 24 says energy briefs are meant to deepen students' comprehension and are not endorsements or sources of primary data.

- **Link:** https://drive.google.com/file/d/1IWolyGYxOv5ENI3frcRU8U0Mge_fbWUr/view
- **How far we got:** read in full. PDF downloaded by hand by the project owner on 2026-11-11 and read locally. Read all 26 PDF pages: the complete text extraction (body, 24 endnotes, references, Appendix A1-A4) plus the page-by-page text to fix page numbers, and rendered pages 1-2 (cover, executive summary), 9-13 (Figures 2, 3, 4, 5 and the surrounding text) and 26 (back cover, no content). Figure 1 (schematic) and Figure 6 (NERC reserve margins) were read by caption and text only. Appendix Tables A1-A4b were read from the text extraction, which was unambiguous. Page numbers below are the printed page numbers, which sit at the top of each page. Printed page = PDF page minus 1 (the cover is unnumbered). Skipped: nothing. No instruction-like text aimed at a reader was found in the document.
- **Funding or affiliation:** Prepared 'for the USC Marshall Zage Business of Energy Initiative' (p. 24, closing line; logo on every page). No funding statement, conflict-of-interest disclosure, acknowledgments, or author affiliation lines are printed. Data providers named: S&P Global 451 Research (facility data), GridStatus (LMPs), EIA (EIA-861, 861-M, 860, 923, Henry Hub), HIFLD (service territories), NERC (reserve margins), LBNL / Wiser et al. 2025 (RPS controls). The PDF file metadata shows a template title 'Name of Energy Brief Goes here' and a different author name; I note this only as a sign that the file was produced from a template, and it is not part of the printed document.
- **Unit:** Three separate panels. (1) Retail: utility-state-year, 16,163 observations, 1,798 utilities, 51 jurisdictions, 2015-2024, unbalanced; utilities with fewer than 3 years dropped (Sec. 2.2, p. 5; Table A3, p. 22). Table A3 splits this into 13,993 observations and 1,688 utilities for single-state utilities and 2,170 observations and 110 utilities for multi-state utilities. Ownership subsamples in the primary window: cooperative N=7,203, municipal N=6,384, investor-owned N=1,858 (Table A4b, p. 24). Through 2025: N=16,556 pooled (Table A4a). The paper does NOT state how many of the 1,798 utilities, or how many observations, have any data center capacity (nonzero DC MW). (2) CAISO wholesale: node-month panel, 319 unique nodes, 365 California facilities mapped to nodes, 77 nodes that are the closest match to at least one facility; first-entry sample has 40 treated nodes (27 with no matched pre-2020 data center, 13 with at least one); continuous-MW check uses 96 treated nodes, N=1,695 (Sec. 2.2 p. 4; Sec. 4.1 pp. 8-9). Months sampled: January, April and July of each year. (3) Non-CAISO wholesale: load-zone-month panel, 55 load zones (23 PJM, 18 SPP, 10 MISO, 4 ERCOT), 58 entry cohorts (a cohort is a zone and entry year), N=344 for Total LMP in the primary specification (Sec. 2.2 p. 5; Table A1 p. 20).
- **Years:** Retail: primary window 2015-2024 using annual EIA-861 (Sec. 2.2 p. 5; fn. 1 p. 16). A preliminary extension adds 2025 from Form EIA-861M, the monthly sample, giving 2015-2025 (Sec. 2.2 p. 5; Table A4a p. 24). LAST YEAR OF RETAIL DATA: 2024 for the primary pooled regression (Table A3 and A3a, A4a row A), for the primary ownership regressions (Table A4b col A), and for the commercial, industrial and total-price regressions whose coefficients are printed (Table A3a). 2025 enters ONLY in the 'extended' pooled row B of Table A4a (beta 0.0090, N=16,556), the 'through 2025' ownership column of Table A4b, the Fig. 4 red squares, and the bill map in Fig. 5. The 2025 data are a preliminary sample weighted toward large utilities: every IOU reports monthly but about one in six cooperatives and municipals do (fn. 12, p. 16); the 2025 cross-section has 309 utilities and 452 utility-state-year observations, adding 393 new observations (Sec. 2.2 p. 5). Fig. 5 states the 2024 residential rate is used because it is the most recent year with full annual EIA-861 coverage (p. 12). Facility inventory: S&P Global 451 Research, 'reflects updates as of Q2 2025' (Sec. 2.1, p. 4). Wholesale: 2020-2025 (January, April, July each year); CAISO entry events 2021-2024 only because both one year before and one year after must be observable (Sec. 3.1, p. 6); non-CAISO cohorts span entry years 2021-2024 (Table A2, p. 21). Treatment cohorts in the retail design are not defined because the retail treatment is continuous.
- **Outcome:** Retail: Form EIA-861 annual utility revenue and sales by customer class, reported separately for each state of operation; price = revenue divided by sales (cents per kWh) within each utility-state-year, computed for residential, commercial, industrial and total (Sec. 2.2, p. 5). The dependent variable is ln(price) (Sec. 3.3, p. 7). The paper does not say whether prices are nominal or real; year fixed effects are included, so a common deflator would not change the coefficients, but it is 'not stated'. Retail choice: the sample is restricted to 'bundled-service utilities reporting ownership-type data' (p. 5). The paper does not explain how delivery-only revenue is treated, whether a utility reporting both bundled and delivery service enters, or how restructured-state default-service prices are handled; no restructured versus vertically integrated split is reported. About 560 Short Form (861-S) utilities, mostly small co-ops and municipals, are excluded because they lack class detail; fn. 10 (p. 16) says they have median sales of about 3,500 MWh, about 360 customers each, about 0.05 percent of national sales. 315 observations (1.9 percent) are dropped for missing residential price (fn. 11, p. 16). 2025 prices are summed monthly revenue and sales from EIA-861M recomputed as 100 x revenue/sales (Table A4a text, p. 24). Wholesale: GridStatus five-minute LMPs with energy, congestion and loss components, aggregated to monthly medians, then to load zones using published node-to-zone mappings (Sec. 2.2, pp. 4-5); levels in $/MWh, not logs. Dollar conversions of retail estimates (e.g. $1.04-$1.10/MWh) are the authors' own: beta x ln(2) x an illustrative residential price of $0.16-$0.17/kWh (fn. 2, p. 16, EIA Electric Power Monthly Table 5.3). No usage, kWh sales, customer count, bill or utility-cost outcome is estimated. Water: no water rates or water bills appear; 'water' occurs only as a siting factor in fn. 15 (p. 17).
- **Data center data:** S&P Global 451 Research data center database, 'reflects updates as of Q2 2025'. It 'records facility-level utility power capacity (kW), operational status, build year, and coordinates for operational hyperscale and colocation facilities, excluding cryptocurrency miners, powered shells, and telecom facilities' (Sec. 2.1, p. 4). Commercial vendor; not stated whether it is publicly available. The paper does not state the total number of facilities, the number matched to retail utilities, the number operational by year, or how capacity is defined beyond 'utility power capacity (kW)' (whether contracted, design or measured is not stated). The only facility counts given are for California: 365 facilities mapped to 319 CAISO nodes (p. 4). The authors report no validation of dates or capacities against another inventory. Cryptocurrency mines are excluded. Because the inventory is as of Q2 2025 while the retail panel runs through 2025, capacity added after Q2 2025 would not be captured in the 2025 extension (my inference, not stated by the authors). Wholesale geography inputs: CAISO node coordinates from an open-source LMP Location Scraper (fn. 7, p. 16); zone shapefiles from public ArcGIS endpoints with cross-reference match rates of 94 percent in PJM, 88 percent in MISO, 86 percent in ERCOT and 80 percent in SPP (fn. 8, p. 16).
- **Treatment:** Retail: continuous. Treatment is ln(DC_MW + 1), where DC MW is the cumulative operating data center capacity, in megawatts, in the utility's service territory in that year (Sec. 3.3, p. 7; Table A3 note, p. 22). Text at Sec. 2.1 (p. 4) says the treatment variable is 'the sum of operating capacity (in MW) of all matched facilities in a utility's service territory in a given year'. 'Doubling' is the authors' way of reading the log coefficient: a 100 percent increase in operating capacity changes price by about beta times ln(2) (Sec. 4.3, p. 10, 'the 100 percent increase in operating capacity is associated with about a 0.65 percent increase'). For a utility with zero capacity, ln(0+1)=0, so the first MW is a move from 0 to ln(2) on the regressor; the paper does not discuss what 'doubling' means for utilities starting from zero. Start dates: the paper says the database records 'build year' and 'operational status' (p. 4); how a facility's capacity is phased in across years, whether partial years are counted, and how pre-2015 capacity enters are not stated. Facility-to-utility link: facilities are point-in-polygon matched to the HIFLD electric retail service territory shapefile (fn. 6, p. 16 gives the ArcGIS item link; HIFLD vintage not stated). Because polygons overlap (investor-owned envelopes extend over rural areas actually served by cooperatives or municipals as carve-outs), 'a facility was matched to the utility whose polygon strictly contains the facility's location, prioritizing retail providers over wholesale-only territories' (p. 4). The paper does not say how ties are resolved when more than one polygon still contains the point, and reports no match rate for the retail link (match rates in fn. 8, p. 16 are for wholesale zone shapefiles only). The link is therefore to a territory polygon, not to metered service of the facility. Multi-state utilities carry identical utility-level capacity into each state observation (p. 7). Wholesale CAISO: each facility is matched to its five nearest CAISO LMP nodes by geodesic distance with its MW allocated across nodes by inverse-distance weights; a node is post-entry in a year if any matched facility is operational at that node by then; binary treatment; sample restricted to nodes with at least one entry during the window (Sec. 3.1, p. 6). Wholesale non-CAISO: facilities assigned to zones by published zone shapefiles; binary post-entry indicator for the year after the zone's first within-panel entry; one cohort per zone-entry-year (Sec. 3.2, pp. 6-7). The authors stress the entry is 'first within panel', not first ever, because all four non-CAISO RTOs hosted data centers before 2020 (pp. 3, 6, 15). Median entry event is 59 MW, range 2 to 1,886 MW (fn. 17, p. 17).
- **Method:** Retail: two-way fixed effects, ln(Price_ut) = beta ln(DC_MW_ut + 1) + X_ut gamma + utility FE + year FE + error (Sec. 3.3, p. 7). Controls X: gas share and wind+solar share of generation (EIA-923; for the roughly 75 percent of utilities that do not generate, state-year shares are substituted), ln(coal+nuclear retired MW + 1) (EIA-860), RPS incremental generation and RPS cost intensity (state-year, from Wiser et al. 2025; zero if no RPS), and the anticipated reserve margin (NERC summer assessment, by assessment area and year) (Sec. 2.3, pp. 5-6; Table A3). Standard errors clustered by state (51 clusters; Table A3). Levels or logs: log price, log (capacity+1). Comparison: within-utility change over time against all utilities, with no never-treated or not-yet-treated group and no event window. Ownership: separate beta for cooperative, municipal and investor-owned (about 96 percent of 2024 customers); the remaining roughly 4 percent (political subdivisions, state, federal) enter the pooled regression but not the ownership betas (p. 7). The Ns in Table A4b (7,203, 6,384, 1,858) are consistent with separate subsample regressions but the text does not say so explicitly. Robustness shown: utility-state FE (beta 0.0091), single-state only (0.0096), multi-state only (0.0076) (Table A3); no-controls column (0.0095) (Table A4a); Henry Hub x state-mean-gas-share interaction (0.0095) (Table A3a). No event study, no pre-trend or lead test, and no design aimed at heterogeneous treatment effects for the retail panel. Authors state it is a 'conditional association', and that 'unobserved utility-specific trends correlated with data center growth would bias beta upward' (fn. 18-19, p. 17). CAISO: stacked DiD, node x month-of-year FE and entry-year FE, SEs clustered by node; first-entry versus all-entry specifications; comparison is not-yet-treated nodes within the entry window; pre-trends 'not directly testable through pre-trend leads' because the pre-period is collapsed into one indicator (fn. 15-16, pp. 17-18). Non-CAISO zones: 2x2 stacked DiD on relative year -1 and +1, zone, month and entry-year FE, monthly Henry Hub control, SEs clustered by cohort (58 clusters); supply-side controls are omitted because they are RTO-year measures (Sec. 3.2, pp. 6-7). No pre-trend check is reported for the zone design either.

**Headline findings**

- [primary] Residential price, pooled, primary window 2015-2024: coefficient on ln(DC MW + 1) in a regression of ln(residential price). Text translates this to about 0.65 percent per doubling of operating capacity (my check: 0.0094 x ln 2 = 0.65 percent). **0.0094*** (SE 0.0026); N=16,163; 1,798 utilities; within R2 0.013; table prints 0.651 percent per doubling** (Table A3 col. 1, p. 22; Table A4a row A, p. 24; Sec. 4.3, p. 10)
- [primary] Residential price, pooled, extended through 2025 using preliminary EIA-861M data for the 2025 year **0.0090** (SE 0.0027); N=16,556; table prints 0.624 percent per doubling; no-controls version 0.0092** (0.0028). Text: about 0.62 percent, $1.00-$1.06/MWh** (Table A4a row B, p. 24; Sec. 4.3, p. 10)
- [primary] Authors' conversion to $/MWh (the authors' own arithmetic at an illustrative residential price of $0.16-$0.17/kWh; not a separate estimate). Executive summary pairs 0.65 percent with $1.00-$1.06/MWh, while Sec. 1, Sec. 4.3 and the conclusion pair 0.65 percent with $1.04-$1.10/MWh and 0.62 percent with $1.00-$1.06/MWh. The paper never states a dollars-per-month figure for the pooled estimate. My own arithmetic at the paper's 0.9 MWh/month: about $0.94-$0.99 per month for the 0.65 percent case. **$1.04-$1.10/MWh (0.65 percent); $1.00-$1.06/MWh (0.62 percent); Fig. 5 map scale runs from $0.05/month to $6.30/month for individual utility territories** (Exec. summary p. 1; Sec. 1 p. 4; Sec. 4.3 p. 10; Conclusion pp. 15-16; Fig. 5 p. 12)
- [primary] Commercial, industrial and total price, pooled, 2015-2024, none distinguishable from zero **Commercial 0.0043 (0.0060), N=16,118; Industrial 0.0017 (0.0084), N=13,779; Total 0.0020 (0.0045), N=16,163. For 2025 the paper says only that comparable models give 'small, statistically insignificant coefficients' with no numbers.** (Table A3a Panel A col. 1, p. 23; Table A4a note, p. 24)
- [primary] Residential price by ownership, 2015-2024 (primary window), beta on ln(DC MW + 1) **Cooperative 0.0067** (0.0024), N=7,203; Municipal 0.0080 with dagger p<0.10 (0.0044), N=6,384; Investor-owned 0.0006 (0.0043), N=1,858. Text $/MWh: cooperative $0.74-$0.79, municipal $0.89-$0.94, IOU $0.07-$0.08** (Table A4b col. A, p. 24; Table A3a Panel B col. 1, p. 23; Sec. 4.3, p. 10; Fig. 4, p. 11)
- [primary] Residential price by ownership, extended through 2025 (preliminary) **Cooperative 0.0060* (0.0026), N=7,320; Municipal 0.0091* (0.0044), N=6,454; Investor-owned 0.0001 (0.0043), N=2,036. Text $/MWh: municipal $1.01-$1.07, cooperative $0.67-$0.71. Exec. summary percentages for a doubling: cooperative about 0.42 percent, municipal 0.63 percent (my check: 0.0060 x ln 2 = 0.42, 0.0091 x ln 2 = 0.63)** (Table A4b col. B, p. 24; Sec. 4.3, p. 10; Exec. summary, p. 1)
- [primary] Share of customers by ownership, from 2024 EIA-861 as cited by the authors **IOUs about 68 percent of retail customers (Sec. 4.3, p. 10) but the executive summary says 68 percent 'of the U.S. population' (p. 1); cooperatives about 16 percent; municipals about 11 percent; the three ownership groups together about 96 percent** (Sec. 4.3 p. 10; Exec. summary p. 1; Sec. 3.3 p. 7)
- [primary] Sensitivity of pooled residential beta to fixed effects and sample **Utility-state FE 0.0091*** (0.0024); single-state utilities 0.0096** (0.0035), N=13,993, 1,688 utilities; multi-state utilities 0.0076* (0.0033), N=2,170, 110 utilities; with Henry Hub x state gas share 0.0095*** (0.0026); no controls 0.0095*** (0.0026)** (Table A3 cols. 2-4, p. 22; Table A3a Panel A col. 2, p. 23; Table A4a, p. 24)
