- [primary] Ownership results with the Henry Hub x state gas share control **Cooperative 0.0068** (0.0024); Municipal 0.0080 dagger (0.0043); Investor-owned 0.0007 (0.0042)** (Table A3a Panel B col. 2, p. 23)
- [primary] CAISO node congestion component, first-entry specification **+$2.30/MWh, p<0.01 (SE not printed in text or figure; read from Fig. 2 panel A with the label '+2.30 **'); Total LMP -2.29 n.s.; energy -4.76 n.s. (text says -$4.77); loss +0.17 n.s.** (Sec. 4.1, p. 8; Fig. 2 panel A, p. 8)
- [primary] CAISO node congestion component, all-entry specification **+$1.45/MWh, p<0.001; Total LMP -4.54 n.s.; energy -5.79 n.s.; loss -0.21 dagger** (Sec. 4.1, p. 8; Fig. 2 panel B, p. 8)
- [primary] CAISO first-entry result split by pre-2020 exposure of the node **27 nodes with no matched pre-2020 data center: +$0.97/MWh, p=0.20; 13 already-exposed nodes: +$5.07/MWh, p<0.01; 40 treated nodes in all** (Sec. 4.1, p. 8)
- [primary] CAISO continuous-MW check, congestion per MW of cumulative operating capacity **+$0.0195/MWh per MW, p<0.001; 96 treated nodes; N=1,695; at the mean 12.8 MW per treated node-month implies about $0.25/MWh (authors), far below $2.30** (Sec. 4.1, pp. 8-9)
- [primary] Non-CAISO zone-level stacked DiD, primary specification (all 58 cohorts, Henry Hub control): Total LMP and energy component; congestion and loss null **Total LMP +3.44** (1.18); Energy +5.43*** (1.16); Congestion -1.00 (0.71); Loss -0.03 (0.13); N=344 for Total LMP; 58 cohorts; Henry Hub coefficient +$8.39/MWh per $1/MMBtu** (Table A1 col. 3, p. 20; Fig. 3 panel A, p. 9; Sec. 4.2, p. 9)
- [primary] The same zone design without the Henry Hub control and with different cohort sets **Pooled, no HH: Total LMP +5.75 (3.56), not significant; excluding 2023 and no HH: +16.49*** (2.84), N=266, 45 cohorts; excluding 2023 with HH: +4.97*** (1.25). Energy pooled no HH: +8.58* (4.00)** (Table A1 cols. 1-2, p. 20; Table A2 Panels A-B, p. 21)
- [primary] Zone design, single-cohort and leave-one-out dispersion (Total LMP with HH control) **Leave-one-out +2.93 to +4.97 (Total LMP) and +4.52 to +8.21 (energy); single-year cohorts: 2021 +8.67 (11.48), 2022 +1.61 (1.96), 2023 -2.81 (7.50), 2024 +12.93* (4.91). Without HH: 2023 -30.86***, 2021 +40.58***** (Table A2 Panel A, p. 21; Fig. 3 panel B, p. 9)
- [primary] Zone design, entry size: small (<59 MW) versus large (>=59 MW) entries, and continuous-MW slope **Small: Total LMP +3.75** (1.29), energy +4.42** (1.43), 29 cohorts, N=173. Large: Total LMP +2.91 (1.87), energy +6.48** (1.86), 29 cohorts, N=171. Per-MW slope: Total LMP +0.0063* (0.0030), energy +0.0064** (0.0023); authors say this implies about +$1.21/MWh at the mean entry size versus +$3.44 binary** (Table A2 Panels C-D, p. 21; Conclusion, p. 16)
- [primary] Model-implied historical monthly residential bill impact map. The authors state it is 'not a separate estimate or a forecast', it applies the 2025 ownership coefficients to each utility's observed 2015-2025 change in ln(DC_MW + 1), the 2024 residential rate and 0.9 MWh/month; concentrated in rural Texas and the Tennessee Valley; IOUs shown faintly **Color scale +$0.05/month to +$6.30/month by utility territory; no aggregate national dollar total is stated** (Fig. 5 and caption, pp. 12; Sec. 4.3 p. 11-12)
- [primary] Authors' own forward-looking context (not estimated): NERC LTRA 2025 projects reserve margins falling below reference levels in PJM from 2028 (-12.4 percentage points by 2030) and MISO (-4.2 points by 2030); the authors say they do not estimate the compounding with scarcity pricing **Not an estimate by the authors; figures are from NERC as quoted** (Sec. 5, pp. 13-14)

**Heterogeneity reported:** By ownership (the main split): cooperative, municipal, investor-owned, in both the 2015-2024 and 2015-2025 windows, with betas and SEs in Table A4b (p. 24), Table A3a Panel B (p. 23) and Fig. 4 (p. 11). Cooperative 0.0067** (0.0024) and municipal 0.0080 dagger (0.0044) versus IOU 0.0006 (0.0043) in 2015-2024; the municipal estimate is significant only at the 10 percent level in the primary window and at 5 percent in the 2025 extension. No test of equality across ownership groups is reported. By customer class: residential versus commercial, industrial, total (Table A3a, p. 23). By multi-state versus single-state utilities (Table A3, p. 22). Size: the text says the retail association 'is strongest among smaller utilities' (p. 4) and that effects concentrate in smaller utility systems (p. 11), but no table or figure by utility size appears; this claim is not backed by a printed estimate. Region and market: Fig. 5 shows implied bill impacts concentrated in rural Texas and the Tennessee Valley, Tennessee and Kentucky, but that is an application of the coefficients, not an estimated regional split. Wholesale: by pre-2020 exposure of the CAISO node (+$0.97, p=0.20, versus +$5.07, p<0.01; p. 8); by zone-entry cohort year (Table A2 Panels A-B, p. 21); by entry size at 59 MW (Table A2 Panel C). Not reported: retail-choice versus vertically integrated states, load size relative to the utility, spare capacity or utilization (reserve margin enters only as a control), hyperscaler versus colocation, early versus recent years for retail, or a PJM versus ERCOT versus MISO versus SPP split of the wholesale effect.

**Limitations the authors state:** (1) Focus on recent years, not on what happens as the announced pipeline materializes; estimates are 'best interpreted as recent entry effects in markets with varying degrees of prior data-center exposure' (Exec. summary p. 1; Conclusion p. 15). (2) Wholesale designs identify first-within-panel entry, not first-ever entry, because all non-CAISO RTOs and some CAISO nodes hosted data centers before 2020 (pp. 3, 15, and Sec. 3.1-3.2, pp. 6-7). (3) Retail estimates are 'conditional within-utility associations rather than definitive causal pass-through effects' (p. 10); beta is 'a conditional association' and 'unobserved utility-specific trends correlated with data center growth would bias beta upward' (fn. 18-19, p. 17). (4) The wholesale CAISO identifying assumption is not directly testable through pre-trend leads and node-specific time-varying shocks are not controlled (fn. 15-16, pp. 17-18). (5) Binary wholesale coefficients are per-event effects, not per-MW and not to be extrapolated to future GW additions; the continuous specification gives much smaller implied effects (p. 7; Sec. 4.1-4.2, pp. 9-10; Conclusion p. 16). (6) Multi-state utilities carry identical capacity into each state, so observations are 'not strictly independent' (p. 7). (7) 2025 data are preliminary, sampled, weighted toward large utilities and subject to revision (fn. 1, p. 16; fn. 12). (8) The retail estimate 'captures only the share of any wholesale price increase that survives pass-through within a single year' and likely understates longer-run burden; longer-horizon pass-through is left to future work (p. 16). (9) The bill map is illustrative, not a complete accounting (Fig. 5 caption, p. 12). (10) Supply-side controls are omitted from the zone design because they are RTO-year measures (p. 7). (11) Treatment is a continuous, non-random siting outcome; the authors argue (fn. 15, p. 18) siting is driven by land, water, fiber and interconnection availability, which is an assertion without a test.

**What a skeptical referee would raise:** Items marked [derived] are my own arithmetic from printed numbers. 1. Treated sample not reported. The paper never says how many utilities or utility-state-years have any data center capacity, so the reader cannot tell whether the 0.0094 comes from tens or hundreds of utilities. Identification is within-utility variation in ln(MW+1) with 51 state clusters (Table A3). The facility count and a distribution of capacity are also absent. 2. The IOU null is imprecise, and the contrast is untested. IOU beta 0.0006 with SE 0.0043 gives a 95 percent interval of about -0.0078 to +0.0090 [derived], which includes the pooled estimate 0.0094 almost exactly at its upper end. Municipal 0.0080 with SE 0.0044 is only p<0.10 in the primary window, and its interval [derived: about -0.0006 to +0.0166] contains the IOU point estimate. The paper reports no equality test between ownership groups; the headline 'no significant effect on IOUs' is a failure to reject, with only 1,858 IOU observations. Fig. 4 shows overlapping intervals (p. 11). 3. Facility-to-utility link is territory containment, not service. HIFLD polygons overlap; the rule of strict containment and preferring retail over wholesale-only (p. 4) is not validated and no match rate is given for the retail link. A cooperative or municipal carved out of an IOU envelope, or a facility served by a different utility than the polygon owner, is misassigned. This could produce or mask the ownership gradient, since small co-ops and municipals are the units most exposed to carve-out ambiguity. The paper also does not discuss that many co-ops and municipals buy wholesale power through other entities. 4. Timing and capacity are unvalidated. The S&P 451 Research file gives 'build year' and kW capacity; there is no cross-check against another inventory, no statement of how capacity ramps by year, and the inventory is dated Q2 2025 while the 2025 extension runs to December 2025 (pp. 4-5). 5. Retail choice is unaddressed. The sample is 'bundled-service utilities' (p. 5) but how delivery-only revenue and restructured-state default-service prices enter is not described, and there is no restructured versus vertically integrated split, although the largest implied bill effects in Fig. 5 are in rural Texas (p. 12), where retail competition rules differ. 6. Average revenue per kWh is not a bill or a rate. No usage, sales or customer outcome is shown, so a rise in price from a change in load composition or fixed-cost spreading is not distinguished. 7. No pre-trends and no event study in the retail panel. The design is two-way fixed effects with a continuous treatment; there is no test of leads, no region-by-year effects, and the authors themselves say unobserved utility trends would bias beta upward (fn. 18-19, p. 17). Within R2 is 0.013 (Table A3). Data center growth and price rises in the same regions after 2020 (rural Texas, Tennessee Valley) could be common regional cost shocks. Reserve margin and fuel mix are included as controls; they could be channels rather than confounders, and the paper does not discuss that. 8. Functional form. ln(MW+1) with MW in megawatts means the elasticity is dominated by small values, and a 'doubling' from zero is undefined; the authors' own wholesale results show the binary and continuous specifications disagree by a factor of two to nine (CAISO $2.30 versus about $0.25; zones $3.44 versus $1.21 at the mean size; pp. 9, 16), which is a warning about scaling any single coefficient. 9. The 2025 extension changes the sample, not just the year. EIA-861M covers every IOU but about one in six cooperatives and municipals (fn. 12, p. 16), so the 2025 ownership coefficients come from a differently composed sample; the 'through 2025' municipal and cooperative estimates are not strictly comparable to the 2024 ones. The executive summary's co-op (0.42 percent) and municipal (0.63 percent) numbers come from this preliminary sample while the pooled 0.65 percent comes from the 2024 sample (pp. 1, 10). 10. Wholesale evidence is thin and fragile. Only January, April and July are sampled; the zone design compares one year before with one year after entry; the primary result needs the Henry Hub control and the inclusion of all cohorts (without it, pooled Total LMP is +5.75 with SE 3.56, Table A2 Panel A, p. 21). Single-cohort estimates swing from -2.81 to +12.93 (with HH) and from -30.86 to +40.58 (without), p. 21. The components should sum to the total, but +5.43 -1.00 -0.03 = +4.40 [derived] versus +3.44, and Table A1 reports N=344 for Total LMP but Table A2 reports N=284 for energy for the same specification (pp. 20-21); this is unexplained. Clustering by 58 cohorts when zones recur across cohorts and sampled months share shocks may overstate precision. The CAISO design assigns each facility to five nodes, so clusters at the node level are not independent; the authors' selection explanation for zero congestion in zones is untested. Control zones are 'not yet treated' zones in the same RTO, which share region-wide wholesale price movements. 11. The zone result is for a year after entry in zones already hosting data centers (pp. 3, 6, 15) and is not connected to the retail estimate except by narrative; the authors say the two are 'not directly comparable as point estimates' (p. 16). 12. Reporting inconsistencies a reader will trip on: $1.00-$1.06 paired with 0.65 percent in the executive summary versus $1.04-$1.10 elsewhere; 68 percent 'of the U.S. population' (p. 1) versus 'of customers' (p. 10); text -$4.77 versus Fig. 2 -4.76 for the CAISO first-entry energy estimate; footnote 1 points to Table A4a for the 2015-2024 ownership pattern, which is in Table A4b; the 'strongest among smaller utilities' claim (p. 4) has no supporting table; Fig. 4's caption mentions total price across classes that the figure does not show. 13. Many splits, no multiple-testing adjustment, no code or data, and a proprietary facility database.

**What it would take to reproduce:** No code, replication package, data-availability statement or posted dataset is mentioned anywhere in the PDF. Public inputs: EIA-861 and 861-M, EIA-860/923, Henry Hub, HIFLD territories (link in fn. 6), NERC assessments, LBNL RPS data, public wholesale data via GridStatus and zone shapefiles. The key proprietary input is the S&P Global 451 Research facility database (commercial; the paper does not say it can be obtained freely). Main retail specification in two sentences: regress ln(residential revenue/sales) on ln(cumulative operating data center MW in the utility's HIFLD territory + 1), gas share, wind+solar share, ln(coal and nuclear retired MW + 1), RPS incremental generation, RPS cost intensity and the NERC anticipated reserve margin, with utility and year fixed effects and state-clustered SEs, on 16,163 utility-state-years in 2015-2024 (1,798 utilities). Details a replicator would have to guess: nominal or real prices; the treatment of delivery-only revenue; what 'bundled-service utilities' means in practice; HIFLD vintage and tie-breaking; how capacity is phased in by build year; how many utilities have nonzero capacity; whether ownership betas come from separate regressions or interactions; the exact controls for 2025 (RPS carried forward from 2024).

**Corrections to what we had heard:** 1. Retail data are utility-level EIA-861, 2015-2025: PARTLY WRONG. Primary regressions use annual EIA-861 for 2015-2024 only (Sec. 2.2, p. 5; Table A3, A4a row A). 2025 comes from Form EIA-861M, a preliminary monthly sample weighted to large utilities, and enters only in the 'extended' row and column (Table A4a row B, Table A4b col. B, Fig. 4 red squares, Fig. 5) (pp. 5, 24; fn. 1 and 12, p. 16). The executive summary's 'retail data from 2015-2025' (p. 1) blurs this. 2. 0.62 percent and $1.00-$1.06/MWh per doubling: CONFIRMED but it is the 2025-extension figure (0.0090, Table A4a row B, p. 24; text p. 10). The paper's headline pooled figure is 0.65 percent (0.0094, 0.651 percent in Table A4a row A), with $1.04-$1.10/MWh in Sec. 1, 4.3 and the conclusion; the executive summary (p. 1) attaches $1.00-$1.06 to 0.65 percent, an internal inconsistency. 'Under $1 a month' is NOT in the paper; it is my arithmetic ($0.94-$0.99 at 0.9 MWh/month) and the authors give no pooled per-month figure. 3. Effect mainly in co-ops and municipals, no significant IOU effect, IOUs 68 percent: CONFIRMED (Table A4b, p. 24; Exec. summary p. 1; Sec. 4.3 p. 10). Caveats: municipal is only p<0.10 in the 2015-2024 window (0.0080, SE 0.0044), 68 percent is 'of the U.S. population' on p. 1 but 'of U.S. retail electricity customers' on p. 10, and IOU SE is 0.0043 so the IOU estimate is imprecise. 4. CAISO node congestion +$2.30 at first entry and +$1.45 across all entries: CONFIRMED (Sec. 4.1, p. 8; Fig. 2). First-entry means first within the 2020-2025 panel, not first ever; and the first-entry estimate rests on 40 treated nodes of which the 27 with no pre-2020 data center show +$0.97, p=0.20. 5. 55 zones show +$3.44 total and +$5.43 energy: CONFIRMED with correction. 55 is the number of load zones in the panel (Sec. 2.2, p. 5); the estimate is per entry event across 58 cohorts (Table A1 col. 3, p. 20), measured in the year after entry. Total LMP +3.44** (1.18), energy +5.43*** (1.16). The result depends on the Henry Hub control: pooled Total LMP without it is +5.75 (3.56), not significant (Table A2 Panel A, p. 21). 6. Retail estimates described as conditional associations, not causal: CONFIRMED (p. 10; fn. 18-19, p. 17). The wholesale designs are called stacked DiD and the paper says 'effects' there without a causal disclaimer, although fn. 15-16 (pp. 17-18) concede pre-trends cannot be tested. 7. Last year of retail data: 2024 in every regression whose coefficients are printed in the main tables, except the extended row B of Table A4a (N=16,556) and the 2025 ownership column of Table A4b, which add 2025 from EIA-861M. The 7 October 2025 EIA-861 release footnote does not appear in this document (the only 'October' in the text is a 2020 reference); I could not find a footnote about a 2015-2024 ownership sample either, other than the paper's own 2015-2024 window in Table A4b. 8. Full working paper or brief: it calls itself an 'Energy Brief' on the cover and 'this report' and 'this brief' in the text (pp. 4, 15, 22, 24). It does not refer to a longer version, working paper or forthcoming full paper; Appendix A1-A4 is the only supplementary material. It is 26 PDF pages including cover and back cover.

## Herman Lange, Sahil Bagwe, Aditya Murlidharan, Lothar Rojas (author order and spelling as printed on the title page; footnote on p. 1: all four are "MPA/ID students at the Harvard Kennedy School, Harvard University"; emails at hks.harvard.edu). "Shared Infrastructure, Shared Burdens? Data Centers and Electricity Price Incidence". SSRN abstract_id 6685418 (the SSRN number is not printed in the PDF itself). Title page says "Preliminary draft for comments", "April 2026" (no day). No version or revision marker. 38 PDF pages (text and conclusion pp. 1-27, references pp. 28-29, Data and Code Availability p. 30, Appendix A figures pp. 30-35, Appendix B tables pp. 35-38). Not peer reviewed: it is a student working draft circulated for comments, and a draft note on p. 1 says it is evidence on price incidence, not a full welfare analysis.

- **Link:** https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=6685418
- **How far we got:** read in full. PDF downloaded by hand by the project owner on 2026-11-11 and read locally. Read all 38 PDF pages in the pdftotext extraction (title page through Table B7, including references and the data-availability note). Rendered PDF pages 17-20 were also viewed to check Figure 1, Table 2, Figure 2, Figure 3 and Table 3; the other figures (Figure 4, Figures A1-A6) were read by caption only and their plotted values were not inspected. Page numbers below are the printed page numbers; printed page equals PDF page (offset zero). Where I read a point off a figure by eye I say so.
- **Funding or affiliation:** No funding is stated. Authors are MPA/ID students at the Harvard Kennedy School (p. 1 footnote). Acknowledgments: 'Professor Gautam Nair, Assistant Professor of Public Policy at the Harvard Kennedy School, for guidance and helpful comments' and 'the Harvard Kennedy School for institutional support'. No conflict-of-interest statement. Data providers named: Business Insider (facilities), EIA Form 861, FERC Form 1 via the Public Utility Data Liberation (PUDL) project, 2016 DOE City and County Energy Profiles. The paper discusses an Amazon-funded E3 report (Riu et al. 2025) as an industry benchmark (pp. 4, 7).
- **Unit:** County by year. Price panel covers 2002-2025 (76,861 county-years in total; 49,223 in 2010-2025; 49,126 with a nonmissing residential price and 48,929 with a nonmissing industrial price in 2010-2025) (Table B3, p. 37; Sec. 4.3, p. 13). Counties: 189 direct host counties, 2,935 never-direct counties, 1,514 mapped-indirect counties, 1,610 never-mapped-indirect counties (Table B3, p. 37). Estimating samples (Table 3, p. 20): indirect residential 2,055 counties = 505 treated + 1,550 controls; indirect industrial 2,045 = 505 + 1,540; direct residential 3,036 = 109 + 2,927; direct industrial 3,026 = 109 + 2,917. A different decomposition in Table B2 (p. 36) gives 777 treated and 1,195 comparison counties for the mapped-indirect clean design and 108 treated and 2,927 comparison counties for the direct design; the paper does not reconcile 505 vs 777 or 1,550 vs 1,195 (see critique). The number of treated UTILITIES in the county analysis is not stated. Utility-year mechanism panel (FERC Form 1 via PUDL): 131 or 225 utilities depending on outcome, 22 treated in every row of Table 4 (p. 22); 92 utilities for load and per-MWh outcomes. Geography: contiguous and all-U.S. coverage is not stated; the paper says air permits were collected from all 50 states and DC (p. 10).
- **Years:** County price data 2002-2025, with a harmonized analysis window of 2010-2025 for all main estimates (Sec. 4.3, p. 13; figure and table notes). Last year of retail price data in the regressions is 2025, which is the stated sample end; I am uncertain this is complete EIA-861 data for 2025 (the paper cites "EIA 2025a" only and does not give a data year or release date, p. 29). Treatment must fall within 2010-2025 (p. 13). Treatment years: first permit issuance year in a county (direct) or first year a serving utility is assigned a project outside the county (indirect). Of 189 raw direct hosts, 69 were first treated before 2010, and of 1,897 raw mapped-indirect counties 946 were first treated before 2010 (Table B2, p. 36). Event window k = -7 to k = +7, omitted k = -1 (Sec. 5.2, pp. 14-15). Mechanism sample 2010-2024 because FERC/PUDL outcomes end in 2024 (p. 11, p. 16). Facility data: Business Insider 2025 release (Sec. 4.1, p. 10); exact access date not stated. Bill calibration uses 2016 DOE county profiles and a 2024 national residential bill base (p. 23).
- **Outcome:** County-year average revenue per kilowatt-hour by customer class, built from EIA Form 861 retail revenue and sales and aggregated to county-year through the county-utility crosswalk (Sec. 4.1, p. 10). The main specifications are 'equal-weighted county price variables, which average across the utilities serving each county' (p. 10), so a county's price is the simple average of the average revenue per kWh of every utility listed for it, not weighted by sales or customers. Outcomes are the log residential price and log industrial price; the estimation uses the log of the price after winsorizing the price at the 1st and 99th percentiles within year and re-taking logs (p. 10); no winsorization, 2/98 and 5/95 are in Table B7. Whether revenue is bundled versus delivery-only, how retail-choice utilities are handled, whether the price is nominal or real, and the price units (cents/kWh or $/MWh) are NOT stated. Table 1 (p. 14) shows 2010 residential price means of 10.95, 12.02 and 10.63 and industrial 7.79, 7.06, 6.80 with no unit printed (the magnitudes look like cents per kWh; that is my reading, not stated). Whether the sales-weighted (non-equal-weighted) variant is reported is not stated: the text says 'main specifications use equal-weighted', implying an alternative exists, but none is shown. Commercial and all-sector prices are not studied. Coefficients are 100 times the log-point effect and the paper reads them as approximate percent effects (Table 2 notes, p. 18). Utility outcomes (Table 4, p. 22): FERC Form 1 purchased power total settlements and energy charges (Schedule 326), O&M (Sched. 320), retail operating revenue (Sched. 300), via PUDL crosswalks; load per customer from EIA-861; dollar outcomes in inverse hyperbolic sine, loads in logs. No bill, usage-as-outcome (other than 'industrial MWh per customer'), or wholesale price outcome. No water outcome.
- **Data center data:** Business Insider's 2025 investigation of U.S. data centers, cited as 'Business Insider. 2025. The True Cost of the Data Center Boom. Investigative data project and methodology.' (reference list, p. 28; Sec. 4.1, p. 10). The paper describes it as air-permit applications obtained from all 50 states and DC 'using federal industry codes associated with data-center operations and cross-validated each permit against company disclosures, DataCenterMap, Baxtel, and trade press'. The cleaned dataset 'includes 1,240 existing or planned facilities, each with a county location and a year of first permit issuance' (p. 10). The authors 'recovered the structured facility-level data embedded in the publicly released article page, parsed it in Python, and geocoded each facility to a U.S. county' (p. 10): i.e. they scraped the article page. So yes, the data are the Business Insider data center permit dataset; the version beyond 'Business Insider 2025' (publication date, retrieval date, release number) is not stated. No capacity (MW) variable is used or described. Cryptocurrency mines: not mentioned (neither included nor excluded). Whether backup-generator permits are the permit type: not stated. Validation: only the validation Business Insider is said to have done; the authors report none of their own. Raw data are not redistributed (p. 30). The paper itself says permits can precede operation 'by months or years' (p. 10).
- **Treatment:** Two binary, absorbing treatments built from permit-year timing, not load. DIRECT: the first year a county has a permitted facility in the Business Insider data ("the year of first observed permit issuance in a county", p. 10). INDIRECT (the paper's focus): a county is treated from the first year in which at least one utility serving it (per EIA-861 county service-territory files) has been assigned a data center project located in a different county. Five steps (Sec. 4.2, pp. 11-12): (1) county-utility links A_cut from EIA-861 with equal weights across the utilities listed for a county-year; (2) project-utility assignment B_put = 1 for projects 'that can be uniquely matched to a serving utility from facility location and utility geography', and for ambiguous projects exposure is split across the host county's serving utilities using pre-shock county-utility weights; the preferred specification uses the mapped assignments; how the unique matching is done (what 'utility geography' means, any spatial data) is not stated; (3) utility shock S_ut = 1 if any assigned project has entered (extensive margin; no MW, no counts); (4) leave-one-out: drop projects located in the county itself; (5) county exposure = weighted sum of utility shocks; first year it is positive = indirect treatment year G^I. Counties never treated get G=0. Thresholds: none (any facility, any size). Operating versus planned: the dataset mixes existing and planned facilities and the paper says some are 'planned or permitted but not yet fully online' (p. 25), and treats permit issuance as the date. Load in MW: not used; the paper says permit timing and assignment are 'measured more reliably than continuous facility-level load' (p. 12) and that the treatment is 'permit-based rather than load-based' (p. 4). Whether the permits are backup-generator permits is not stated; the paper says only 'air-permit applications from all fifty states and the District of Columbia using federal industry codes associated with data-center operations' (p. 10). Direct estimate: counties with a facility; comparison is not-yet-directly-treated counties. Preferred indirect design: treated = mapped-indirect non-host counties; controls = counties never mapped-indirectly exposed and never directly treated; direct hosts are dropped (pp. 13, 15-16). A 'broader not-yet' indirect control version is in Fig. A5 (p. 34). A geographic placebo assigns treatment to counties adjacent to host counties that are not utility-linked (p. 18).
- **Method:** Callaway and Sant'Anna (2021) staggered difference-in-differences ('CSDID', named in table notes), group-time ATTs aggregated by event time k, omitted k=-1, reported k=-7 to +7, with pre- and post-treatment averages (Sec. 5.2, pp. 14-15). Controls: 'state indicators' in the preferred specification; Table 2 also shows no controls, 'baseline county covariates' (demographic) and state indicators plus baseline price level. Which covariates enter (e.g. through the doubly robust or outcome-regression step) and how state indicators are used inside CSDID are not stated. Standard errors clustered by county (utility in the mechanism tests). Comparison groups: direct design uses not-yet-directly-treated counties (these may themselves be indirectly exposed, which the authors flag, p. 15); preferred indirect design uses never-mapped, never-direct counties; sensitivity with broader not-yet controls (Fig. A5). Identifying assumption is conditional parallel trends (Sec. 5.3, p. 15). Pre-trend evidence: pre-treatment averages in Table 2 and Table 3. For the preferred indirect residential cell the pre average is 0.10 (0.08) with state indicators and 0.00 (0.08) with state plus baseline price, but 0.50 (0.09) with no controls (Table 2, p. 18; the text says the no-controls pre-average is statistically significant, p. 18). In Figure 1 (read by eye, p. 17) several pre-period coefficients are individually distinguishable from zero: roughly -1.7 at k=-4 with an interval that excludes zero, and roughly +1.5 and +1.8 at k=-2 and k=-1 with intervals above zero (k=-1 is described as the omitted period yet a nonzero point is plotted; the paper does not explain this). Robustness: 128-specification sweep varying start year {2008, 2010, 2012, 2014}, controls {none, state}, outcome and winsorization (Table B6, p. 38); geographic placebo (Fig. 2, Fig. A6); clean-control direct runs (Figs. A2-A3). No formal sensitivity to parallel-trend violations (Rambachan-Roth) and no alternative estimators are run; the paper lists them as future work (p. 26). No instruments. The mechanism tests use the same CSDID on 22 treated PUDL-matched utilities (Sec. 5.5, p. 16).

**Headline findings**

- [primary] Residential price in mapped-indirect (non-host, shared-utility) counties: post-treatment average, preferred specification (state indicators, 1/99 winsorized, 2010-2025). This is the 'about 2 percent' in the abstract. Coefficient is 100 times log points. **Post avg. 2.07*** (0.40); pre avg. 0.10 (0.08)** (Table 2 col. (3) row 'Mapped indirect x Residential' p. 18; Table 3 row 1 p. 20; Table B7 row 'Indirect Residential 1/99' p. 38; abstract p. 1)
- [primary] Same cell, the table's separate 'ATT' column (the paper does not define how ATT differs from 'Post avg.'; both appear in Table 3) **ATT 1.85*** (0.37)** (Table 3 row 'Indirect Residential' p. 20; Table B7 p. 38)
- [primary] Residential mapped-indirect effect by event time: near zero for the first years, then growing. Table gives only t=4 and t=7; abstract says 'delayed' and 'increasing at longer horizons'. Figure 1 read by eye: about 0.3 at k=0, about -1.3 at k=1, about 0.3 at k=2 and k=3, about 2.4 at k=4, about 3.2 at k=5, about 5.5 at k=6, about 5.9 at k=7. **t=4: 2.45*** (0.65); t=7: 5.88*** (0.73)** (Table 3 row 1 p. 20; Figure 1 p. 17 (k=0 to 6 values are my visual reads, not printed); text p. 16 says 'close to 6 percent by the seven-year horizon')
- [primary] Residential mapped-indirect post average under four control sets **(1) none 3.20*** (0.44); (2) demographic 3.06*** (0.44); (3) state indicators 2.07*** (0.40); (4) state + baseline price 1.69*** (0.41). Pre averages 0.50 (0.09), 0.56 (0.09), 0.10 (0.08), 0.00 (0.08)** (Table 2 p. 18)
- [primary] Industrial price in mapped-indirect counties: not significant in the preferred specification; large and significant only without state controls **Table 2: no controls 6.98*** (1.10), demographic 6.30*** (1.07), state 1.56 (1.25), state+baseline price 0.64 (1.13). Table 3: ATT 1.26 (1.03), t=4 1.09 (1.63), t=7 7.79** (3.19)** (Table 2 p. 18; Table 3 p. 20)
- [primary] Host counties (direct): industrial price, preferred specification **ATT 2.57* (1.45); post avg. 2.81** (1.37); pre avg. -0.13 (0.36); t=4 3.60 (2.53); t=7 3.36 (2.50). Table 2 post avg.: no controls 6.51*** (1.78), demographic 3.85** (1.64), state 2.81** (1.37), state+baseline price 1.88 (1.35)** (Table 3 row 4 p. 20; Table 2 p. 18)
- [primary] Host counties (direct): residential price, preferred specification. Weak and unstable; authors say 'positive in some specifications but less stable' **ATT 1.33** (0.62); post avg. 0.86 (0.57); pre avg. -0.18 (0.15); t=4 0.74 (0.94); t=7 2.37** (1.05). Table 2 post avg.: 1.76* (0.90), -0.16 (0.90), 0.86 (0.57), 0.52 (0.58)** (Table 3 row 3 p. 20; Table 2 p. 18; text p. 19)
- [primary] Sample sizes behind the four preferred cells (counties; treated; controls) **Indirect residential 2,055; 505; 1,550. Indirect industrial 2,045; 505; 1,540. Direct residential 3,036; 109; 2,927. Direct industrial 3,026; 109; 2,917** (Table 3 p. 20)
- [primary] Sample decomposition: raw ever-treated, dropped for pre-2010 first treatment, and the estimating sample. Treated and comparison counts differ from Table 3 for the indirect design **Direct: 189 raw, 69 before 2010, 12 insufficient pre/post, 108 treated, 2,927 comparison. Mapped-indirect: 1,897 raw, 946 before 2010, 59 direct hosts excluded, 115 insufficient pre/post, 777 treated, 1,195 comparison** (Table B2 p. 36)
- [primary] Geographic placebo (adjacent, not utility-linked counties, residential): authors say weaker than the mapped pattern and 'not perfectly flat'. No table numbers. Figure 2 read by eye: roughly 2.7 at k=4 and 2.5 at k=5 with intervals near or above zero, roughly -2.9 at k=-2, and wide intervals at k=6 and 7 **not tabulated; see figure** (Figure 2 p. 19; text p. 18-19; Fig. A6 p. 35 (never-placebo controls; not inspected))
- [primary] Utility-level mechanism: purchased-power settlements and energy charges rise after exposure (inverse hyperbolic sine, coefficients times 100; 131 utilities, 22 treated) **Settlements: pre 2.71 (4.29), post 27.31*** (9.53), t=4 45.14*** (13.54), t=5 56.18*** (17.72). Energy charges: pre 3.60 (4.29), post 32.15** (12.72), t=4 44.84** (18.36), t=5 56.32** (23.00)** (Table 4 p. 22; robustness variants Table B1 p. 35)
- [primary] Other utility outcomes: T&D O&M not increasing; retail revenue rises (with some nonzero pre-period differences); industrial load per customer positive but imprecise (225 utilities, 22 treated; load 92 utilities) **T&D O&M post -17.72 (13.99); total retail revenue pre 2.03 (1.51), post 4.62** (1.97), t=5 9.43*** (3.64); residential revenue pre 3.20* (1.75), post 5.83*** (2.22), t=5 11.76*** (3.29); industrial MWh per customer post 16.70 (11.96); non-grid expenses post -16.17* (8.89)** (Table 4 p. 22; text p. 22 ('Revenue outcomes are interpreted descriptively when pre-period estimates are nonzero'))
- [primary] Authors' own aggregate (scaling exercise, NOT an estimate): cumulative gross additional residential payments implied by the event-time coefficient path in mapped-exposed counties over event years 0-7. Applied to county-years actually observed, using exp(beta_h)-1 times a county bill base from 2016 DOE modeled expenditure shares scaled to a 2024 national residential bill base **$6.16 billion (headline '$6 billion'); 892 counties; 5,409 county-years; bill base $48.68 bn/year; 2016 bill share of exposed counties 19.71%; benchmark of 2.07% times base = $1.02 bn per year** (Table 5 p. 24; Sec. 6.7 pp. 22-24; introduction p. 3-4)
- [primary] Robustness sweep: mapped-indirect residential estimates are positive and significant in all 16 state-control specifications; median ATT and post average **Median ATT 1.80, median post avg. 2.26, median pre avg. 0.10; post significantly positive in 100%; pre insignificant in 69%; all late horizons positive in 100%. With no controls: median post 2.92, pre insignificant in 0%. Mapped-indirect industrial with state controls: positive and significant in 38%. Direct residential with state controls: 12%** (Table B6 p. 38)
- [primary] Winsorization sensitivity of preferred cells **Indirect residential ATT 2.02*** (0.37) none, 1.85*** (0.37) 1/99, 1.85*** (0.37) 2/98, 1.80*** (0.35) 5/95; post avg. 2.19, 2.07, 2.08, 1.90 (all ***). Indirect residential 5/95 pre avg. 0.14** (0.07)** (Table B7 p. 38)
- [primary] Start-year sensitivity of the preferred residential cell (state controls, 1/99): estimates rise with later start year, especially at t=7 **start 2008: ATT 2.41, post 2.93, t=7 4.79; start 2010: 1.85, 2.07, 5.88; start 2014: 1.55, 3.07, t=7 10.40 (standard errors not shown in the ranked table)** (Table B5 p. 37)
- [primary] Size of the first data center relative to the utility (not the authors' estimate; quoted from another paper) **Median first-entry data center about ten megawatts, about 1.2 percent of the median serving utility's annual sales (attributed to Yue and Zeng 2026)** (Sec. 3.1 p. 6)

**Heterogeneity reported:** Only splits by channel (direct host versus mapped-indirect) and by class (residential versus industrial), by event time (t=4 and t=7), by control set (Table 2), by start year (2008, 2010, 2012, 2014) and by winsorization (Tables B4-B7). The paper reports NO split by investor-owned versus public or cooperative, by retail-choice versus vertically integrated states, by load size relative to the utility, by spare capacity, by hyperscaler versus colocation, by region or market (PJM, ERCOT etc.), or by early versus recent entry other than the start-year sweep. State indicators are used as controls only. Ownership, restructuring and region are not mentioned as dimensions of the analysis. 'Early versus recent years' is covered only through the start-year sweep: later start years give larger residential estimates at t=7 (Table B5 p. 37). Mechanism results are for 22 treated utilities pooled.

**Limitations the authors state:** Authors list five (Sec. 7, p. 25): (1) data center entry may correlate with regional trends in demand, industrial growth, capacity, transmission or regulation, and the leave-one-out construction 'does not by itself make exposed and unexposed utility networks comparable'; (2) utility service-territory mappings and facility-to-utility assignments are measured with error, especially near boundaries or in counties with several utilities; (3) treatment is permit-based, not load-based, and some facilities are 'planned or permitted but not yet fully online', so timing may precede load; (4) no observation of rate cases, capex, tariffs and class cost allocation; (5) the geographic placebo is weaker but 'not perfectly flat'. Also: the title-page draft note says rate cases, tariff provisions and capital allocations are not yet observed (p. 1); the introduction calls the evidence 'suggestive rather than definitive' and a 'first pass' (p. 4); the mechanism results are evidence of utility-level cost pressure, not of residential cost allocation (pp. 9, 22, 25-26); revenue outcomes with nonzero pre-trends are descriptive only (p. 22); rate-base measures 'are not yet suitable for interpretation' (Table 4 note p. 22); the magnitude exercise is not a welfare estimate or forecast and uses one 2016 cross-section of modeled DOE expenditure shares (pp. 23-24); the direct estimates may be conservative because not-yet-treated controls can be indirectly exposed (p. 15); the paper says alternative estimators and parallel-trend sensitivity analyses (Borusyak-Jaravel-Spiess; Rambachan-Roth) should be added (p. 26).

**What a skeptical referee would raise:** Items marked [derived] are my own arithmetic from the paper's tables. 1. The 'treated' unit is a county that shares an EIA-861 utility with a county that has a permit, so the exposure is far broader than the facility's serving utility. Facility-to-utility assignment is claimed to be unique in some cases and allocated pro rata in others, with no description of how uniqueness is determined or how many projects are ambiguous (Sec. 4.2 pp. 11-12; limitation 2 on p. 25). Exposure that is 'allocated' across serving utilities by county weights means a utility that does not serve the facility can be coded exposed. 2. Start dates are year of first air-permit issuance, not energization. The authors concede permits can precede operations by 'months or years' (p. 10) and that some are planned but not online (p. 25). The paper reads lead time as biasing against sharp effects, but it also means effects at k=0 to 3 are not load effects, and the facilities never come on line in some cases. No MW, no check that any load arrived. A skeptic cannot tell whether the 2% reflects data center load or anticipatory, regional or planning-driven cost changes. 3. Pre-trends are not clean in the preferred figure. By eye in Figure 1 (p. 17), k=-4 is about -1.7, k=-2 about +1.5 and k=-1 about +1.8, with intervals that exclude zero, and the omitted period is plotted as nonzero without explanation; the paper's own table reports a significant 0.50 (0.09) pre average without controls (Table 2 p. 18) and 0.14** (0.07) with 5/95 winsorization (Table B7). The comfort offered rests on adding state indicators and baseline price. No parallel-trend sensitivity is run (p. 26). 4. The sample counts do not reconcile. Table 3 (p. 20) has 505 treated and 1,550 controls for the indirect residential cell; Table B2 (p. 36) gives 777 treated and 1,195 comparison counties for the same design; Table B3 (p. 37) gives 1,610 never-mapped counties. [derived] 505 plus 1,550 = 2,055 matches Table 3's total but not B2's 777 plus 1,195 = 1,972. The note to Table 3 attributes shrinkage to support restrictions, but 505 versus 777 is a large gap. The number of treated utilities behind the county result is not given at all; the county result is therefore carried by an unknown number of utilities whose counties are many and highly correlated. Standard errors are clustered at the county level even though treatment varies at the utility-network level, so counties sharing a utility are likely to have correlated errors and the stated SEs are probably too small (my judgment; the paper does not discuss clustering by utility for the county design). 5. County price construction. Counties get the simple average of the average revenue per kWh of every utility listed for them, regardless of how many customers each serves, with no mention of bundled versus delivery-only revenue, retail choice, real versus nominal units, or customer-class reporting. Because the outcome is a mean of utility averages, treatment can move through changes in which utilities are listed for a county in the annual EIA service-territory file, or through composition changes among small utilities, not only through rates. The 1/99 winsorization within year is applied to the price before logs, so the extreme tails are trimmed (limits shown in Table B7: little change). 6. Revenue per kWh cannot distinguish a rate increase from lower kWh sales or class mix. No usage or customer outcome is shown for counties; the utility-level 'industrial MWh per customer' is imprecise (Table 4). 7. Controls contaminated by region-wide costs. Control counties are in the same states (state indicators) as treated ones, but state-level wholesale and capacity cost increases, which could hit both data-center and non-data-center utilities, are not separately measured. The placebo (Fig. 2) is not flat (about +2.7 at k=4 and +2.5 at k=5 by eye, with wide intervals), and the authors say it is 'not perfectly flat' (p. 18); the placebo is also computed on a different control set (not-yet-placebo treated) from the main result. No wholesale price outcome is used. 8. Mechanism evidence is from 22 treated utilities (Table 4, p. 22), outcomes in inverse hyperbolic sine at scale (coefficients 27 to 56 percent), and the authors themselves say these are not pass-through rates. Utilities that report FERC Form 1 are mostly investor-owned, so the mechanism sample is not the county sample. 9. The dollar figure ($6.16 billion, Table 5 p. 24) is a scaling of regression coefficients by modeled 2016 DOE expenditure shares, uses 892 counties that the authors say are a broader population than the estimation sample (p. 23), and sums across all observed event years 0-7. [derived] 1,897 raw mapped-indirect counties minus 946 pre-2010 minus 59 direct hosts is 892, so the calibration population includes counties dropped from estimation for insufficient pre/post support (115 in Table B2) and counties that may have little of the lagged effect. [derived] A national residential bill base implied by 48.68 / 0.1971 is about $247 billion, which I did not check against any source. The headline is explicitly not a welfare estimate or forecast. 10. Specification search. The paper reports a 128-specification sweep and 'highest-scoring' specification tables (Tables B4-B5) chosen by a pre-trend, post-treatment and late-horizon score, then calls the residential result robust because 100% of state-control residential specs are positive. Start year moves the t=7 residential estimate from 4.79 (2008) to 10.40 (2014) (Table B5), so the long-horizon number is sensitive. No multiple-testing adjustment. Industrial result differs by control set (6.98*** with no controls vs 1.56 with state indicators, Table 2). 11. Magnitude and reading. The headline 2.07% is the average of event-time coefficients 0 to 7 in which early coefficients are about zero; the t=7 coefficient is 5.88%. 'About 2 percent' is therefore an average over lags, not a steady price increase. 12. Source quality. The facility inventory is a news organization's data page scraped by the authors, with no capacity, no operator, no data-center type (hyperscale versus colocation), no cryptocurrency filter described, 1,240 facilities in total, and the version is not given. It is a student draft, preliminary, not peer reviewed, with replication code only 'where permitted'. 13. Data timing. The sample runs through 2025 prices; the paper does not say which EIA-861 release supplies 2025, and I am uncertain whether a complete 2025 EIA-861 file existed at the time of writing (April 2026). Worth checking before relying on the 2025 observations. No content on WATER rates or water bills appears anywhere in the paper (searched the full text for 'water'; zero hits).

**What it would take to reproduce:** All inputs are public or journalistic: EIA-861 (sales, revenue, county service-territory files), FERC Form 1 via PUDL, DOE 2016 county energy profiles, and Business Insider's data-center dataset, which is not a downloadable file: the authors parsed structured data embedded in the article page (p. 10), so a replicator must re-scrape or ask the authors; the raw facility data are not redistributed (p. 30). Data and Code Availability (p. 30): replication code 'will be made available where permitted'; derived county-year treatment variables 'can be shared subject to the terms of the underlying sources'. No repository link appears; the table notes of Table B3 say counts come from 'the paper-output do-file', i.e. Stata. Specification of the main result in two sentences: county-by-year log residential average revenue per kWh from EIA-861 (equal-weighted across serving utilities, 1/99 winsorized by year, times 100), Callaway-Sant'Anna estimator with state indicators, 2010-2025, treated = non-host counties whose serving utility is first assigned a Business Insider facility located in another county, controls = never-mapped, never-host counties, SEs clustered by county. Details that cannot be reproduced from the text: how facilities are 'uniquely matched' to a serving utility, how ambiguous projects are split in practice, which year's EIA-861 service-territory file defines the links (the paper says 'annual' links), the covariate treatment inside CSDID, the sales-weighting alternative, price units and nominal versus real, and how the 'ATT' column is aggregated.

**Corrections to what we had heard:** 1. Authors: PARTLY WRONG. Printed list is Herman Lange, Sahil Bagwe, Aditya Murlidharan, Lothar Rojas (title page; footnote p. 1). The fourth author is 'Lothar Rojas', not 'Mateo Rojas Lothar'. The footnote gives the first-listed author's email as nlange@hks.harvard.edu (first name printed is Herman). All four are 'MPA/ID students at the Harvard Kennedy School'. 2. County exposure to data centers through shared utilities: confirmed (abstract p. 1; Sec. 4.2 pp. 11-12). It is a leave-one-out, extensive-margin, binary exposure for NON-host counties; it is not an MW or intensity measure. 3. Host counties see higher industrial prices: confirmed with caveats. Direct x industrial: ATT 2.57* (1.45), post avg. 2.81** (1.37), significant only at 10% for ATT; not significant with baseline price control, 1.88 (1.35) (Table 3 p. 20; Table 2 p. 18). The abstract says 'show evidence of'. 4. Non-host counties served by the same utility see residential increases of about 2%, with a lag: confirmed. Post average 2.07*** (0.40) (Table 2 col. 3, p. 18), with the event-time path near zero in early years and 5.88*** (0.73) at t=7 (Table 3 p. 20); abstract says 'averaging about 2 percent after exposure and increasing at longer horizons'. The treated group is mapped-indirect non-host counties, not 'utilities' or 'households'. The 2% is a county-level equal-weighted average of utility average revenue per kWh, not a bill. 5. Data: permit data for facilities, EIA service-territory maps, FERC Form 1 financials: confirmed (abstract p. 1; Sec. 4.1 pp. 10-11). The permit data are Business Insider's data center database (1,240 existing or planned facilities); FERC Form 1 comes through PUDL and is used only for the utility mechanism tests (22 treated utilities), not for the price estimates. The paper also uses 2016 DOE City and County Energy Profiles for the bill calibration (p. 11, p. 23). 6. Posted on SSRN around 11 April 2026: not in the paper. The title page says only 'April 2026' and 'Preliminary draft for comments'; the SSRN ID and posting date are not printed.

## Work we had missed

Short answer: yes, a plain utility-level panel through 2025 would substantially duplicate existing work. It would not duplicate anything if it were built as an open reconciliation of the existing estimates. Why it duplicates. Counting your list plus what I found, at least five papers already run a utility-level retail panel: Meeks et al. (2010-2024), Watten et al. (2015-2024), Scalera et al. (about 1,200 utility territories, 2015-2024), Rana/Kaps/Netessine (42 utilities, 2020-2025), and the one you are missing, Ryu and Hiatt at USC (EIA-861, described as 2015-25). Garimella also runs a utility-territory check. Two of these already reach 2025. I did not re-open the papers on your list in this session, so their sample details above come from search summaries and should be checked against your copies. What you are missing, in order of importance: 1. Ryu and Hiatt (USC Marshall, 2026): utility-level difference-in-differences, +0.62% residential per doubling of capacity, concentrated in co-ops and munis. I read only the author-coauthored NCEA brief; the paper is on a Google Drive link. 2. Bagwe, Lange, Murlidharan and Rojas Lothar (SSRN 6685418, April 2026): shared-utility exposure, about 2% residential increase in non-host counties. Not obtained (SSRN 403). 3. Feng and You, Nature Energy, 9 November 2026: the first peer-reviewed ex-post paper, on wholesale prices and docket cost allocation through 2025. Abstract page only. 4. Wang (Yale senior essay, April 2026): county panel on tariff components, +0.74% residential fixed charge per 100 MW. Read in full through the results tables. 5. Mamkhezri, Sun and Yang (SSRN 5736562, 2025): Virginia wholesale, about 7%. Citation only. 6. Lower-weight items: IER state-level correlations; Synapse's Mississippi docket accounting for Earthjustice; a third E3 report (for CloudHQ) and a December 2025 E3 ratepayer study; the Columbia CGEP literature review; Fearon's Ireland model; Darmouni et al. (projection); two theory papers. Crypto precedents: Benetton, Compiani and Morse (NBER w31312) is the one that matters methodologically. It uses the Bitcoin price as a demand shifter on monthly town-level supply charges and finds about $88 per household per year in upstate New York. Lee, Wu and Dessler give about 2% per GW on ERCOT wholesale prices. I found no retail-bill study for Texas crypto, and no primary source for the Wood Mackenzie $1.8 billion figure. Critiques and replications: none found in formal form. Only journalism and blogs. What would be new for an independent study: - A replication package on open data that runs the competing designs side by side and shows why the sign flips. Nobody has published this. - The ownership split. Ryu and Hiatt find the effect in co-ops and munis and none in IOUs; a search summary says Meeks et al. find it concentrated in IOUs. That is a direct, testable conflict. - Fully open facility data. Three of the papers I opened (Wang, Alvarez et al., Feng and You) rely on licensed 451 Research data, which blocks replication. - Outcomes beyond average revenue per kWh: fixed charges, riders, bills. - Tariff and capacity heterogeneity: before and after large-load tariffs, tight vs slack reserve margins. Uncertainties and limits: - I am uncertain whether final 2025 EIA-861 data are public yet or whether any existing paper truly uses full-year 2025 retail data. The NCEA brief says 2015-25 in the text but cites the October 2025 EIA-861 release and a 2015-24 sample in a footnote. - My web search budget ran out. Not completed: verification of Mamkhezri et al. and the Goldman Sachs report; state consumer advocate and PUC staff studies beyond Mississippi; pre-2025 literature on large industrial loads and rates; dissertations. One search each for RFF, Energy Institute at Haas, EPIC Chicago and the non-Dallas regional Feds returned nothing relevant, which is weak evidence of absence. Google Scholar, SSRN and RePEc could not be queried directly. - One lead is unresolved: a Dayton Daily News story headlined as a study finding data centers are driving up regional electric costs returned a rate-limit error, so I could not identify the study. - Items marked primary were either read as PDFs page by page (Wang, NBER w31312, NCEA brief, Synapse, IER, E3, CGEP commentary, TSE, Lee and Schmalz) or read as a publisher or repository page through a summarizing fetch (Nature Energy abstract, arXiv abstracts, Zenodo record, CGEP review). The second group carries more transcription risk; check numbers before quoting. - No files were written or modified. PDFs fetched during verification were cached automatically by the fetch tool in the session's tool-results folder.

**Update, 2026-11-11: Ryu and Hiatt, and the paper listed here as Bagwe et al., have since been read in full from PDFs downloaded by hand; their records are above and replace what this section said about them. The second paper's printed author order is Lange, Bagwe, Murlidharan and Rojas (the fourth author is Lothar Rojas). Ryu and Hiatt's annual retail panel ends in 2024; their 2025 figure adds preliminary monthly data.**

- [primary] Wang, Ryan (2026). The Data Center Next Door: Macroeconomic Incidence of Compute. Yale College senior essay, advisor Pascual Restrepo, dated 1 April 2026. https://economics.yale.edu/sites/default/files/2026-05/Wang_The%20Data%20Center%20Next%20Door.pdf. County-year TWFE, 2014-2025, tariff data from OpenEI URDB: +0.74% residential monthly fixed charge per 100 MW in regulated states; no effect on volumetric rate *Why it matters:* Read directly (pp. 1-21). Design is two-way fixed effects with continuous MW treatment, not IV (a search summary mislabeled it). Data: S&P 451 Research Datacenter KnowledgeBase, 4,688 US facilities (p.10, proprietary); retail outcomes from the OpenEI Utility Rate Database mapped to counties with EIA-861 territories (p.10, Table 1); 247 treatment counties at a 10 MW threshold (p.12). Table 4 (p.19): rate case approval +0.65 percentage points per 100 MW (coef 0.0065, SE 0.0027); log fixed charge 0.0074 (SE 0.0023); log variable rate 0.0026 (SE 0.0017, not significant); balancing-authority spillover on fixed charge 0.0006 (SE 0.0003). Author converts this to about $0.16/month per 100 MW (p.19). Stated limit: URDB base tariffs omit power-cost and transmission riders and competitive retailer prices, so the estimate is described as a lower bound (p.11). For a new study: this is the only paper I found that separates fixed charges from volumetric rates and uses rate-case approval as an outcome. EIA-861 revenue per kWh blends both, so a new panel should either add a tariff-component outcome or say plainly that it cannot see the fixed-charge channel. The figures also show weak pre-trends for fixed charges (Figure 3, p.15), a caution for any county or utility DiD.
- [primary] Feng, Cheng and Fengqi You (2026). Data centres asymmetrically exacerbate energy burdens through wholesale price increases and regressive grid cost shifting. Nature Energy. DOI 10.1038/s41560-026-02163-x. https://www.nature.com/articles/s41560-026-02163-x. Peer-reviewed, published two days ago: wholesale price increases attributable to data centers exceed 10% in the most exposed hubs by 2025; residential customers get a median 47% of allocated grid costs *Why it matters:* Only the publisher abstract page, figure captions and data statement were readable; the article is subscription-only, so full text and Supplementary methods NOT obtained. Seen on the abstract page: wholesale increases above 10% in the most exposed hubs by 2025; median 47% residential cost allocation; rural burden increase 76% larger than urban; up to 28 percentage points of added energy burden for the lowest-income households; local spending elasticity 0.16. Coverage per Extended Data Fig. 1 caption: PJM, CAISO, ERCOT, MISO, 2020-2025, 32 load zones, 192 zone-years. Facility data are licensed S&P Capital IQ / 451 Research; aggregates and code are on Zenodo (doi 10.5281/zenodo.23019736). The 38% / $8.25 billion figures circulating in press come from the Cornell release (news.cornell.edu/node/333906), not from anything I saw on the journal page, so they are secondhand. I could not confirm the causal estimator. For a new study: this is now the first peer-reviewed ex-post paper in the space and it goes through 2025, but its outcomes are wholesale prices and docket cost allocations, not utility retail rates. A retail panel is complementary. The Zenodo zone-level aggregates are an open input worth checking before building anything from scratch.
- [secondhand] Mamkhezri, Jamal, Xiaochen Sun and Yuting Yang (2025). The Hidden Cost of the Cloud: Data Centers and Electricity Market Inefficiency. Draft. SSRN 5736562. https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=5736562. Reported causal estimate: data center entry raised Virginia wholesale power prices by about 7%, 2015-2024 *Why it matters:* Full text NOT obtained (SSRN 403; no open copy found before my search budget ran out). The 7% Virginia wholesale figure and the 2015-2024 window are as described by Crampes and Estache (TSE WP 1726, p.2), whose reference list (p.29) gives the citation and labels it a Georgia Tech draft; I could not confirm the authors' affiliation. Download by hand from SSRN. It is a single-state wholesale study, so it does not duplicate a retail panel, but it predates all the 2026 papers and should be in the literature review. It also offers a Virginia benchmark against which a Dominion-specific retail estimate can be compared.
- [primary] Pyle, Tom and Daniel Simmons (2026). Have Data Centers Driven Up Electricity Prices? The State-Level Data Don't Support the Narrative. Institute for Energy Research. https://www.instituteforenergyresearch.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/Have-data-centers-increased-the-price-of-electricity.pdf. Descriptive 50-state cross-section: no significant correlation between data center counts and price levels or price growth; sales growth strongly negatively correlated with price growth *Why it matters:* Read directly (6 pp). The PDF carries no date on its face; March 2026 comes from the URL path and a secondary listing. EIA state prices and sales paired with 2025 state data center counts. Page 3: Pearson r = -0.053 (p = 0.72) between counts and current prices; top-10 data center states average 14.46 cents/kWh vs 14.39 for the rest; 2015-2025 price change 36.3% vs 28.1% (p = 0.144); 2021-2025 26.5% vs 21.1% (p = 0.228). Page 4: sales growth vs price change 2015-2025 Pearson -0.710, Spearman -0.807. No controls, no identification, counts rather than MW, and the authors concede reverse causality (p.3). For a new study: this is the naive benchmark that readers will have seen. Reproduce the state-level sales-growth correlation on open data, then show what happens to it within state (utility fixed effects) and after separating data center load from other sales growth. IER is an advocacy group.
- [primary] Glaser Schoff, Aidan, Ben Havumaki and Melissa Whited (2026). In the Dark: Data Centers Could Be Costing Mississippi Households. Synapse Energy Economics, prepared for Earthjustice and Environmental Advocates Mississippi. https://earthjustice.org/document/in-the-dark-data-centers-could-be-costing-mississippi-households. Docket-based single-utility accounting: Entergy Mississippi residential customers estimated to have paid about $38 million by March 2026, about $10.60 per month on the average bill *Why it matters:* Read the cover, contents, executive summary and introduction directly (pp. i, 1-3 of 31); the calculation is in Appendix A section 5.3 (p.23), which I did not read. Executive summary (p. i): about $38 million paid as of March 2026, $74 million by end of 2026, about $10.60/month, with the authors stressing these are estimates because key filings are confidential. This is the only consumer-advocate-side ex-post dollar estimate I found. For a new study: (1) it is a bottom-up method (trace rider recoveries in public dockets) that a panel regression cannot replicate but can be checked against, so Entergy Mississippi is a natural case study or validation point; (2) it shows that special-contract confidentiality limits what any open-data study can see, which belongs in the limitations section. Entergy disputes the findings (press, secondhand).
- [primary] Bertolacini, Molly, Shana Ramirez, Megan Ahern, Anna Clark and Kush Patel (2026). Beyond the Headlines: An Empirical Analysis of Data Center Grid Utilization, Cost-of-Service and Revenue Contributions. E3, funded by CloudHQ. https://www.ethree.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/06/E3_Large-Load-Economics_June-2026.pdf. A third 2026 E3 paper, separate from the Data Center Coalition review and the Amazon tariff study: hypothetical 100 MW data center on Dominion's GS-5 tariff yields a $37 million net system benefit in the base case *Why it matters:* Read directly (pp. 1-9). Despite the title, it is a modeled marginal-cost exercise, not ex-post evidence. CloudHQ funded it and reviewed it before publication (p.1). Base case net benefit $37 million NPV; sensitivities run from a $166 million benefit to a $92 million net cost (p.5); full load departure costs $57 million under GS-5 vs $415 million under the prior tariff (p.7). The paper calls itself illustrative rather than predictive and says it does not address PJM capacity price movements (p.8). It also cites a fourth E3 document, a Ratepayer Impact Study dated December 2025 at ethree.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/12/RatepayerStudy.pdf (p.4 figure source), which a separate paper attributes to PG&E; I did not open that one. For a new study: confirm which E3 documents are already in your set, and treat this one as a statement of the fixed-cost-spreading hypothesis to test, not as evidence.
- [primary] Rivera Rivota, Diego, Leander Weigelt, Gaspar Simon Guevara et al. (2026). The Effects of Load Growth on Electricity Prices in the United States: A Literature Review. Columbia SIPA Center on Global Energy Policy. Companion commentary: Millican, Arent and Sandalow, Electricity Affordability and Load Growth: Diagnosing and Fixing the Problem (June 2026). https://www.energypolicy.columbia.edu/publications/the-effects-of-load-growth-on-electricity-prices-in-the-united-states-a-literature-review/. The main published literature review, and it omits every 2026 causal working paper *Why it matters:* I read the commentary PDF directly (8 pp) and the literature review through a page-summarizing fetch of the HTML, including its reference list. Both are funded by Google.org (commentary p.1). The review's references include LBNL/Brattle, CRA for EEI, E3 for Amazon, Peskoe and Martin, and UCS, but none of Meeks, Watten, Scalera, Garimella, Yue and Zeng, Kay, Alvarez or Netessine. It also cites one item you did not list: Murphy, Vincent and Rauschkolb (2026), Factors Driving Electricity Prices in Maine, Brattle for Maine DOER, which I did not open. The commentary contains no original regression; it restates LBNL figures (for example residential prices up 6% nominal and 2.6% real in 2025, p.2). For a new study: there is still no review that sets the 2026 causal papers side by side, so a comparison table of designs, samples and signs would be new. Check your LBNL/Brattle numbers against the July 2026 update, which is the version these documents lean on.
- [primary] Fearon, Sean (2026). The Cost of Data Centres: Modelling the household electricity costs of Ireland's data centre sector. Commissioned by Friends of the Earth Ireland and Beyond Fossil Fuels. Zenodo, DOI 10.5281/zenodo.20487363. https://zenodo.org/records/20487363. Ireland, where data centers are about 22% of electricity use: residual-demand merit-order model puts the cumulative cost at 360 euros per average household, 2015-2023 *Why it matters:* I read the Zenodo record page only; the 21.6 MB PDF is openly downloadable (CC BY 4.0) but I did not open it, so page numbers are not available. The 360 euro figure and the method (demand-side merit-order simulation on EirGrid and ENTSO-E data) are from the record description. Press reports give other totals (715 million euros for 2015-2023; about 1.4 billion for 2015-2024) that I could not reconcile; check the PDF before quoting any of them. It is a counterfactual simulation, not a regression on observed retail prices, and it is advocacy-commissioned. For a new study: Ireland is the highest-penetration case anywhere and is a useful external comparison for the wholesale channel, but it does not duplicate a US retail panel.
- [primary] Benetton, Matteo, Giovanni Compiani and Adair Morse (2023). When Cryptomining Comes to Town: High Electricity-Use Spillovers to the Local Economy. NBER Working Paper 31312. https://www.nber.org/system/files/working_papers/w31312/w31312.pdf. Crypto analogue, upstate New York: mining cost households $204 million and small businesses $92 million a year; about $88 per household per year *Why it matters:* Read directly (pp. 1-6 of the NBER PDF). Abstract: $92 million small business and $204 million household extra cost annually in Upstate NY. Design (p.3): provider-town-month electricity prices and usage, with the Bitcoin price as an instrument that shifts miner demand; first-stage F about 700; demand elasticities -0.07 households and -0.17 small business. Page 5: average household pays $88 more per year and small business $168; added local tax revenue of almost $40 million offsets about 14%; net consumer surplus loss $257 million. Mine locations were hand-collected from local media (p.4). The earlier 2019 draft on Morse's Haas page reports electricity prices up 16% for business and 6% for households when Bitcoin rose from about $5,000 to over $15,000 (p.3). I found no journal version. For a new study this is the key methodological precedent: (1) identification from a high-frequency external demand shifter rather than from entry timing; an analogue for data centers would be something like GPU shipment or cloud capex cycles interacted with pre-existing capacity; (2) the outcome is the supply-charge pass-through component of the bill at monthly frequency, which annual EIA-861 cannot see, so consider EIA-861M or state supply-charge data for a subset; (3) it nets tax revenue against bill costs, a comparison a data center study will be asked for. Funding note: two authors acknowledge Ripple's University Blockchain Research Initiative (title page).
- [primary] Lee, Jangho, Lily Wu and Andrew E. Dessler (2023). A quantification of how much crypto-miners are driving up the wholesale cost of energy in Texas. arXiv 2302.02221. https://arxiv.org/abs/2302.02221. Crypto analogue, ERCOT: each GW of mining load is associated with about a 2% rise in wholesale prices *Why it matters:* I read the arXiv abstract page only (single version, no journal reference); full text not opened, so no table or page locations. The abstract gives about 2% per GW of mining load on ERCOT wholesale prices, with roughly 1 GW online at the time. It is a wholesale estimate with no retail pass-through step. For a new study: it supplies a per-GW wholesale benchmark for Texas that can be set beside the Dallas Fed and Ryu-Hiatt zone estimates, and it shows the gap every wholesale paper leaves: nobody has traced ERCOT wholesale effects into retail plans. The widely repeated Wood Mackenzie figure of $1.8 billion a year for Texas appeared only in press coverage; I found no primary document for it.
- [primary] Majumder, Subir, Ignacio Aravena and Le Xie (2024). An Econometric Analysis of Large Flexible Cryptocurrency-mining Consumers in Electricity Markets. arXiv 2408.12014 (v2 30 Dec 2024; accepted at HICSS-58). https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.12014. Crypto analogue, Texas: large miners' short-run consumption responds to temperature and electricity prices and drops in summer to avoid 4CP transmission charges *Why it matters:* I read the arXiv abstract page only. It is a demand-side study and does not estimate price effects on other customers. It matters for design: crypto load is price-responsive and avoids coincident peaks, while AI data center load is usually treated as flat. A new study that pools crypto mines and data centers as one 'large load' treatment would be mixing two different load shapes with different cost-causation. Separate them, or at least test whether results change when crypto-heavy utilities (for example in ERCOT) are dropped.
- [primary] Crampes, Claude and Antonio Estache (2026). On the predictability of the effects of data centers electricity demand shocks. Toulouse School of Economics Working Paper 1726. See also Lee, Seung Joo and Martin C. Schmalz (2026), Data Centers and Electricity Bills: Pecuniary Externality?, SSRN 6157126, open copy at seungecon.github.io/files/Pecuniary_Externality.pdf. https://www.tse-fr.eu/sites/default/files/TSE/documents/doc/wp/2026/wp_tse_1726.pdf. Theory: incumbent customers lose in the short run and recover only if the entrant finances its own capacity and lacks excessive bargaining power *Why it matters:* Both PDFs read directly (TSE pp. 1-3, 21-22, 27-29; Lee and Schmalz title page and p.1, version dated 28 Feb 2026). Neither contains estimates. Crampes and Estache state that the literature lacks modelling of why impacts differ so much across markets (p.3), and their reference list is how I found Mamkhezri et al. and a Goldman Sachs report by Abecasis and Wei (Feb 2026, The Macroeconomic Spillovers From AI Electricity Demand; not opened, secondhand). For a new study: these give testable heterogeneity predictions that the empirical papers have mostly not tested: effects should be larger where the utility must add capacity, where the large customer negotiated a special contract, and before a dedicated large-load tariff exists. Interacting treatment with tariff adoption dates and reserve margin would be a contribution that a plain average effect is not.
- [secondhand] Darmouni, Olivier, Clemens Lehner and Yuqi Zhang (2026). The Energy Cost of AI and Data Centers. SSRN 6751399 (revised 18 May 2026). Summarized by HEC Paris, 8 Sep 2026. https://www.hec.edu/en/dare/sustainability-society/ai-data-centers-amplify-grid-and-energy-price-risks. Forward-looking model of 420 planned US developments: wholesale prices could rise 20-40% in Texas, Virginia and the Carolinas by 2035 if no new generation is built *Why it matters:* I read only the HEC summary page; the SSRN paper itself was not opened (SSRN blocks automated access; download by hand at papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=6751399). It is a projection, so it is not missing ex-post evidence, but reviewers may cite it. It names the regions where effects should show up first, which is a ready-made pre-specified heterogeneity test for an ex-post panel: do Texas, Virginia and Carolinas utilities already look different by 2025?
- [secondhand] Jenkins, Lisa Martine (2026). Studies say data centers don't raise rates. Does it matter? Latitude Media, 24 June 2026. Related: Philadelphia Inquirer, 6 Nov 2026, quoting Ryan Hledik of Brattle; E3 (May 2026) review for the Data Center Coalition; Kiesling, Knowledge Problem, 11 Mar 2026; Royal, Compute Heat Rate Substack, 2 June 2026. https://www.latitudemedia.com/news/studies-say-data-centers-dont-raise-rates-does-it-matter/. Critique and replication check: I found no formal replication, comment or rebuttal paper for any study on your list *Why it matters:* Searches for critiques or replications of Meeks et al., Watten et al. (including its 1947 interstate highway plan instrument), Scalera et al., Rana/Kaps/Netessine and the E3 review returned only journalism and blog commentary. What exists: Latitude Media stresses that the EPRI analysis stops at 2024 and that its own authors warn supply constraints could reverse the result; the Inquirer piece quotes Brattle's Hledik to the effect that the sign of the historical estimate depends on the technique used; the E3 review notes that many of the studies it surveyed rely on correlation. A search snippet also reports Severin Borenstein estimating a retail effect of 2 to 3 cents per kWh at a Technology Policy Institute event; I did not open that source and it is a speech, not a paper. I am uncertain about this null result: my search budget ran out, and SSRN and Google Scholar could not be queried directly. For a new study, this is the clearest opening. Putting the competing specifications (binary entry DiD, log-capacity IV, shift-share by fiber, facility counts) on one open panel and showing which choice flips the sign would be new work rather than a seventh estimate.

## Water: is there any statistical study of rate effects?

The statement survives. I found no multi-utility, ex-post statistical study of data centers' effect on municipal water rates or household water bills as of 2026-11-11. Confidence is moderate, not high: the shared web-search budget ran out partway through, and several primary documents could not be opened. What I looked for and did not find: extended searches aimed at SSRN, NBER, arXiv, AWWA journals, Utilities Policy, Water Resources Research, UNC EFC, Duke Nicholas, Pacific Institute, Ceres and state auditors returned no panel, difference-in-differences or event-study paper on water rates or bills. I did not get to run separate searches for Brookings, EPA, TWDB/HARC or state auditors other than Virginia's JLARC. The closest work falls into four groups: 1. Forward-looking engineering estimates. Han, Li, Wierman and Ren (arXiv 2603.02705) project capacity needs and put a dollar value on them. They do not measure rates. 2. Municipal bond evidence. Hsu and Moussawi (Georgia Tech, SSRN 7202741) is a real multi-jurisdiction causal study, but of bond spreads and debt issuance, not water rates. Its reported utility-bond result is a null, and I have that only secondhand. 3. Reviews and advocacy papers. The Water Foundation paper says costs "can" or have "the potential to" fall on residential customers (pp. 2, 13) and cites no measured rate effect. Akinade et al. (arXiv 2606.21760) have no rate analysis and rest on the Newton County anecdote. 4. Single-utility documents and journalism, covered below. (a) Hsu and Moussawi, "Who Pays for Data Centers? Evidence from Municipal Financing": full text NOT obtained. SSRN and Forbes both returned 403 and I did not work around them. From the author's own research page (abstract level only): neighbouring municipalities see wider spreads and issue more debt after a data center arrives, while hosts barely respond. Secondhand, from search summaries of the Forbes piece: about 10 basis points per additional data center within 25 miles, and "little evidence" of higher spreads on utility or transportation bonds. No water or sewer breakout was visible anywhere. Download by hand: https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=7202741 (b) Han et al., read through automated extraction of the arXiv HTML v2 (section and table numbers only, no page numbers): - Method (Eq. 1, Sec. 4.1): peak demand = peaking factor x average daily use. Section 3.1.2 documents peaking factors of roughly 3 to 10; Section 4.2.3 adopts a single average of 4.5. - Result (Table 3): 2024 baseline peak capacity is 306-357 MGD. New capacity needed 2024 to 2030 is 697 / 1,074 / 1,451 MGD (low / mid / high growth) if water intensity stays flat, 431 / 702 / 972 MGD at 5% a year improvement, and 227 / 415 / 604 MGD at 10% a year. - Valuation (Sec. 4.2.4, App. A.7 Table 11): $10-40 million per MGD, from 17 projects, giving $7-28B (low) to $15-58B (high) at baseline. - Its only rate figure is the Newton County "33% over two years" projection (Sec. 3.2), attributed to a press-reported official. (c) Local cases, who paid for capacity: - Newton County GA: the water authority's FYE 2026 budget shows adopted increases of 5.0% water and 6.0% sewer effective 2025-07-01, after 2.0% and 6.0% the prior year (pp. 42-43). The reasons it gives are declining consumption and rising operating costs. The 33% figure does not appear in that budget, and I found no authority document supporting it. The two Meta accounts total about 37 million gallons a year (p. 19) against roughly 2.0 billion gallons billed (p. 20), which I calculate as about 2%; press reports say about 10% of county use, on a different denominator. A roughly $98M state ARPA grant, tied to Rivian, funds much of the expansion (pp. 60, 67). - Loudoun Water VA: the 2024 Stantec rate study states "growth pays for growth" through availability charges (Sec. 5). 70% of existing debt principal is recovered from availability charges (Sec. 2). User rates still rise 7% a year for 2025-2027 (Table 2-19). Reclaimed-water rates do not cover that system's costs (Sec. 4). - The Dalles OR: the city's page says revenue must rise 7.3% next year, and 23-27% without Google's tax-agreement payments. It says Google is building about $28M of infrastructure. The "99% by 2036" figure is secondhand (OPB); the master plan PDF was too large to open. - Quincy WA: an EPA case study says Microsoft financed the full $31M reuse utility, which the city owns. - Goodyear AZ, Prineville OR, Columbus/New Albany OH: press reports only. - Mesa AZ and Council Bluffs IA: search summaries only; source pages returned 403 and 429.
