Other documents to fetch by hand: The Dalles 2024 Water System Master Plan PDF; the ICPRB March 2026 "Data Centers and Water Use" PDF (too large to open); Mesa Council Report 21-0552 and Goodyear Resolution 2024-2417 (identifiers from a search summary, unverified). I wrote no files deliberately. The fetch tool auto-saved four PDFs under the local fetch cache.

- [primary] Han, Y., Li, P., Wierman, A., Ren, S. (2026). Small Bottle, Big Pipe: Quantifying and Addressing the Impact of Data Centers on Public Water Systems. arXiv:2603.02705 (v1 2026-03-03, v2 2026-03-18). https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02705. 697-1,451 MGD of new peak-day capacity by 2030, valued at $7-58B; no measured rate effect *Why it matters:* Read via automated extraction of the arXiv HTML v2, so locations are section/table numbers, not pages. Method: Eq. 1 (Sec. 4.1), peak demand = peaking factor x total annual water / days. Sec. 3.1.2 documents peaking factors (Phoenix cooling towers about 2.19; Iowa at least 6.45; Leesburg VA about 8; Boone County IN at least 6.3; summary range 3 to 10). Sec. 4.2.3 adopts a single average of 4.5. Table 3: 2024 baseline capacity 306-357 MGD (68-79 MGD average daily demand); 2030 average daily demand 223/312/402 MGD; 2024-2030 capacity increase 697/1,074/1,451 MGD (baseline), 431/702/972 (5%/yr WUE cut), 227/415/604 (10%/yr). Valuation at $10-40M per MGD (Sec. 4.2.4; App. A.7 Table 11, 17 projects, six partly data-center related): $7-28B low growth to $15-58B high growth. Table 1 (Sec. 2.3) uses EPA's 2006 survey: average surplus capacity 0.72 MGD across system sizes. Only rate figure: Newton County GA 'projected to increase local water rates by 33% over the next two years' (Sec. 3.2, citing a water authority representative via ref. 25). Appendix D: the Lebanon/Boone County IN data center has 8 MGD peak and 4 MGD average, about 32% of the new 25 MGD supply; $560M of that supply is financed through Indiana's Drinking Water SRF. It is a projection plus case material, not a statistical study of rates.
- [secondhand] Hsu, A., Moussawi, H. (2026). Who Pays for Data Centers? Evidence from Municipal Financing. Georgia Tech Scheller College of Business working paper, SSRN abstract 7202741. https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=7202741. Neighbours' bond spreads widen; reported null on utility bonds; no water/sewer result seen *Why it matters:* This is the closest thing to a multi-jurisdiction causal study touching utility finance, but its outcome is bond spreads and debt issuance, not water rates. SSRN returned 403 and Forbes returned 403; no open copy was found, and web.archive.org was unreachable from this tool. From the author's own research page (halamoussawi.com/research, abstract-level summary, which I did read): identification uses proximity to high-power transmission lines and staggered state tax-incentive adoption; neighbours face wider spreads and issue more debt, hosts barely respond; where incentives exist, reduced state transfers push school districts to borrow more. Secondhand only, from search summaries of the Forbes 2026-09-14 article: about 10 bp per additional data center within 25 miles, about $673 per student lower transfers, about 12 bp on education bonds, and 'little evidence of higher spreads on utility or transportation bonds'. The SSRN date is also from a search summary. No water or sewer bond breakout was visible in anything I could open. A person should download the PDF from the SSRN URL to verify every number.
- [primary] The Water Foundation (2026). Water & Data Centers (white paper). https://waterfdn.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/07/Water-Data-Centers-2026.pdf. Says costs 'can fall on residential ratepayers'; cites no measured rate effect *Why it matters:* Read pp. 1-20 directly. A 2026 synthesis that would be expected to cite a rate study if one existed. p. 2: infrastructure costs 'can fall on residential ratepayers rather than developers'. p. 13: connection fees 'do not necessarily cover the full costs', pro rata policies 'are not universal', so the burden 'has the potential to fall on residential customers'; stranded-asset risk is described hypothetically. Its evidence for system cost is Han et al.'s $58B ceiling (p. 12, fn. 22). No statistical study of rates or bills is cited in the body. I did not read the Works Cited list (p. 24 on), so a cited-but-undiscussed study cannot be ruled out from this document alone.
- [primary] Akinade, B.A., Amanambu, A.C., Frame, J.M., Ren, S. (2026). AI Data Centers and the Water Use Feedback Loop. arXiv:2606.21760. https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21760. Ten-site capacity-burden index (0.2% to 134%); no rate, bill or finance analysis *Why it matters:* Read the first 100,000 of about 256,000 characters of the arXiv HTML via automated extraction. Sec. 6.2 defines a Water Consumption Impact index = peak-day consumptive use / host utility maximum capacity, applied to ten US sites (Table 6): Lebanon IN 134%, Henderson NV 0.2%, The Dalles 0.49, Douglas County GA 0.31. This is a multi-site capacity measure, not a price measure. Its only rate evidence is the Newton County 33% projection (Sec. 3.5), drawn from grey literature. It shows the literature's rate claims rest on one anecdote.
- [primary] Cooley, H., Shimabuku, M. (2026). How Much Water Do Data Centers Use? And Why Does It Matter? Pacific Institute Issue Brief. https://pacinst.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Issue-Brief_AI-Data-Centers-2026_Final.pdf. Water-use primer; no rate analysis in the pages read *Why it matters:* Read pp. 1-8 only (the brief is longer). p. 1: withdrawals range from 270 gallons to 3.9 million gallons per day per facility; 74% consumed at a typical facility. pp. 1-2: large data centers 'may create unique challenges for municipal water systems'. Nothing on rates, bills or utility finance in the pages read. The remaining pages were not read, so this is partial verification.
- [primary] Virginia Joint Legislative Audit and Review Commission (2024). Data Centers in Virginia (Report 598), web summary page. https://jlarc.virginia.gov/landing-2024-data-centers-in-virginia.asp. Water use 'currently sustainable'; cost analysis covers electricity only *Why it matters:* Read the JLARC landing page only, not the full report. The water section says most data centers use about as much water as a large office building or less, with a few using substantially more, and recommends authorising localities to require water-use estimates. The page's cost and rate analysis concerns electric utilities only. A state auditor that looked hard at ratepayer effects did not analyse water rates. The full report's water chapter was not opened.
- [primary] Newton County Water and Sewerage Authority. FYE 2026 Budget and Financial Plan. https://ncwsa.us/wp-content/uploads/NCWSA-GA-Budget-Financial-Plan-FYE-2026-Compressed-2.pdf. Adopted 5.0% water / 6.0% sewer; the 33% figure is not in this budget *Why it matters:* Text-extracted and read. pp. 42-43: board adopted 5.0% water and 6.0% sewer effective 2025-07-01, after 2.0% and 6.0% the prior year; stated reasons are 'decreasing consumption, conservative growth forecast, rising operating expenditures, and economic impacts'. p. 19 top-ten table: Baymare LLC 26,078,000 gallons a year, Morning Hornet LLC (Pit #2) 11,000,000; Takeda is far larger at 194,442,000. p. 20: total billed water about 2.0 billion gallons a year, so the two Meta accounts are about 2% by my calculation. Uncertain: the table year is not stated, Baymare was still under construction, and other Meta accounts may exist. p. 13: capital assets are funded by developer and customer contributions, grants and loans. p. 67: FYE 2026-2030 CIP about $164M; state ARPA award $234,062,160, about 42% (about $98M) to the Authority, described on p. 60 as funding infrastructure to serve Rivian. pp. 62-63: forecast assumes further 'incremental' rate adjustments and new debt. A text search of this budget for '33%' found nothing. The press-reported 33% is not corroborated here; I did not find any NCWSA document that supports it, and did not locate a FYE 2027 budget.
- [primary] Meta (2026). Operating responsibly at the Stanton Springs Data Center. Meta Data Centers blog. https://datacenters.atmeta.com/2026/04/operating-responsibly-at-the-stanton-springs-data-center/. Meta says it pays full water and sewer cost including new infrastructure *Why it matters:* Company's own claim, not independent: NCWSA supplies 100% of site water, no groundwater is used, and Meta pays the full cost of water and sewer service including infrastructure to reach and loop the site. Quotes NCWSA's executive director that Meta has 'honored those service agreements'. The page gives no gallon figures and does not address the 33% claim. The 33% figure and the 'about 10% of county daily use' figure were seen only in press summaries (secondhand, with conflicting timing: one outlet says a one-year rise, most say a two-year projection).
- [primary] Stantec for Loudoun Water (2024). Water and Sewer Rate Study, Final Report, October 28, 2024. https://www.loudounwater.org/sites/default/files/loudoun_water_2024_water_and_sewer_rate_study_final_report_10282024%20no%20signature%20(002).pdf. 'Growth pays for growth' via availability charges; user rates still up 7%/yr 2025-2027 *Why it matters:* Text-extracted and read; page numbers are PDF sequence pages (printed page = PDF page minus 4). Sec. 5, PDF p. 35: availability charges are set so growth costs are funded by new customers, 'growth pays for growth'. Sec. 2, PDF p. 17: 30% of principal on existing debt is recovered from user rates and 70% from availability charges. PDF p. 19: about 25% of the five-year capital plan is funded from user rates. Table 2-19, PDF p. 26: recommended water and sewer user rate adjustments of 7.0% in each of 2025, 2026, 2027, then 4.0% in 2028 and 2029. Tables 5-3/5-4: 5/8-inch water availability charge $7,846 rising to $8,966 by 2027; sewer $9,519 to $12,280; the study found current charges below the cost of capacity (PDF pp. 35-36). Sec. 4, PDF p. 34: reclaimed-water rates 'will not cover the anticipated expenditures'; recommended 7%/yr, $1.80 to $2.21 per 1,000 gallons. PDF p. 42: sample 'Data Center' quarterly bill $13,190 rising to $16,149. The study does not attribute the rate increases to data centers and has no data-center cost allocation.
- [primary] Loudoun County, Virginia. Data Centers: Water Considerations (county web page citing Loudoun Water). https://www.loudoun.gov/6402/Water-Considerations. Data centers are 9% of potable use on an average day *Why it matters:* County page states data centers account for 9% of total potable water use on an average day, and that Loudoun Water's reclaimed system delivered more than 750 million gallons to roughly 40 customers in 2025. No peak-day figures and nothing on residential rate impact. Loudoun Water's own data-center page (loudounwater.org/node/2492) holds average-day and maximum-day figures in infographic images I could not read. Han et al. Appendix B reports a 2024 measured data-center peak of 10.9 MGD in the Loudoun Water service area.
- [primary] City of The Dalles, Oregon. Generational Investments: Drinking Water System (Water Master Plan page). https://www.thedalles.gov/department/public_works/master_plans/water_master_plan.php. Revenue must rise 7.3% next year; 23-27% without Google SIP payments *Why it matters:* City's own statement: water revenue must rise 7.3% next year with more increases likely; rates had not been adjusted in 10 years; without Google Strategic Investment Program funding the city would need a 23-27% revenue increase; Google is building an estimated $28 million of water supply and sewer infrastructure; all commercial and industrial customers pay the same rates. Funding sources listed: rates, system development charges, grants, loans, SIP payments, bonds. The master plan PDF exceeded the fetch size limit, so the financial tables were not verified; download by hand from https://www.thedalles.gov/departments/public%20works/master%20plans/cotd-water-system-master-plan-update-nov-2024-final.pdf
- [secondhand] Oregon Public Broadcasting (2026-01-23). The Dalles' mayor called OPB's data center story inaccurate. Here are the facts. https://www.opb.org/article/2026/01/23/the-dalles-mayor-data-center-google/. Google about one-third of city water; residential rates projected up 99% by 2036 *Why it matters:* Press, citing the 2024 Water System Master Plan and November 2021 council packet and minutes. Google's use rose from 104 million gallons (2012) to 434 million (2024), about a third of city consumption per OPB's analysis of city data. A correction says the master plan projects residential rates rising 99% by 2036 (the original article said 7%). The city authorised the $28.5M infrastructure agreement on 2021-11-08; Google's tax agreement was signed the next day. None of these numbers were verified in the master plan itself. A later council action reportedly accepted Google-built infrastructure valued at about $19.6M (search summary only).
- [primary] U.S. EPA. Water Reuse Case Study: Quincy, Washington. https://www.epa.gov/waterreuse/water-reuse-case-study-quincy-washington. Microsoft financed the $31M reuse utility; the city owns and operates it *Why it matters:* Federal case study, not the city's own records. Microsoft financed all capital and related costs ($31 million) and covers operating costs under a 30-year agreement; the City of Quincy owns and operates the Quincy Water Reuse Utility; Microsoft pays a monthly treatment fee with periodic rate adjustments. About 138 million gallons a year of potable groundwater avoided; about 260 million gallons a year of canal make-up water. Other data centers may connect by paying connection fees for their share. The study says reduced discharge to the municipal plant 'can help' keep municipal rates lower, with no figures. Clearest documented case of the developer paying for capacity.
- [secondhand] ABC15 Arizona (March 2023). Microsoft agrees to make data centers air-cooled amid water infrastructure challenges in Goodyear. https://www.abc15.com/news/business/microsoft-agrees-to-make-data-centers-air-cooled-amid-water-infrastructure-challenges-in-goodyear. Microsoft pays $36M of a $90M wastewater plant expansion *Why it matters:* Press only; the city's agreement was not opened. Council unanimously approved an amended development agreement on March 6: Microsoft pays $36 million toward an estimated $90 million expansion of the 157th Avenue Wastewater Treatment Plant, replacing and reimbursing its wastewater impact fees, for 1.2 million gallons per day of plant capacity at build-out, and switches later buildings to air cooling. The city lacked sewer line and plant capacity. A $5M sewer-line assurance deposit and $800K coordinator payment appear only in a search summary of another outlet. Nothing on residential rates.
- [secondhand] KTVZ (2026-03-05). Problem Solvers: Tech investment in state-of-the-art water system could reshape Prineville's economy. https://ktvz.com/news/problem-solvers/2026/03/05/problem-solvers-tech-investment-in-state-of-the-art-water-system-could-reshape-prinevilles-economy/. Meta and Apple funded 'nearly the entire' aquifer storage system *Why it matters:* Press only, quoting the assistant city manager: Meta and Apple funded nearly the whole aquifer storage and recovery system; data centers pay the same rates and fees as other industrial users; the three data centers use about 10% of Prineville's water. No dollar amounts or resident rate numbers in the article. Apple's roughly $8.7M contribution and Meta's second well ($1M-$3.2M projected) come from search summaries of other outlets and were not verified. No city document was opened.
- [secondhand] Scripps News Group (September 2026). How much water do data centers really use? Here's what we learned from public records. 'The Price of Data' series. https://www.denver7.com/the-price-of-data/how-much-water-do-data-centers-really-use-heres-what-we-learned-from-public-records. Columbus: data centers about 3% of system use; no rate analysis *Why it matters:* Press, based on Columbus Water & Power consumption records. Google's New Albany site used over 1 billion gallons in 2025; Google's Columbus site about 448 million. Data centers are about 3% of consumption per the city's water administrator. The system supplied about 156 MGD against 245 MGD capacity. The New Albany business park used about 9 MGD of a 16 MGD entitlement under a 2015 service plan. Columbus flattened its declining-block commercial rates and is studying peak pricing. Covers one utility, with no residential bill data and no statistical analysis. No Columbus rate filing was opened; I could not confirm anything about a fourth water plant or capacity charges.
- [secondhand] Mesa, Arizona: Meta (Redale LLC) water agreement, 2021, as described in search summaries of DatacenterDynamics and local press. https://www.datacenterdynamics.com/en/news/huge-data-center-moves-forward-in-mesa-despite-arizona-water-concerns/. Developer must buy and transfer water credits; city bonds fund wider system expansion *Why it matters:* Not verified: the source page returned 403 and I did not work around it. Search summaries say the agreement allows up to about 1,400 acre-feet a year at build-out (550 at phase 1), a maximum flow of up to 4 million gallons a day, requires the developer to buy water credits and sign them over to the city before connecting, and that a 2019 ordinance fines large users that exceed their allowance. A Mesa official is quoted saying the city was approving over $250M of utility revenue bonds in a five-year capital plan for water infrastructure, which would be a rate-backed public cost; no split between developer and ratepayers was found. A person should pull Mesa Council Report 21-0552 (identifier from a search summary, unverified).
- [secondhand] Council Bluffs, Iowa: Google-funded expansion of the Council Point Water Purification Plant, as described in a search summary of The Gazette (Cedar Rapids). https://www.thegazette.com/news/environment/google-data-center-would-be-among-cedar-rapids-largest-water-and-energy-users/article_70095b9e-c333-58c3-b664-b25ba3df016f.html. Google reportedly paid to expand the plant; no rate or contract terms found *Why it matters:* Not verified: the page returned 429. Search summary says an agreement obtained by The Gazette shows Google paid to expand a Council Bluffs treatment plant to serve its data centers, and that the plant could pump up to 10 million gallons a day when completed in 2022 per the Water Works' 2022 annual report. Other unverified summary claims: Google is the Water Works' largest customer, about 1.4 billion gallons withdrawn in 2024. I found no Water Works board minutes, annual report, rate schedule or contract. The search budget ran out before I could look for them.
- [primary] Lipton, J. (2026-10-29). Exclusive research: Who pays for the data center buildout? The Bond Buyer. https://www.bondbuyer.com/opinion/exclusive-research-who-pays-for-the-data-center-buildout. Muni professionals expect more water/wastewater debt; no spread data *Why it matters:* Read the article. Survey of 109 municipal finance professionals fielded 2026-08-21 to 2026-09-08: over 80% expect higher debt issuance for public power and water and wastewater over three years; respondents allocate capital cost 51 points to developers and hyperscalers, 21 to public power ratepayers, 14 to taxpayers, 14 to governments. It is expectation data, with no spread, rate or bill measurement. Useful context for system finance, but it is not the Georgia Tech paper and it is not evidence of an effect.
- [primary] Adjacent 2026 studies checked at abstract level and ruled out as rate studies: Wise, W.A., Princeton senior thesis, 'Are Data Centers Draining America?'; Sultana and Ahmed, arXiv:2609.23421; Rogers et al., arXiv:2602.10526. https://theses-dissertations.princeton.edu/entities/publication/d5c79989-0782-4c52-ba8c-60ca7599d4ad. Withdrawals, audit frameworks, policy roadmaps; none measures rates or bills *Why it matters:* Abstract pages only. The Princeton thesis is a multi-county empirical study of Meta and Google withdrawals against drought severity and news sentiment (1,266 articles, 2014-2026); no rates. arXiv:2609.23421 proposes a local audit framework illustrated on three Illinois facilities. arXiv:2602.10526 is a Georgia policy roadmap from an expert convening that mentions 'ratepayer equity' without data. These were the nearest candidates the searches surfaced, and none contradicts the statement. Not opened because of size limits: the ICPRB March 2026 report on data centers and water use in the Loudoun Water and Prince William Water service areas, which by its search summary studies average and peak use, not rates (https://www.potomacriver.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/ICPRB.DataCentersandWaterUse.ICPRB_.March2026.pdf).
